% \end{equation}
With the manipulations complete, as before, we can further partition into a non-interacting part $\hat{H}_0$ and an interaction $\hat{W}$ as
\begin{align}
    \hat{H}_0 &= \epsilon_{p} + E_c^{\text{RPA}} + \sum_\mu \Omega_\mu \hat{\beta}_\mu^\dagger \hat{\beta}_\mu \\
    \hat{W} &= \sum_{\nu} M_{p\nu} \left( \hat{\beta}_\nu^\dagger + \hat{\beta}_\nu \right).
    % \hat{H}_0 &= \epsilon_{p} + \sum_{\mu\nu} \Biggl( A_{\mu\nu} \hat{b}_\mu^\dagger \hat{b}_\nu + \frac{1}{2}B_{\mu\nu} \Bigl(\hat{b}_\mu^\dagger \hat{b}_\nu^\dagger + \hat{b}_\mu \hat{b}_\nu \Bigr) \Biggr) \\
    % \hat{V} &= \sum_{\nu} V_{p\nu} \left( \hat{b}_\nu^\dagger + \hat{b}_\nu \right).
\end{align}
We can plug this into the definition of the retarded Green's function.
\begin{align}
G_{pp}^{R}(t) &\approx -i \theta(t) \bra{\Psi_p} e^{-i\hat{H}_{\text{eB}} t} \ket{\Psi_p} \\
&= -i \theta(t) \bra{\Psi_p} e^{-i\left( \hat{H}_0 + \hat{V} \right) t} \ket{\Psi_p} \\
&= -i \theta(t) \bra{\Psi_p} e^{-i\hat{H}_0 t} \mathcal{T} \exp \left( -i \int_0^t dt' e^{i\hat{H}_0 t'} \hat{V} e^{-i\hat{H}_0 t'} \right) \ket{\Psi_p} \\
&= -i \theta(t) e^{-i (\epsilon_p + E_c^{\text{RPA}}) t} \underbrace{\bra{\Psi_p}  \mathcal{T} \exp \left( -i \int_0^t dt' V_I(t') \right) \ket{\Psi_p}}_{*} \label{rpa_int}
\end{align}
where we were able to get to \ref{rpa_int} as before because by definition $\beta|0_{RPA}\rangle = 0$, but notice that now 
. Again, we can work out * by a Taylor expansion of the time-ordered exponential:
\begin{align}
* &= \bra{\Psi_p} \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{(-i)^n}{n!} \int_0^t dt_1 \cdots \int_0^t dt_n \mathcal{T} \left[ V_I(t_1) \cdots V_I(t_n) \right] \ket{\Psi_p} \\
&= 1 + (-i) \int_0^t dt' \bra{\Psi_p} V_I(t') \ket{\Psi_p} + \frac{(-i)^2}{2!} \int_0^t dt_1 \int_0^t dt_2 \bra{\Psi_p} \mathcal{T} \left[ V_I(t_1) V_I(t_2) \right] \ket{\Psi_p} + O(M^3) \\
&= 1 - \frac{1}{2} \int_0^t dt_1 \int_0^t dt_2 \bra{\Psi_p} \mathcal{T} \left[ V_I(t_1) V_I(t_2) \right] \ket{\Psi_p} + O(M^4) \label{eq:time_ordered_expansion}
\end{align}
where in \ref{eq:time_ordered_expansion} we have used the fact that terms with an odd number of $V_I$'s will vanish because they do not conserve the number of bosons. We can evaluate $V_I(t)$ as
% ==========================================================
% 1) Compute V_I(t) explicitly in second quantization
% ==========================================================
% Useful commutators / identities:
%   [\hat{b}_\nu^\dagger \hat{b}_\nu, \hat{b}_\nu] = -\hat{b}_\nu
%   [\hat{b}_\nu^\dagger \hat{b}_\nu, \hat{b}_\nu^\dagger] = +\hat{b}_\nu^\dagger
% hence:
%   e^{+i \Omega_\nu \hat{b}_\nu^\dagger \hat{b}_\nu t}\,\hat{b}_\nu\,e^{-i \Omega_\nu \hat{b}_\nu^\dagger \hat{b}_\nu t} = e^{-i\Omega_\nu t}\hat{b}_\nu
%   e^{+i \Omega_\nu \hat{b}_\nu^\dagger \hat{b}_\nu t}\,\hat{b}_\nu^\dagger\,e^{-i \Omega_\nu \hat{b}_\nu^\dagger \hat{b}_\nu t} = e^{+i\Omega_\nu t}\hat{b}_\nu^\dagger
% Also, \hat{n}_p commutes with \hat{H}_0 (and with all boson operators), so it is invariant in the interaction picture.
\begin{align}
\hat{V}_I(t)
&= e^{i\hat{H}_0 t}\Big[\sum_\nu M_{p\nu}\,\big(\hat{\beta}_\nu^\dagger + \hat{\beta}_\nu\big)\Big]e^{-i\hat{H}_0 t}\\
&= \sum_\nu M_{p\nu}\Big(
e^{i\hat{H}_0 t}\hat{\beta}_\nu^\dagger e^{-i\hat{H}_0 t}
+ e^{i\hat{H}_0 t}\hat{\beta}_\nu e^{-i\hat{H}_0 t}
\Big)\\
&= \sum_\nu M_{p\nu}\Big(
\hat{\beta}_\nu^\dagger (t) + \hat{\beta}_\nu(t)
\Big)\\
&= \sum_\nu M_{p\nu}\big(
e^{+i\Omega_\nu t}\hat{\beta}_\nu^\dagger
+ e^{-i\Omega_\nu t}\hat{\beta}_\nu
\big).
% &= \sum_\nu W_{p\nu}\,\hat{n}_p\Big(
% e^{i\hat{H}_0 t}\hat{b}_\nu^\dagger e^{-i\hat{H}_0 t}
% + e^{i\hat{H}_0 t}\hat{b}_\nu e^{-i\hat{H}_0 t}
% \Big)\\
% &= \sum_\nu W_{p\nu}\,\hat{n}_p\Big(
% \hat{b}_\nu^\dagger (t) + \hat{b}_\nu(t)
% \Big)\\
% &= \sum_\nu W_{p\nu}\,\hat{n}_p\big(
% e^{+i\Omega_\nu t}\hat{b}_\nu^\dagger + e^{-i\Omega_\nu t}\hat{b}_\nu
% \big).
\end{align}
so the two-time expectation value is
\begin{align}
\langle \Psi_p|\hat{V}_I(t_1)\hat{V}_I(t_2)|\Psi_p\rangle
&= \sum_{\mu\nu} M_{p\mu} M_{p\nu}
\langle \Psi_p|\,\big(e^{+i\Omega_\mu t_1}\hat{\beta}_\mu^\dagger + e^{-i\Omega_\mu t_1}\hat{\beta}_\mu\big)\,
\big(e^{+i\Omega_\nu t_2}\hat{\beta}_\nu^\dagger + e^{-i\Omega_\nu t_2}\hat{\beta}_\nu\big)
|\Psi_p\rangle\\
&= \sum_{\mu\nu} M_{p\mu} M_{p\nu}\;
e^{-i\Omega_\mu t_1}e^{+i\Omega_\nu t_2}
\langle 0_{\text{RPA}}|\hat{\beta}_\mu \hat{\beta}_\nu^\dagger|0_{\text{RPA}}\rangle\\
&= \sum_{\nu} |M_{p\nu}|^2\;e^{-i\Omega_\nu (t_1-t_2)}.
% &= \sum_{\nu,\nu'} W_{p\nu}W_{p\nu'}
% \langle \Psi_p|\,\hat{n}_p\hat{n}_p\,

% &= \sum_{\nu} |W_{p\nu}|^2\;e^{-i\Omega_\nu (t_1-t_2)}.
\end{align}

% ==========================================================
% 4) Second-order cumulant C_2(t) and its closed form
% ==========================================================
% The second-order cumulant truncation sets  \chi(t) \approx e^{C_2(t)}  with
So, recognizing 
\begin{align}
* &=
1 - \frac{1}{2} \int_0^t dt_1 \int_0^t dt_2 \bra{\Psi_p} \mathcal{T} \left[ V_I(t_1) V_I(t_2) \right] \ket{\Psi_p} + O(M^4)
\\&=
1 - \int_0^t dt_1 \int_0^{t_1} dt_2 \bra{\Psi_p} V_I(t_1) V_I(t_2) \ket{\Psi_p} + O(M^4) \\
&=
1 - \sum_\nu |M_{p\nu}|^2 \int_0^t dt_1 \int_0^{t_1} dt_2 e^{-i\Omega_\nu (t_1 - t_2)} + O(M^4) \\
&=
1 - \sum_\nu |M_{p\nu}|^2 \left( \frac{t}{i\Omega_\nu} + \frac{1 - e^{-i\Omega_\nu t}}{\Omega_\nu^2} \right) + O(M^4),
% 1-\frac{1}{2}\int_0^t dt_1\int_0^{t} dt_2\;
% \langle \Psi_p|\mathcal{T}\left[\hat{V}_I(t_1)\hat{V}_I(t_2)\right]|\Psi_p\rangle + O(W^4)
% \\
% &=
% 1-\int_0^t dt_1\int_0^{t_1} dt_2\;
% \langle \Psi_p|\hat{V}_I(t_1)\hat{V}_I(t_2)|\Psi_p\rangle + O(W^4) \\
% &=
% 1-\sum_\nu |W_{p\nu}|^2
% \int_0^t dt_1\int_0^{t_1} dt_2\;e^{-i\Omega_\nu (t_1-t_2)} + O(W^4)\\
% &=
% 1-\sum_\nu |W_{p\nu}|^2\left(\frac{t}{i\Omega_\nu}+\frac{1-e^{-i\Omega_\nu t}}{\Omega_\nu^2}\right) + O(W^4)
\end{align} 
we can plug back into the expression for the retarded Green's function, yielding
\begin{align}
G_{pp}^{R,GW+C}(t)
&=
-i \theta(t) e^{-i(\epsilon_p + E_c^{\text{RPA}}) t} \left[1 - \sum_\nu |M_{p\nu}|^2 \left( \frac{t}{i\Omega_\nu} + \frac{1 - e^{-i\Omega_\nu t}}{\Omega_\nu^2} \right) + O(M^4) \right] \\
&\approx
-i \theta(t) e^{-i(\epsilon_p + E_c^{\text{RPA}}) t} e^{C_2(t)}
% -i\,\theta(t)\,e^{-i\epsilon_p t} \left[1-\sum_\nu |W_{p\nu}|^2\left(\frac{t}{i\Omega_\nu}+\frac{1-e^{-i\Omega_\nu t}}{\Omega_\nu^2}\right) + O(W^4)\right]\\
% &\approx
% -i\,\theta(t)\,e^{-i\epsilon_p t} e^{C_2(t)} 
\end{align}
where the second-order cumulant, which is correct up to the second order in the screened interaction $M$, is given by
\begin{equation}
C_2(t) = -\sum_\nu |M_{p\nu}|^2\left(\frac{t}{i\Omega_\nu}+\frac{1-e^{-i\Omega_\nu t}}{\Omega_\nu^2}\right).
\end{equation} 
This shows that one recovers the same cumulant irregardless if we chose the bosonic ground state to be the RPA ground state or the non-interacting bosonic vacuum. It also provides a rigorous justification why our $G_0$ can be from a correlated single particle reference and not just the MF one. In other words, it is defining an algebraic procedure for how to obtain an improved Green's function apart from how we think diagrammatically as
\begin{equation}
G^{GW} = G^{HF} + G^{HF} \Sigma^{GW} G^{GW} \quad\Rightarrow\quad G^{GW+C} = G^{GW} e^{C}.
    % G^{HF} \xrightarrow{\text{Dyson}} G^{G_0W_0} \xrightarrow{\text{Cumulant}} G^{G_0W_0+C}
\end{equation}
Here we were able to get to $G^{GW+C}$ directly without any Dyson iteration.

\subsubsection{Comparison to the Landau formula for the cumulant}
The Landau formula for the cumulant is given by
\begin{equation}
C(t) = \int d\omega \frac{\beta(\omega)}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right)
\end{equation}
where $\beta(\omega) = \frac{1}{\pi} |\text{Im} \Sigma(\omega + \epsilon_p)|$ is proportional to the GW self-energy.
Comparing this to the expression we have derived for $C_2(t)$, we see that the two expressions are consistent \emph{only in the TDL} if we identify $\beta(\omega) = \pi \sum_\nu |M_{p\nu}|^2 \delta(\omega - \Omega_\nu)$, which is precisely the spectral density of the self-energy in the GW approximation. This shows that the
% \begin{equation} \label{eq:HeB}
% 	\hH_\text{eB} = \hH_\text{e} + \hH_\text{B} + \hV_\text{eB}
% \end{equation}
\begin{align}
    \hat{H}_{\text{eB}} = \hat{H}_{\text{e}} + \hat{H}_{\text{B}} + \hat{V}_{\text{eB}}
\end{align}
where
\begin{align}
    \hat{H}_{\text{e}}
    & \equiv \sum_{pq} f_{pq} c_p^\dagger c_q = \sum_p \epsilon_p c_p^\dagger c_p
    \\
    \label{eq:HB}
    \hat{H}_{\text{B}}
    & = \sum_{\mu\nu} A_{\mu\nu} b_\mu^\dagger b_\nu 
    + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{\mu\nu} B_{\mu\nu} \left( b_\mu^\dagger b_\nu^\dagger + b_\mu b_\nu \right)
    \\
    \hat{V}_{\text{eB}} 
    & \equiv \sum_{pq\nu} V_{pq,\nu}  c_p^\dagger c_q \left( b_\nu^\dagger + b_\nu \right) = \sum_{p\nu } W_{p\nu} c_p^\dagger c_p \left( b_\nu^\dagger + b_\nu \right)
\end{align} 
respectively describe the fermionic subsystem, the bosonic bath, and their mutual coupling.

 In the cumulant approximation, this effective Hamiltonian can be expressed as a supermatrix in the space of fermionic and bosonic excitations.
Tolle claimsthat any vertex corrections can be added by modifying certain quantities in the  EOM supermatrix:
\begin{equation}
\bm{H}^{\mathrm{EOM}} = \begin{bmatrix}
    \bm{F} & \bm{W}^< & \bm{W}^> \\
    \left(\bm{W}^<\right)^\dagger&  \bm{d}^< & \bm{0} \\
    \left(\bm{W}^>\right)^\dagger & \bm{0} & \bm{d}^>
\end{bmatrix}
\end{equation}
where $\bm{F}$ represents the fermionic space, $\bm{d}^<$ and $\bm{d}^>$ reprsent the bosonic deexcitation and excitation spaces, respectively, while $\bm{W}^<$ and $\bm{W}^>$ represent the coupling matrices between the fermionic and bosonic sectors.
\subsection{Examples}
A good place to start in an effort to understand this abstract framework is to consider what we have encountered in the past. 
\subsubsection{GW}
$\bm{F}$ is a generalized Fock matrix,
\begin{equation}
    \bm{W}^<_{pk\nu} = \sum_{ia} (pk|ia) \left( X_{ia}^{\nu} + Y_{ia}^{\nu} \right) \quad \text{and} \quad \bm{W}^>_{pc\nu} = \sum_{ia} (pc|ia) \left( X_{ia}^{\nu} + Y_{ia}^{\nu} \right),
\end{equation}
and 
\begin{equation}
    \bm{d}^<_{k\nu , l\nu'} = \left(\epsilon_k - \Omega_\nu\right) \delta_{k,l} \delta_{\nu,\nu'} \quad \text{and} \quad \bm{d}^>_{c\nu , d\nu'} = \left(\epsilon_c + \Omega_\nu\right) \delta_{c,d} \delta_{\nu,\nu'},
\end{equation}
where the bosonic excitation energies $\Omega_\nu$ and amplitudes $X_{ia}^\nu$ and $Y_{ia}^\nu$ are due to the RPA.
\subsubsection{MP2}
$\bm{F}$ is the HF Fock matrix,
\begin{equation}
\bm{W}^<_{pk\nu} \equiv W^<_{pkia} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\langle p a||i k\rangle \quad \text{and} \quad \bm{W}^>_{pc\nu} \equiv W^>_{pcia} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\langle p i||c a\rangle,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\bm{d}^<_{k i a, l j b} = \left(-\epsilon_a+\epsilon_i+\epsilon_k\right) \delta_{k l} \delta_{i j} \delta_{a b} \quad \text{and} \quad \bm{d}^>_{c a i, d b j} = \left(-\epsilon_i+\epsilon_c+\epsilon_a\right) \delta_{c d} \delta_{a b} \delta_{i j}.
\end{equation}
It has been shown that MP2 contains diagrams (vertex corrections) that are absent in GW.
\subsubsection{ECC}
Tolle's argument is that ECC can also be placed in this framework, which is able to systematically treat vertex corrections, albeit at a high computational cost.
\subsection{Why this framework doesn't apply to the cumulant approximation}
The cumulant approximation considers a single fermionic state $p$ coupled to a bosonic bath of excitations and deexcitations. With this simplification, we can replace the fermionic space $\bm{F}$ with the single particle energy of a state $p$ as $\epsilon_p$. The coupling matrices lose one dimension, becoming $\bm{W}^<\equiv \delta_{pk} W^<_{k\nu}$ and $\bm{W}^>\equiv \delta_{pc} W^>_{c\nu}$, while the bosonic deexcitation and excitation spaces remain unchanged. The EOM supermatrix then takes the form
\begin{equation}
\bm{H}^{\mathrm{EOM}} = \begin{bmatrix}
    \epsilon_p & 0 & \bm{W}^> \\
    0&0 & \bm{0} \\
    \left(\bm{W}^>\right)^\dagger & \bm{0} & \bm{d}^>
\end{bmatrix}\end{equation}

\subsubsection{MP2}
We can consider a concrete example such as MP2, where $W^<_{k\nu} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\langle p a||i p\rangle$, expanded as 
\begin{align}
W^<_{k\nu} &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\langle p a||i p\rangle = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left( \langle p a|i p\rangle - \langle p a|p i\rangle \right) = 0.
\end{align}
We reached the final equality because we know that for periodic systems the two-electron integrals must vanish due to momentum conservation, since we must have $\bm{k}_p + \bm{k}_a = \bm{k}_i + \bm{k}_p \implies \bm{k}_a = \bm{k}_i$, but this is impossible because $a$ is a virtual index and $i$ is an occupied index. So, the coupling matrix $\bm{W}^<$ vanishes and likewise $\bm{W}^>$ also vanishes. This means that the EOM supermatrix in the single fermion approximation is block diagonal, written as
\begin{equation}
\bm{H}^{\mathrm{EOM}} = \begin{bmatrix}
    \epsilon_p & \bm{0} & \bm{0} \\
    \bm{0} &  \bm{d}^< & \bm{0} \\
    \bm{0} & \bm{0} & \bm{d}^>
\end{bmatrix}\end{equation}
We can now apply the Lowdin partitioning technique to this matrix. We first define an excitation/deexcitation vector $\bm{R} = \begin{bmatrix}
    R_p & \bm{R}^< & \bm{R}^>
\end{bmatrix}^T$. We can then write the following system of equations:
\begin{align}
\epsilon_p R_p &= \omega R_p \\
 \bm{d}^< \bm{R}^< &= \omega \bm{R}^< \\
 \bm{d}^> \bm{R}^> &= \omega \bm{R}^>
\end{align}
This implies that we got a complete decoupling of the fermionic and bosonic sectors.
\section{PT2}
\subsection{SRG-MP2}
We are interested in finding the self consistent solution to
\begin{equation}
    \bm{F}^{QP}= \bm{F}^{(0)} + \bm{F}^{(2)}[\bm{F}^{QP}]
\end{equation}
where $\bm{F}^{(0)}$ is the canonical HF Fock matrix and $\bm{F}^{(2)}$ is the second order correction to the Fock matrix, which we take to be diagonal with elements given by
% $\begin{aligned} F_{p q}^{(2)}(s) & =\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i j a} \frac{\Delta_{i j}^{p a}+\Delta_{i j}^{q a}}{\left(\Delta_{i j}^{p a}\right)^2+\left(\Delta_{i j}^{q a}\right)^2}\langle p a||i j\rangle\langle i j||q a\rangle \\ & \times\left[1-e^{-\left[\left(\Delta_{i j}^{p a}\right)^2+\left(\Delta_{i j}^{q a}\right)^2\right]^s}\right] \\ & +\frac{1}{2} \sum_{a b i} \frac{\Delta_{a b}^{p i}+\Delta_{a b}^{q i}}{\left(\Delta_{a b}^{p i}\right)^2+\left(\Delta_{a b}^{q i}\right)^2}\langle p i||a b\rangle\langle a b||q i\rangle \\ & \times\left[1-e^{-\left[\left(\Delta_{a b}^{p i}\right)^2+\left(\Delta_{a b}^{q i}\right)^2\right] s}\right] .\end{aligned}$
\begin{align}
F_{p p}^{(2)}(s) & =\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i j a} \frac{|\langle p a||i j\rangle|^2}{\Delta_{i j}^{p a}}\left[1-e^{-2\left(\Delta_{i j}^{p a}\right)^2 s}\right] +\frac{1}{2} \sum_{a b i} \frac{|\langle p i||a b\rangle|^2}{\Delta_{a b}^{p i}}\left[1-e^{-2\left(\Delta_{a b}^{p i}\right)^2 s}\right] \\
&= F_{pp}^{2h1p}(s) + F_{pp}^{2p1h}(s)
\end{align}
where $F_{pp}^{2h1p}(s)$ and $F_{pp}^{2p1h}(s)$ are the contributions from the 2h1p and 2p1h channels, and
 $\Delta_{i j}^{p a} = \epsilon_p + \epsilon_a - \epsilon_i - \epsilon_j$ and $\Delta_{a b}^{p i} = \epsilon_p + \epsilon_i - \epsilon_a - \epsilon_b$.
We always follow the same procedure when applying the one-shot cumulant.
\subsubsection{Deriving the analytic gradient}
Since our objective function to minimize is
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{L} = \frac{1}{2} \sum_p \left( \Delta \epsilon_p \right)^2
\end{equation}
where at iteration $n$,
\begin{equation}
    \Delta \epsilon_p^n = \epsilon_p^0 + F_{pp}[\left\{\epsilon_q^{n-1}\right\}] - \epsilon_p^{n-1},
\end{equation}
Taking the derivative of the Lagrangian with respect to $\epsilon_p$, we get
\begin{align}
    \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \epsilon_p} &= \sum_q \Delta \epsilon_q \frac{\partial \Delta \epsilon_q}{\partial \epsilon_p}\\& = \sum_q \Delta \epsilon_q \left( \frac{\partial F_{qq}}{\partial \epsilon_p} - \delta_{pq} \right) \\
\end{align}
Partition $\frac{\partial F_{qq}}{\partial \epsilon_p}$ into its 2h1p and 2p1h contributions:
Working out $\frac{\partial F_{qq}^{2h1p}}{\partial \epsilon_p}$, we get
\begin{align}
    \frac{\partial F_{qq}^{2h1p}}{\partial \epsilon_p} & = \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i j a} |\langle q a||i j\rangle|^2 \partial_{\epsilon_p} \left[ \frac{1}{\Delta_{i j}^{q a}} \left(1-e^{-2\left(\Delta_{i j}^{q a}\right)^2 s}\right) \right] \\
& = \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i j a} |\langle q a||i j\rangle|^2 \left[ \partial_{\epsilon_p} \left[ \frac{1}{\Delta_{i j}^{q a}} \right] \left[1-e^{-2\left(\Delta_{i j}^{q a}\right)^2 s}\right]  + \frac{1}{\Delta_{i j}^{q a}} \partial_{\epsilon_p} \left[1-e^{-2\left(\Delta_{i j}^{q a}\right)^2 s}\right] \right] 
% \left[1-e^{-2\left(\Delta_{i j}^{q a}\right)^2 s}\right] \partial_{\epsilon_p} \left[ \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i j a} \frac{|\langle q a||i j\rangle|^2}{\Delta_{i j}^{q a}}\right]  + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i j a} \frac{|\langle q a||i j\rangle|^2}{\Delta_{i j}^{q a}} \partial_{\epsilon_p} \left[1-e^{-2\left(\Delta_{i j}^{q a}\right)^2 s}\right] \\
\end{align}
and
\begin{align}
\partial_{\epsilon_p} \left[ \frac{1}{\Delta_{i j}^{q a}} \right] & = \frac{\partial}{\partial \Delta_{i j}^{q a}} \frac{\partial \Delta_{i j}^{q a}}{\partial \epsilon_p} \left[ \frac{1}{\Delta_{i j}^{q a}} \right] = -\frac{\partial_{\epsilon_p} [\Delta_{i j}^{q a}]}{\left(\Delta_{i j}^{q a}\right)^2}
    % \partial_{\epsilon_p} \left[ \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i j a} \frac{|\langle q a||i j\rangle|^2}{\Delta_{i j}^{q a}}\right] & = -\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i j a} \frac{|\langle q a||i j\rangle|^2\partial_{\epsilon_p} [\Delta_{i j}^{q a}]}{\left(\Delta_{i j}^{q a}\right)^2}\\
\end{align}
Consider 
\begin{align}
    \partial_{\epsilon_p} \left[\Delta_{i j}^{q a}\right] & = \partial_{\epsilon_p} \left[ \epsilon_q + \epsilon_a - \epsilon_i - \epsilon_j \right]\\& = \delta_{pq} + \delta_{pa} - \delta_{pi} - \delta_{pj} \\
\implies \partial_{\epsilon_p} \left[ \frac{1}{\Delta_{i j}^{q a}} \right] & = -\frac{\delta_{pq} + \delta_{pa} - \delta_{pi} - \delta_{pj}}{\left(\Delta_{i j}^{q a}\right)^2}
\end{align}
Next, do
\begin{align}
    \partial_{\epsilon_p} \left[ 1 - e^{-2\left(\Delta_{i j}^{q a}\right)^2 s} \right] & = \frac{\partial}{\partial \Delta_{i j}^{q a}} \frac{\partial \Delta_{i j}^{q a}}{\partial \epsilon_p} \left[ 1 - e^{-2\left(\Delta_{i j}^{q a}\right)^2 s} \right] \\
&=  (\delta_{pq} + \delta_{pa} - \delta_{pi} - \delta_{pj}) \left( - \exp\left[-2\left(\Delta_{i j}^{q a}\right)^2 s\right] \cdot (-4s \Delta_{i j}^{q a}) \right) 
\end{align}
So, we found
\begin{align}
\frac{\partial F_{qq}^{2h1p}}{\partial \epsilon_p}
&= \frac{1}{2}\sum_{ija} |\langle qa||ij\rangle|^2\,
(\delta_{pq}+\delta_{pa}-\delta_{pi}-\delta_{pj})
\Biggl[
-\frac{1-e^{-2(\Delta_{ij}^{qa})^2 s}}{(\Delta_{ij}^{qa})^2}
+4s\,e^{-2(\Delta_{ij}^{qa})^2 s}
\Biggr].
\end{align}
Analogously, we can work out $\frac{\partial F_{qq}^{2p1h}}{\partial \epsilon_p}$, yielding
\begin{align}
\frac{\partial F_{qq}^{2p1h}}{\partial \epsilon_p}
&= \frac{1}{2}\sum_{abi} |\langle qi||ab\rangle|^2\,
(\delta_{pq}+\delta_{pi}-\delta_{pa}-\delta_{pb})
\Biggl[
-\frac{1-e^{-2(\Delta_{ab}^{qi})^2 s}}{(\Delta_{ab}^{qi})^2}
+4s\,e^{-2(\Delta_{ab}^{qi})^2 s}
\Biggr].
\end{align}
Thus, the formula for the gradient is given by
\begin{align}
\frac{\partial F_{qq}}{\partial \epsilon_p} 
&= \frac{\partial F_{qq}^{2h1p}}{\partial \epsilon_p} + \frac{\partial F_{qq}^{2p1h}}{\partial \epsilon_p} \nonumber\\
&= \frac{1}{2}\sum_{ija} |\langle qa||ij\rangle|^2\,
(\delta_{pq}+\delta_{pa}-\delta_{pi}-\delta_{pj})
\Biggl[
-\frac{1-e^{-2(\Delta_{ij}^{qa})^2 s}}{(\Delta_{ij}^{qa})^2}
+4s\,e^{-2(\Delta_{ij}^{qa})^2 s}
\Biggr] \nonumber\\
&\quad + \frac{1}{2}\sum_{abi} |\langle qi||ab\rangle|^2\,
(\delta_{pq}+\delta_{pi}-\delta_{pa}-\delta_{pb})
\Biggl[
-\frac{1-e^{-2(\Delta_{ab}^{qi})^2 s}}{(\Delta_{ab}^{qi})^2}
+4s\,e^{-2(\Delta_{ab}^{qi})^2 s}
\Biggr]
\end{align}
The cumulant ansatz for the interacting, retarded Green's function is given by:
\begin{equation}
    \bm{G}(t) = \bm{G}^0(t)e^{\bm{C}(t)}
\end{equation}
where $\bm{C}(t)$ is the retarded cumulant and $\bm{G}^0(t)$ is the noninteracting, retarded Green's function. By relating the Dyson equation to the Taylor series expansion of the exponential, we can write:
\begin{align}
\bm{G}^0(t) + \bm{G}^0(t) \bm{C}(t) + O(v^4) &= \bm{G}^0(t) + \iint \dd t_1 \dd t_2 \bm{G}^0(t-t_1) \bm{\Sigma}^c(t_1 - t_2) \bm{G}^0(t_2) + O(v^4) \\
    \implies \bm{G}^0(t) \bm{C}(t) &= \iint \dd t_1 \dd t_2 \bm{G}^0(t-t_1) \bm{\Sigma}^c(t_1 - t_2) \bm{G}^0(t_2) \quad \text{(up to second order in $v$)}
\end{align}
{As indicated, when we use a second order self-energy, the cumulant should be exact to second order in the bare Coulomb interaction $v$.}
Projecting to the spin-orbital basis and inserting the resolution of the identity, we get:
\begin{align}
    \sum_{r}\bra{p}\bm{G}^0(t) \ket{r}\bra{r}\bm{C}(t)\ket{q} &= \sum_{rs}\iint \dd t_1 \dd t_2 \bra{p}\bm{G}^0(t-t_1)\ket{r} \bra{r}\bm{\Sigma}^c(t_1 - t_2)\ket{s} \bra{s}\bm{G}^0(t_2)\ket{q} \\
    \implies \sum_{r}\bm{G}_{pr}^0(t) \bm{C}_{rq}(t) &= \sum_{rs}\iint \dd t_1 \dd t_2 \bm{G}_{ps}^0(t-t_1) \bm{\Sigma}_{sr}^c(t_1 - t_2) \bm{G}_{rq}^0(t_2)\\
    \implies \bm{G}_{pp}^{0}(t) \bm{C}_{pq}(t) &= \underbrace{\iint \dd t_1 \dd t_2 \bm{G}_{pp}^{0}(t-t_1) \bm{\Sigma}_{pq}^c(t_1 - t_2) \bm{G}_{qq}^{0}(t_2)}_{*}
    \label{eqn:cumulant_connection}
\end{align}
where $\bm{G}^0(t)$ is diagonal in the spin-orbital basis, as $\bm{G}_{pp}^{0}(t) = -i\Theta(t)e^{-i\epsilon_p t}$, where $\epsilon_p$ is the single-particle energy of the $p$-th spin-orbital.
The formula for the inverse Fourier transform is given by:
\begin{equation}
    f(t) = \int \frac{\dd \omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t} f(\omega)
\end{equation}
which implies that
\begin{align}
    \bm{G}_{pp}^{0}(t-t_1) = \int \frac{\dd \omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega (t-t_1)} \bm{G}_{pp}^{0}(\omega)\\
    \bm{\Sigma}_{pq}^c(t_1 - t_2) = \int \frac{\dd \omega'}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega' (t_1 - t_2)} \bm{\Sigma}_{pq}^c(\omega')\\
    \bm{G}_{qq}^{0}(t_2) = \int \frac{\dd \omega''}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega'' t_2} \bm{G}_{qq}^{0}(\omega'')
\end{align}
and plugging into the double time integral *, we get:
\begin{align}
    * &= \iint \dd t_1 \dd t_2 \left[\int \frac{\dd \omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega (t-t_1)} \bm{G}_{pp}^{0}(\omega)\right] \left[\int \frac{\dd \omega'}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega' (t_1 - t_2)} \bm{\Sigma}_{pq}^c(\omega')\right] \left[\int \frac{\dd \omega''}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega'' t_2} \bm{G}_{qq}^{0}(\omega'')\right]\\
    &= \underbrace{\int \dd t_1 e^{-i \left(\omega' -\omega\right)t_1} \int \dd t_2 e^{-i \left(\omega'' -\omega'\right)t_2}}_{4\pi^2 \delta(\omega' -\omega)\delta(\omega'' -\omega')} \iiint \dd \omega \dd \omega' \dd \omega'' \frac{e^{-i\omega t}}{8\pi^3} \bm{G}_{pp}^{0}(\omega)\bm{\Sigma}_{pq}^c(\omega')\bm{G}_{qq}^{0}(\omega'')\\
    &= \int \frac{\dd \omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t} \bm{G}_{pp}^{0}(\omega)\bm{\Sigma}_{pq}^c(\omega)\bm{G}_{qq}^{0}(\omega)
\end{align}
With $\eta \to 0^+$, we get
\begin{align}
    -i \Theta(t) e^{-i\epsilon_p t} \bm{C}_{pq}(t) &= \int \frac{\dd \omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t} \bm{G}_{pp}^{0}(\omega)\bm{\Sigma}_{pq}^c(\omega)\bm{G}_{qq}^{0}(\omega) \\
\implies {C}_{pp}(t) &=  i\int \frac{\dd \omega}{2\pi} e^{-i(\omega - \epsilon_p) t} \frac{{\Sigma}_{pp}^c(\omega)}{(\omega - \epsilon_p + i\eta)^2} \quad \forall t>0\\ 
&=  i\int  \frac{\dd \omega'}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega' t} \frac{{\Sigma}_{pp}^c(\omega' + \epsilon_p)}{(\omega' + i\eta)^2} \label{eq:Cpp_0}
\end{align}
A retarded second order self-energy can be written as
\begin{align}
\Sigma_{pq}^{(2)}(\omega) =& \frac{1}{2} \sum_{iab} \frac{\langle pi || ab \rangle \langle ab || qi \rangle}{\omega + \epsilon_i - \epsilon_a - \epsilon_b + i\eta} + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{ija} \frac{\langle pa || ij \rangle \langle ij || qa \rangle}{\omega + \epsilon_a - \epsilon_i - \epsilon_j + i\eta} \\
\implies \Sigma_{pp}^{(2)}(\omega+\epsilon_p) =&
\frac{1}{2} \sum_{iab}
\frac{\left< pi \left| \right| ab \right>^2}
{\omega-\epsilon_{pi}^{ab}} + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{ija}
\frac{\left< pa \left| \right| ij \right>^2}
{\omega-\epsilon_{pa}^{ij}}
\end{align}
where
\begin{equation}
    \epsilon_{pi}^{ab} = \epsilon_{a}+\epsilon_{b}-\epsilon_{p}-\epsilon_{i} - i\eta \quad \text{and} \quad \epsilon_{pa}^{ij} = \epsilon_{i}+\epsilon_{j}-\epsilon_{p}-\epsilon_{a} - i\eta
\end{equation}
are generated from combinations of the single particle energies of $G_0$. So we can write the diagonal cumulant\footnote{ 
Note that in order to get from eqn.~\ref{eq:cumulant_2nd_1} to eqn.~\ref{eq:cumulant_2nd} we have used the identity $\int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} \frac{i e^{-i \omega t}}{\omega^2\left(\omega-\epsilon\right)} = \frac{1}{\epsilon^2} \left(e^{-i \epsilon t} +i \epsilon t -1 \right) \mathrm{sgn}(t)$, and since we are dealing with retarded quantities, we only care about $t>0$ so $\mathrm{sgn}(t) = 1$.} as
\begin{align}
C_{pp}(t) &\equiv i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} \frac{ \Sigma_{pp}^c\left(\omega+\epsilon_p\right)}{(\omega + i \eta)^2} e^{-i \omega t} \\
\implies C_{pp}^{(2)}(t) &= \frac{1}{2} \sum_{iab} \left< pi \left| \right| ab \right>^2 \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} \frac{i e^{-i \omega t}}{\omega^2\left(\omega-\epsilon_{pi}^{ab}\right)} + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{ija} \left< pa \left| \right| ij \right>^2 \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} \frac{i e^{-i \omega t}}{\omega^2\left(\omega-\epsilon_{pa}^{ij}\right)} 
\label{eq:cumulant_2nd_1}
\\
&= \frac{1}{2}\sum_{iab} \frac{\left< pi \left| \right| ab \right>^2}{\left(\epsilon_{pi}^{ab}\right)^2}
\left(e^{-i \epsilon_{pi}^{ab} t} +i \epsilon_{pi}^{ab} t -1 \right)
+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ija} \frac{\left< pa \left| \right| ij \right>^2}{\left(\epsilon_{pa}^{ij}\right)^2}
\left(e^{-i \epsilon_{pa}^{ij} t} +i \epsilon_{pa}^{ij} t -1 \right) 
\label{eq:cumulant_2nd}
\\
&= \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i a b}\langle p i \| a b\rangle^2 f\left(\epsilon_{p i}^{a b}\right)+\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i j a}\langle p a \| i j\rangle^2 f\left(\epsilon_{p a}^{i j}\right)\label{eq:cumulant_2nd_2}\\
&= \int d\omega\, \beta(\omega) f(\omega)
\end{align}
where $f(\omega) \equiv \frac{e^{-i \omega t}+i \omega t-1}{\omega^2} $ and we identify the cumulant kernel as
\footnote{To get from eqn.~\ref{eq:beta_def} to eqn.~\ref{eq:beta_2nd}, we have used the identity $\operatorname{Im} \frac{1}{x + i \eta} = -\pi \delta(x)$ as $\eta \to 0^+$.}
\begin{align}
    \beta(\omega) &= -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im} \Sigma_{pp}^{(2)}\left(\omega+\epsilon_p\right) 
\label{eq:beta_def}\\
\\
&=\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i a b}\langle p i \| a b\rangle^2 \delta\left(\omega-\epsilon_{p i}^{a b}\right)+\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i j a}\langle p a \| i j\rangle^2 \delta\left(\omega-\epsilon_{p a}^{i j}\right) \label{eq:beta_2nd}
\end{align}
A connection can be made between the cumulant kernel $\beta(\omega)=\sum_q g_q^2 \delta(\omega-\omega_q)$ for a 2nd-order self energy  to that for electrons coupled to
bosonic excitations at the frequencies $\omega_q\equiv \epsilon_{pq}^{rs}$ in the quasi-boson approximation (QBA)
with coupling coefficients $g_q \equiv \langle p q \| r s\rangle$. 
\subsubsection{Why the cumulant regularizes irregardless of an empirical parameter}
Whenever $\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} \to 0$ (or $\epsilon_{pa}^{ij}$ by the same argument), due to L'Hopital's rule
\begin{align}
\lim_{\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} \to 0} \frac{e^{-i \epsilon_{pi}^{ab} t} +i \epsilon_{pi}^{ab} t -1}{\left(\epsilon_{pi}^{ab}\right)^2} &= \lim_{\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} \to 0} \frac{-i t e^{-i \epsilon_{pi}^{ab} t} + i t}{2 \epsilon_{pi}^{ab}} = \lim_{\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} \to 0} \frac{-t^2 e^{-i \epsilon_{pi}^{ab} t}}{2} = -\frac{t^2}{2}.
\end{align}
This is why the cumulant spectral function should stay stable even in the case of a vanishing gap.
\subsection{Comment on plots}
\subsubsection{First order}
When I say that I am computing the cumulant spectral function \emph{to first order}, it means that I directly compute the spectral function in frequency domain as
\begin{align}
A_{pp}(\omega) \frac{Z_p^{QP}}{\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP} + i\eta} + \sum_{i a b} \frac{Z_{piab}^{sat}}{\omega - \epsilon_{piab}^{sat} + i\eta} + \sum_{i j a} \frac{Z_{pija}^{sat}}{\omega - \epsilon_{pija}^{sat} + i\eta}
\end{align}
where we defined the satellite energies $\epsilon_{piab}^{sat} = \epsilon_p^{QP} + \epsilon_{pi}^{ab}$ and $\epsilon_{pija}^{sat} = \epsilon_p^{QP} + \epsilon_{pa}^{ij}$, and the corresponding spectral weights $Z_{piab}^{sat} = Z_p^{QP} \zeta_{pi}^{ab}$ and $Z_{pija}^{sat} = Z_p^{QP} \zeta_{pa}^{ij}$. These have the definitions $Z_p^{QP} = e^{-\sum_{i a b} \zeta_{pi}^{ab} - \sum_{i j a} \zeta_{pa}^{ij}}$, $\epsilon_p^{QP} = \epsilon_p - \sum_{i a b} \zeta_{pi}^{ab}\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} - \sum_{i j a} \zeta_{pa}^{ij}\epsilon_{pa}^{ij}$, $\zeta_{pi}^{ab} = \frac{1}{2} \frac{\langle p i \| a b\rangle^2}{\left(\epsilon_{p i}^{a b}\right)^2}$, and $\zeta_{pa}^{ij} = \frac{1}{2} \frac{\langle p a \| i j\rangle^2}{\left(\epsilon_{p a}^{i j}\right)^2}$.
\subsubsection{Infinite order}
When I say that I am computing the cumulant spectral function \emph{to infinite order}, it means that I compute the spectral function as $A_{pp}(\omega) = -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im} G_{pp}(\omega)$, where I get $G_{pp}(\omega)$ from the DFT of $G_{pp}(t) = G_{pp}^{0}(t) e^{C_{pp}(t)}$ using
\begin{align}
C_{pp}(t) & = \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i a b}\frac{\langle p i \| a b\rangle^2 }{\left(\epsilon_{p i}^{a b}\right)^2}\left(e^{-i \epsilon_{p i}^{a b} t} +i \epsilon_{p i}^{a b} t -1 \right) +\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i j a} \frac{\langle p a \| i j\rangle^2}{\left(\epsilon_{p a}^{i j}\right)^2} \left(e^{-i \epsilon_{p a}^{i j} t} +i \epsilon_{p a}^{i j} t -1 \right)
\end{align}
and $G_{pp}^{0}(t) = -i \Theta(t) e^{-i \epsilon_p t}$. $\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} = \epsilon_{a}+\epsilon_{b}-\epsilon_{p}-\epsilon_{i} - i\eta$ and $\epsilon_{pa}^{ij} = \epsilon_{i}+\epsilon_{j}-\epsilon_{p}-\epsilon_{a} - i\eta$.
\subsubsection{Where these equations come from}
Loos showed that first order and infinite order share the same features for GW+C; all that I have done is to replace the GW quantites by their MP2 counterparts.  All $\epsilon$'s above are the single particle energies due to SRG-MP2 since I took this as my noninteracting reference.

\subsubsection{Frequency domain version}
Now, if we plug in our derived expression for $C_{pp}(t)$ into the cumulant ansatz for the retarded Green's function:
\begin{align}
G_{pp}(t) & = G_{pp}^{HF}(t) e^{C_{pp}(t)} \\
& = -i \Theta(t) e^{-i \epsilon_p t + C_{pp}(t)} \\
& = -i \Theta(t) e^{-i \epsilon_p t} \exp\left\{ \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i a b}\frac{\langle p i \| a b\rangle^2 }{\left(\epsilon_{p i}^{a b}\right)^2}\left(e^{-i \epsilon_{p i}^{a b} t} +i \epsilon_{p i}^{a b} t -1 \right) +\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i j a} \frac{\langle p a \| i j\rangle^2}{\left(\epsilon_{p a}^{i j}\right)^2} \left(e^{-i \epsilon_{p a}^{i j} t} +i \epsilon_{p a}^{i j} t -1 \right) \right\} \\
&= -i \Theta(t) \underbrace{e^{-\sum_{i a b} \zeta_{pi}^{ab} - \sum_{i j a} \zeta_{pa}^{ij}}}_{Z_p^{QP}} e^{-i\overbrace{\left(\epsilon_p - \sum_{i a b} \zeta_{pi}^{ab}\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} - \sum_{i j a} \zeta_{pa}^{ij}\epsilon_{pa}^{ij}\right)}^{\epsilon_p^{QP}} t} e^{\sum_{i a b} \zeta_{pi}^{ab} e^{-i\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} t} + \sum_{i j a} \zeta_{pa}^{ij} e^{-i\epsilon_{pa}^{ij} t}}
% & = -i \Theta(t) \underbrace{e^{-\sum_{i\nu} \zeta_{pi\nu} - \sum_{a\nu} \zeta_{pa\nu}}}_{Z_p^{QP}} e^{-i\overbrace{\left(\epsilon_p^{HF} - \sum_{i\nu} \zeta_{pi\nu}\Delta_{pi\nu} - \sum_{a\nu} \zeta_{pa\nu}\Delta_{pa\nu}\right)}^{\epsilon_p^{QP}} t} e^{\sum_{i\nu} \zeta_{pi\nu} e^{-i\Delta_{pi\nu} t} + \sum_{a\nu} \zeta_{pa\nu} e^{-i\Delta_{pa\nu} t}} \\ 
% & = -i \Theta(t) Z_p^{QP} e^{-i \epsilon_p^{QP} t} e^{\sum_{i\nu} \zeta_{pi\nu} e^{-i\Delta_{pi\nu} t} + \sum_{a\nu} \zeta_{pa\nu} e^{-i\Delta_{pa\nu} t}} \\
\end{align}
where $\zeta_{pi}^{ab} = \frac{1}{2} \frac{\langle p i \| a b\rangle^2}{\left(\epsilon_{p i}^{a b}\right)^2}$ and $\zeta_{pa}^{ij} = \frac{1}{2} \frac{\langle p a \| i j\rangle^2}{\left(\epsilon_{p a}^{i j}\right)^2}$ are the bosonic coupling coefficients.
 Next, we want to perform a Fourier transform.
\begin{align}
G_{pp}(\omega) & = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} dt e^{i \omega t} G_{pp}(t)
\label{Gpp1}
\\
& = -i Z_p^{QP} \int_0^{\infty} dt e^{i(\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP}) t} \exp\left\{ \sum_{i a b} \zeta_{pi}^{ab} e^{-i\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} t} + \sum_{i j a} \zeta_{pa}^{ij} e^{-i\epsilon_{pa}^{ij} t} \right\}
\label{Gpp2}
\\
& = -i Z_p^{QP} \int_0^{\infty} dt\, e^{i(\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP}) t} \left(1 + \sum_{i a b} \zeta_{pi}^{ab} e^{-i\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} t} + \sum_{i j a} \zeta_{pa}^{ij} e^{-i\epsilon_{pa}^{ij} t} + \ldots \right) \\
&= -i Z_p^{QP} \int_0^{\infty} dt\, e^{[-\eta + i(\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP}) t]}\label{Gpp3} \\
&\quad - i Z_p^{QP} \sum_{iab} \zeta_{pi}^{ab} \int_0^{\infty} dt\, e^{[-\eta + i(\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP} - \epsilon_{pi}^{ab}) t]}\\
&\quad - i Z_p^{QP} \sum_{a\nu} \zeta_{pa}^{ij} \int_0^{\infty} dt\, e^{[-\eta + i(\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP} - \epsilon_{pa}^{ij}) t]} + \ldots\\
&= \frac{Z_p^{QP}}{\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP} + i\eta} + \sum_{i a b} \frac{Z_{piab}^{sat}}{\omega - \epsilon_{piab}^{sat} + i\eta} + \sum_{i j a} \frac{Z_{pija}^{sat}}{\omega - \epsilon_{pija}^{sat} + i\eta} + \ldots
\end{align}
where we defined the satellite energies $\epsilon_{piab}^{sat} = \epsilon_p^{QP} + \epsilon_{pi}^{ab}$ and $\epsilon_{pija}^{sat} = \epsilon_p^{QP} + \epsilon_{pa}^{ij}$, and the corresponding spectral weights $Z_{piab}^{sat} = Z_p^{QP} \zeta_{pi}^{ab}$ and $Z_{pija}^{sat} = Z_p^{QP} \zeta_{pa}^{ij}$.
The diagonal elements of the spectral function are obtained as (the virtual satellites spectral function, whose derivation will mirror that of the occupied satellites, are omitted for the sake of brevity):
\begin{align}
A_{pp}^{MP2+C}(\omega) & = -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im} G_{pp}(\omega) \\
& = -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im}\left[\frac{Z_p^{QP}}{\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP} + i\eta} + \sum_{i a b} \frac{Z_{pi}^{ab}}{\omega - \epsilon_{pi}^{ab} + i\eta} + \sum_{i j a} \frac{Z_{pa}^{ij}}{\omega - \epsilon_{pa}^{ij} + i\eta} + \ldots \right] \\
&= -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im}\left[\frac{\operatorname{Re} Z_p^{QP} + i \operatorname{Im} Z_p^{QP}}{\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP} + i\left(\eta - \operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)} + \sum_{i a b} \frac{\operatorname{Re} Z_{pi}^{ab} + i \operatorname{Im} Z_{pi}^{ab}}{\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{pi}^{ab} + i\left(\eta - \operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{pi}^{ab}\right)} + \ldots \right] \\
&= -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im}\left[\frac{\left(\operatorname{Re} Z_p^{QP} + i \operatorname{Im} Z_p^{QP}\right)\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP} - i\left(\eta - \operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)\right)}{\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)^2 + \left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)^2}\right.\\
& \left. + \sum_{i a b} \frac{\left(\operatorname{Re} Z_{pi}^{ab} + i \operatorname{Im} Z_{pi}^{ab}\right)\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{pi}^{ab} - i\left(\eta - \operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{pi}^{ab}\right)\right)}{\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{pi}^{ab}\right)^2 + \left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{pi}^{ab}\right)^2} + \ldots \right] \\
&= -\frac{1}{\pi}\left[\frac{\left(\operatorname{Re} Z_p^{QP}\right)\left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right) + \left(\operatorname{Im} Z_p^{QP}\right)\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)}{\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)^2 + \left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)^2}\right.\\
& \left. + \sum_{i a b} \frac{\left(\operatorname{Re} Z_{pi}^{ab}\right)\left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{pi}^{ab}\right) + \left(\operatorname{Im} Z_{pi}^{ab}\right)\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{pi}^{ab}\right)}{\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{pi}^{ab}\right)^2 + \left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{pi}^{ab}\right)^2} + \ldots \right] 
% A_{pp}^{GW+C}(\omega) & = -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im} G_{pp}(\omega) \\
% & = -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im}\left[\frac{Z_p^{QP}}{\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP} + i\eta} + \sum_{i\nu} \frac{Z_{pi\nu}^{sat}}{\omega - \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat} + i\eta} + \sum_{a\nu} \frac{Z_{pa\nu}^{sat}}{\omega - \epsilon_{pa\nu}^{sat} + i\eta}\right] \\
% & = -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im}\left[\frac{\operatorname{Re} Z_p^{QP} + i \operatorname{Im} Z_p^{QP}}{\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP} + i\left(\eta - \operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)} + \sum_{i\nu} \frac{\operatorname{Re} Z_{pi\nu}^{sat} + i \operatorname{Im} Z_{pi\nu}^{sat}}{\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat} + i\left(\eta - \operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)} + \ldots \right] \\
% & = -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im}\left[\frac{\left(\operatorname{Re} Z_p^{QP} + i \operatorname{Im} Z_p^{QP}\right)\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP} - i\left(\eta - \operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)\right)}{\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)^2 + \left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)^2}\right.\\
% & \left. + \sum_{i\nu} \frac{\left(\operatorname{Re} Z_{pi\nu}^{sat} + i \operatorname{Im} Z_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat} - i\left(\eta - \operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)\right)}{\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)^2 + \left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)^2} + \ldots \right] \\
% & = -\frac{1}{\pi}\left[\frac{\left(\operatorname{Re} Z_p^{QP}\right)\left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right) + \left(\operatorname{Im} Z_p^{QP}\right)\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)}{\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)^2 + \left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)^2}\right.\\
% & \left. + \sum_{i\nu} \frac{\left(\operatorname{Re} Z_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)\left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right) + \left(\operatorname{Im} Z_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)}{\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)^2 + \left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)^2} + \ldots \right]
\end{align}
\subsection{MP2+C}
Here we start with $G_0 = G^{HF}$. To summarize, we are solving
\begin{equation}
G^{MP2+C} = G^{HF} e^{C^{MP2}}
\end{equation}
where $C^{MP2}$ is given by eqn.~\ref{eq:cumulant_2nd_2} with the single particle energies and two-electron integrals evaluated at the HF level. However, we have seen that doing it this way introduces issues for higher system sizes in metals, where MP2 is known to diverge.
\subsection{SRG-MP2+C}
Here, I tried $G_0 = G^{SRG-MP2}$. That is, I was solving
\begin{equation}G^{SRG-MP2+C} = G^{SRG-MP2} e^{C^{SRG-MP2}}\end{equation}
where $C^{SRG-MP2}$ was determined as
\begin{align}
  C_{pp}^{SRG-MP2}(t)  &= \frac{1}{2}\sum_{iab} \frac{\left< pi \left| \right| ab \right>^2}{\left(\varepsilon_{pi}^{ab}\right)^2}
\left(e^{-i \varepsilon_{pi}^{ab} t} +i \varepsilon_{pi}^{ab} t -1 \right)
+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ija} \frac{\left< pa \left| \right| ij \right>^2}{\left(\varepsilon_{pa}^{ij}\right)^2}
\left(e^{-i \varepsilon_{pa}^{ij} t} +i \varepsilon_{pa}^{ij} t -1 \right) 
\end{align}
where $\varepsilon_{pi}^{ab} = \varepsilon_{a}+\varepsilon_{b}-\varepsilon_{p}-\varepsilon_{i} - i\eta$ and $\varepsilon_{pa}^{ij} = \varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}-\varepsilon_{p}-\varepsilon_{a} - i\eta$ was generated from combinations of the dressed SRG-MP2 single particle energies. This formulation does \emph{not} have firm theoretical grounds. To see why, consider that we derived the second-order cumulant by defining
\begin{align}
G(t) &= G_0(t) + \iint dt_1 dt_2 G_0(t-t_1) \Sigma(t_1 - t_2) G(t_2) \\
&= G_0(t) + \iint dt_1 dt_2 G_0(t-t_1) \Sigma(t_1 - t_2) G_0(t_2) + \ldots
\end{align}
and 
\begin{align}
G(t) & = G_0(t) e^{C(t)} \\
& = G_0(t) \left(1 + C(t) + \ldots\right)
\end{align}
And then we equated the terms linear in $\Sigma$ and $C$ to get the expression for $C$ in terms of $\Sigma$. If we wanted to use $G_0 = G^{SRG-MP2}$, then the self energy that we would be using is the one generated from $G^{SRG-MP2}$, which is not the same as the second order self energy generated from $G^{HF}$. That is, it is no longer true that
\begin{equation}
G_0(t) C(t) = \iint dt_1 dt_2 G_0(t-t_1) \Sigma^{(2)}[G^{SRG-MP2}](t_1 - t_2) G_0(t_2)
\end{equation}
up to second order in the bare Coulomb interaction. BWs2+C is more rigorously defined, as we will see below.

\section{BWs2+C}
\subsection{Electronic structure Hamiltonian }
% \section{QPMP2: Goldstone Self-Energy and Ground-State Correlation}

% We first look at the QPMP2 method proposed by Coveney and Tew \cite{coveney_regularized_2023}. The core idea behind this method is their choice of using the second order self energy as a perturbation to the Fock matrix, and deriving a correlation energy expression this way. It is also important to note that they use Goldstone self energy as their second-order self energy expression.

% We treat the self-energy as a perturbation to the HF Fock operator:

% \hat{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\omega) = \hat{\mathbf{F}} + \lambda \boldsymbol{\Sigma}(\omega)

We define projection operators $\hat{\mathbf{P}}_p = |\phi_p\rangle \langle \phi_p|$, and $\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_p = \hat{\mathbf{1}} - \hat{\mathbf{P}}_p$. Resolve the quasi-particle state as
% \[|\psi_p\rangle = \hat{\mathbf{P}}_p |\psi_p\rangle + \hat{\mathbf{Q}}_p |\psi_p\rangle.\]
% (with intermediate normalization $\langle \phi_p | \psi_p \rangle = 1$.)

% Applying $\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_p$ to the quasi-particle equation gives

% \hat{\mathbf{Q}}_p
% \left[
% \hat{\mathbf{f}}
% +
% \lambda \boldsymbol{\Sigma}(\varepsilon_p)
% \right]
% |\psi_p\rangle
% =

% Which can be further rewritten as:
% \begin{equation}
% \hat{\mathbf{Q}}_p |\psi_p\rangle = \lambda
% \frac{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_p}{\varepsilon_p - \hat{\mathbf{f}}}
% \boldsymbol{\Sigma}(\varepsilon_p)
% |\psi_p\rangle.    
% \end{equation}

% This is the Dyson-type equation for the quasi-particle state. Using the definition of the projection operators and intermediate normalization, we can expand perturbatively in $\lambda$ and get 
% \[
% \varepsilon_p = \varepsilon_p^{\mathrm{HF}} + \lambda \langle \phi_p |\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(\varepsilon_p)| \psi_p \rangle + \mathcal{O}(\lambda^2)\dots
% \]

% One can also expand the self energy $\boldsymbol{\Sigma}$ perturbatively as $\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(\omega) = \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} \mu^n \boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(n)}(\omega)$. Combining both expansions and only retaining terms up to $\mathcal{O}(\mu^2)$, we get $\varepsilon_p^{\mathrm{QP}} = \varepsilon_p + \lambda\mu^2\Sigma_{pp}^{(2)}(\varepsilon_p^{\mathrm{QP}})$. Setting $\mu = \lambda = 1$, we recover the quasi-particle equation:
% \begin{equation}\label{eq:QPeq}
%     \varepsilon_p^{\mathrm{QP}} = \varepsilon_p + \Sigma_{pp}^{(2)}(\varepsilon_p^{\mathrm{QP}}).
% \end{equation}
% The authors link this quasi-particle equation to a Brillouin--Wigner perturbation theory, due to the dependence on $\varepsilon_p^{\mathrm{QP}}$ on both sides of the equation.
% The MP2 partition chooses $\hat{H} = \hat{H}_0 + \hat{H}_1$, where $\mathbf{H}_0 = \mathbf{F}$ and $\mathbf{H}_1$ is the perturbation. The exact Dyson equation is $G^{-1}(\omega) = \omega - F - \Sigma(\omega)$, and we also have $G^{(0)}(\omega)=\big(\omega I - F\big)^{-1}$.
% In contrast,
In the below, it is assumed that we are dealing with retarded quantities and in the spin-restricted formalism. The BWs2 partition chooses
\begin{equation}
    \hat{H} = \hat{H}_0' + \hat{H}_1',
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{H}_0' = \begin{pmatrix} \mathbf{F}_{oo} + \alpha_1 \mathbf{W}^{O} & 0 \\ 0 & \mathbf{F}_{vv} + \alpha_2 \mathbf{W}^{V} \end{pmatrix},
\end{equation}
where we assume a canonical Hartree-Fock matrix and define 
\begin{align}
{W}^{O}_{ii} &= \frac{1}{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{ii}^{(2),2p1h}(\omega = \tilde{\varepsilon}_i)= \frac{1}{2}\sum_{a b j} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{ab}}{\tilde{\varepsilon}_i + \tilde{\varepsilon}_j - \tilde{\varepsilon}_a - \tilde{\varepsilon}_b},\\
\mathbf{W}^{V}_{aa} &= \frac{1}{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{aa}^{(2),2h1p}(\omega = \tilde{\varepsilon}_a)= \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i j b} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{ab}}{\tilde{\varepsilon}_a + \tilde{\varepsilon}_b - \tilde{\varepsilon}_i - \tilde{\varepsilon}_j},
\end{align}
where $\tilde{\varepsilon}$ is the BWs2 ground-state MO energy and we defined $\mathcal K_{pq}^{rs} =
(pr|qs)
\left[
2(pr|qs)-(ps|qr)
\right]$. Note that in this spin-restricted formalism, we must assert that for general $\alpha_1$, $\operatorname{Tr}(\alpha_1\mathbf{W}^{O}) = \frac{\alpha_1}{2}E_{\text{MP2}}$. As usual,
\begin{equation}
\hat{H}_1' = \hat{H} - \hat{H}_0'.
\end{equation}
% \[
% \hat{H} = \hat{H}_0' + \hat{H}_1'.
% \]
% with
% \[
% \mathbf{H}_0' = \mathbf{F} + \alpha \mathbf{W},
% \qquad
% \mathbf{W} = \bm{\Sigma}^{(2),2h1p}(\omega = \tilde{\varepsilon}),
% \]
% where $\tilde{\varepsilon}$ is the BWs2 ground-state MO energy.
% As usual,
% \[
% \hat{H}_1' = \hat{H} - \hat{H}_0'.
% \]
We define the noninteracting Green's function as
\begin{equation}
G^{(0)'}(\omega)
=
\big( \omega - (\mathbf{F} + \underbrace{\alpha_1 \mathbf{W}^O \oplus \alpha_2 \mathbf{W}^V}_{\mathbf{W}}) \big)^{-1}.
\end{equation}
The Dyson equation must remain unchanged under any partition, so we have
\begin{align}
G^{-1}(\omega)
&= \omega - \mathbf{F} - \bm{\Sigma}(\omega) \\
&= \omega - (\mathbf{F} + \mathbf{W})
   - \big[ \bm{\Sigma}(\omega) - \mathbf{W} \big] \\
&= \big( G^{(0)'}(\omega) \big)^{-1}
   - \bm{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\omega).
\end{align}
This gives us the Dyson equation for the BWs2 partition:
\begin{align}
G(\omega)
&= G^{(0)'}(\omega)
 + G^{(0)'}(\omega)
   \bm{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\omega)
   G(\omega) \\
&\approx
   G^{(0)'}(\omega)
 + G^{(0)'}(\omega)
   \bm{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\omega)
   G^{(0)'}(\omega) \\
\implies G(t) &\approx
   G^{(0)'}(t)
 + \int dt' dt'' \,
   G^{(0)'}(t - t')
   \bm{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{eff}}(t' - t'')
   G^{(0)'}(t'').
\label{eq:Dyson_BWs2}
\end{align}
Note that \ref{eq:Dyson_BWs2} is exact up to second order in the bare Coulomb interaction since
\begin{align}
\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\omega)
&\equiv
\bm{\Sigma}(\omega) - \mathbf{W} \\
&\approx
\bm{\Sigma}^{(2)}\!\left[ G^{(0)'} \right](\omega)
- \mathbf{W}
\end{align}
and these quantities are exact to second order in the bare Coulomb interaction.
Alternatively, the cumulant ansatz for the Green's function is
\[
G(t) = G^{(0)}(t) e^{C(t)}.
\]
Thus, the BWs2 cumulant ansatz for state $p$ in the diagonal approximation is
\begin{align}
G_{pp}^{\mathrm{BWs2+C}}(t)
&=
G_{pp}^{(0)'}(t)
e^{C_{pp}^{\mathrm{BWs2}}(t)} \\
&\approx
G_{pp}^{(0)'}(t)
+
G_{pp}^{(0)'}(t)
C_{pp}^{\mathrm{BWs2}}(t).
\label{eq:BWs2_cumulant_ansatz}
\end{align}
where we have retained terms in the Taylor expansion of the exponential up to first order in the cumulant.
Equating \eqref{eq:Dyson_BWs2} and
\eqref{eq:BWs2_cumulant_ansatz} gives
\begin{align}
G_{pp}^{(0)'}(t)
C_{pp}^{\mathrm{BWs2}}(t)
&=
\int dt' dt'' \,
G_{pp}^{(0)'}(t - t')
\Sigma_{\mathrm{eff},pp}(t' - t'')
G_{pp}^{(0)'}(t''),
\end{align}
which implies
\begin{align}
C_{pp}^{\mathrm{BWs2}}(t)
&=
i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi}
e^{-i(\omega - \tilde{\varepsilon}_p)t}
\frac{\Sigma_{\mathrm{eff},pp}(\omega)}
     {(\omega - \tilde{\varepsilon}_p)^2} \\
&=
i \int \frac{d\omega'}{2\pi}
e^{-i\omega' t}
\frac{\Sigma_{\mathrm{eff},pp}(\omega' + \tilde{\varepsilon}_p)}
     {(\omega')^2}. %\\
% &=
% \int d\omega\,
% \frac{\beta_{pp}(\omega)}{\omega^2}
% \left(
% e^{-i \omega t}
% + i \omega t
% - 1
% \right),
\end{align}
% where
% \[
% \beta_{pp}(\omega)
% =
% \frac{1}{\pi}
% \operatorname{Im}
% \Sigma_{\mathrm{eff},pp}
% (\omega + \tilde{\varepsilon}_p)
% \]
% is the cumulant kernel for the BWs2+C method.
\subsection{Occupied $p$}
\begin{align}
\Sigma_{\mathrm{eff},pp}(\omega) &=
\Sigma_{pp}^{(2)}\!\left[ G^{(0)'} \right](\omega)
-  W_{pp} \\
&=
\Sigma_{pp}^{(2)}\!\left[ G^{(0)'} \right](\omega)
- \frac{\alpha_1}{2} \Sigma_{pp}^{(2),2p1h}\!\left[ G^{(0)'} \right](\tilde{\varepsilon}_p)\\
\implies \Sigma_{\mathrm{eff},pp}(\omega + \tilde{\varepsilon}_p) &=
\Sigma_{pp}^{(2),2h1p+2p1h}\!\left[ G^{(0)'} \right](\omega + \tilde{\varepsilon}_p)
- \frac{\alpha_1}{2} \Sigma_{pp}^{(2),2p1h}\!\left[ G^{(0)'} \right](\tilde{\varepsilon}_p)\\
&= \sum_{ija} \mathcal{K}_{pa}^{ij} \left( \frac{1}{\omega + \tilde{\varepsilon}_p+ \tilde{\varepsilon}_a  - \tilde{\varepsilon}_i - \tilde{\varepsilon}_j +i\eta}\right)\\
 &\quad + \sum_{iab} \mathcal{K}_{pi}^{ab} \left( \frac{1}{\omega + \tilde{\varepsilon}_p+ \tilde{\varepsilon}_i  - \tilde{\varepsilon}_a - \tilde{\varepsilon}_b +i\eta} - \frac{\frac{\alpha_1}{2}}{\tilde{\varepsilon}_p+ \tilde{\varepsilon}_i  - \tilde{\varepsilon}_a - \tilde{\varepsilon}_b +i\eta} \right),
% &\quad + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{iab} \frac{\langle p i \| a b\rangle^2}{\omega + \tilde{\varepsilon}_p + \tilde{\varepsilon}_i - \tilde{\varepsilon}_a - \tilde{\varepsilon}_b +i\eta} \\
% &= \frac{1}{2} \sum_{ija} \langle p a \| i j\rangle^2 \left( \frac{1}{\omega - \tilde{\epsilon}^{ij}_{pa}} + \frac{\alpha}{\tilde{\epsilon}^{ij}_{pa}} \right) + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{iab} \frac{\langle p i \| a b\rangle^2}{\omega - \tilde{\epsilon}^{ab}_{pi}}
\end{align}
where we identify $\tilde{\epsilon_{pi}^{ab}} = \tilde{\varepsilon}_a + \tilde{\varepsilon}_b - \tilde{\varepsilon}_p - \tilde{\varepsilon}_i-i\eta$ and $\tilde{\epsilon}_{pa}^{ij} = \tilde{\varepsilon}_i + \tilde{\varepsilon}_j - \tilde{\varepsilon}_p - \tilde{\varepsilon}_a - i\eta$
as the bosonic frequencies in the quasi-boson approximation due to the BWs2 partition.
%  Putting this together, the effective self-energy is
% \begin{equation}
%     \Sigma_{\mathrm{eff},pp}(\omega + \tilde{\varepsilon}_p) = 
%     \frac{1}{2} \sum_{iab} \langle p i \| a b\rangle^2 \frac{1}{\omega - \tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab}} 
%     + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{ija} \langle p a \| i j\rangle^2 \frac{\omega}{\tilde{\epsilon}_{pa}^{ij}(\omega - \tilde{\epsilon}_{pa}^{ij})}.
% \end{equation}
Using the partial fraction decomposition\footnote{
\begin{equation}
    \frac{1}{\omega^2(\omega - \epsilon)} = \frac{1}{\epsilon^2} \frac{1}{\omega - \epsilon} - \frac{1}{\epsilon^2} \frac{1}{\omega} - \frac{1}{\epsilon} \frac{1}{\omega^2}
\end{equation}}
and the time-domain integral identities\footnote{
\begin{align}
    i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t} \frac{1}{\omega - \epsilon} &= e^{-i\epsilon t} \\
    i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t} \frac{1}{\omega} &= 1 \\
    i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t} \frac{1}{\omega^2} &= -it,
\end{align}}
we find for the 2p1h term:
% Now, integral to be solved for the 2p1h term is:
\begin{align}
    I_{2p1h}^{\alpha_1}(t) &= i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} \frac{e^{-i\omega t}}{\omega^2} \left( \frac{1}{\omega - \tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab}} + \frac{\alpha_1/2}{\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab}} \right) \\
&= \frac{e^{-i\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab} t} + i \left( 1-\alpha_1/2 \right) \tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab} t - 1}{(\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab})^2}
%     &= i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t} \left( \frac{1}{(\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab})^2} \frac{1}{\omega - \tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab}} - \frac{1}{(\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab})^2} \frac{1}{\omega} - \frac{1}{\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab}} \frac{1}{\omega^2} \right) \\
%  &= \frac{1}{(\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab})^2} e^{-i\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab}t} - \frac{1}{(\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab})^2}(1) - \frac{1}{\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab}}(-it) \nonumber \\
%     &= \frac{e^{-i\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab} t} + i\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab} t - 1}{(\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab})^2}
\end{align}
The 2h1p term can be integrated as:
% The integral for the 2h1p term is:
\begin{align}
    I_{2h1p}(t) &= i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} \frac{e^{-i\omega t}}{\omega^2} \frac{1}{\omega - \tilde{\epsilon}_{pa}^{ij}} \\
&= \frac{e^{-i\tilde{\epsilon}_{pa}^{ij} t} + i \tilde{\epsilon}_{pa}^{ij} t - 1}{(\tilde{\epsilon}_{pa}^{ij})^2}
%  \frac{1}{\omega \tilde{\epsilon}_{pa}^{ij}(\omega - \tilde{\epsilon}_{pa}^{ij})}
\end{align}
where in the intermediate steps, we use the partial fraction decomposition\footnote{
\begin{equation}
    \frac{1}{\omega(\omega-\epsilon)} = \frac{1}{\epsilon} \left( \frac{1}{\omega - \epsilon} - \frac{1}{\omega} \right)
\end{equation}
} and the time-domain integral identities mentioned above.
Combining the two yields the final form:
\begin{equation}
    C_{pp}^{(2;\alpha_1)}(t) = 
    \sum_{i a b}\frac{\mathcal{K}_{pi}^{ab} }{\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b}\right)^2}\left(e^{-i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b} t} +i \left( 1-\alpha_1 /2\right) \tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b} t -1 \right) +\sum_{i j a} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{pa}}{\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{p a}^{i j}\right)^2} \left(e^{-i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p a}^{i j} t} +i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p a}^{i j} t -1 \right)
\end{equation}
Thus, the retarded Green's function in the BWs2+C method will read
\begin{equation}
G_{pp}^{\mathrm{(2;\alpha_1)+C}}(t) = -i \theta(t) e^{-i\tilde{\varepsilon}_p t} \exp\left\{C_{pp}^{(2;\alpha_1)}(t)\right\}.
\end{equation}
\subsubsection{How this reduces to a HF poled Green's function}
We can rewrite as 
\begin{align}
    G_{pp}^{\mathrm{(2;\alpha_1)+C}}(t) &= -i \theta(t) e^{-i\tilde{\varepsilon}_p t} \exp\left\{C_{pp}^{(2;\alpha_1)}(t)\right\} \\
    &= -i \theta(t) e^{-i\epsilon_p^{\rm HF} t} \exp\left\{-i\underbrace{W_{pp}}_{\frac{\alpha_1}{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{pp}^{(2),2p1h}[G^{(0)'}](\omega=\tilde{\varepsilon}_p)}t+\sum_{i a b}\frac{\mathcal{K}_{pi}^{ab} }{\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b}\right)^2}\left(e^{-i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b} t} +i \left( 1-\alpha_1 /2\right) \tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b} t -1 \right) +\ldots \right\} \\
&= -i \theta(t) e^{-i\epsilon_p^{\rm HF} t} \exp\left\{\sum_{i a b}\frac{\mathcal{K}_{pi}^{ab} }{\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b}\right)^2}\left(e^{-i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b} t} +i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b} t -1 \right) +\sum_{i j a} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{pa}}{\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{p a}^{i j}\right)^2} \left(e^{-i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p a}^{i j} t} +i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p a}^{i j} t -1 \right) \right\}
\end{align}
\subsection{Virtual $p$}
Going through the same procedures will get us to 
\begin{equation}
    C_{pp}^{(2;\alpha_2)}(t) = 
    \sum_{i a b}\frac{\mathcal{K}_{pi}^{ab} }{\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b}\right)^2}\left(e^{-i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b} t} +i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b} t -1 \right) +\sum_{i j a} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{pa}}{\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{p a}^{i j}\right)^2} \left(e^{-i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p a}^{i j} t} +i \left( 1-\alpha_2 /2\right) \tilde{\epsilon}_{p a}^{i j} t -1 \right)
\end{equation}
with the retarded Green's function reading
\begin{equation}
    G_{pp}^{\mathrm{(2;\alpha_2)+C}}(t) = -i \theta(t) e^{-i\tilde{\varepsilon}_p t} \exp\left\{C_{pp}^{(2;\alpha_2)}(t)\right\}.
\end{equation}
This is to be compared with the usual second order cumulant, to which the above reduces when $\alpha_1, \alpha_2 = 0$:
\begin{align}
C_{pp}^{(2)}(t) & = \sum_{i a b}\frac{\mathcal{K}_{pi}^{ab} }{\left(\epsilon_{p i}^{a b}\right)^2}\left(e^{-i \epsilon_{p i}^{a b} t} +i \epsilon_{p i}^{a b} t -1 \right) +\sum_{i j a} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{pa}}{\left(\epsilon_{p a}^{i j}\right)^2} \left(e^{-i \epsilon_{p a}^{i j} t} +i \epsilon_{p a}^{i j} t -1 \right)
\end{align}
The quasi-boson frequencies are potentially different due to the different choice of partition.
\subsection{Self-consistent BWs2 }
At iteration $n>0$, the chemical potential is determined from
\begin{equation}
\frac{N}{2}
=
\sum_p
\int_{-\infty}^{\mu^{(n)}} 
A_p^{(n)}(\omega)\,d\omega,
\end{equation}
where $\mu^{(0)}=\frac{\epsilon^{\rm HF}_{\rm HOMO}+\epsilon^{\rm HF}_{\rm LUMO}}{2}$ and $A_p^{(0)}(\omega)$ is a delta function at the BW single-particle energy. Then, the second-Born spectral kernel is
\begin{align}
B_p^{(n)}(\Omega)
&=
\sum_{qrs}
\mathcal{K}_{pq}^{rs}
\int
d\omega_q\,d\omega_r\,d\omega_s\,
A_q^{(n)}(\omega_q)
A_r^{(n)}(\omega_r)
A_s^{(n)}(\omega_s)
\nonumber\\
&\quad\times
\Big[
f_q^{(n)}
(1-f_r^{(n)})
(1-f_s^{(n)})
+
(1-f_q^{(n)})
f_r^{(n)}
f_s^{(n)}
\Big]
\nonumber\\
&\quad\times
\delta(
\Omega-\omega_r-\omega_s+\omega_q)
% &= \underbrace{\sum_{iab}
% \mathcal{K}_{pi}^{ab} \int d\omega_i d\omega_a d\omega_b
% f_i^{(n)}A_i^{(n)}(\omega_i) f_a^{(n)}A_a^{(n)}(\omega_a) f_b^{(n)}A_b^{(n)}(\omega_b) \delta(\Omega-\omega_i-\omega_a+\omega_b)}_{B^{(n)}_{p,2p1h}(\Omega)}\\
% &= \underbrace{\sum_{ija}
% \mathcal{K}_{ij}^{pa} \int d\omega_i d\omega_j d\omega_a
% f_i^{(n)}A_i^{(n)}(\omega_i) f_j^{(n)}A_j^{(n)}(\omega_j) f_a^{(n)}A_a^{(n)}(\omega_a) \delta(\Omega-\omega_i-\omega_j+\omega_a)}_{B^{(n)}_{p,2h1p}(\Omega)}
\end{align}
and so we can identify 
\begin{align}
B_{p,2p1h}^{(n)}(\Omega)
&=
\sum_{qrs}\mathcal K_{pq}^{rs}
\int d\omega_q\,d\omega_r\,d\omega_s\,
A_q^{(n)}(\omega_q)
A_r^{(n)}(\omega_r)
A_s^{(n)}(\omega_s)
\nonumber\\
&\quad\times
f_q^{(n)}
\left(1-f_r^{(n)}\right)
\left(1-f_s^{(n)}\right)
\delta\!\left(
\Omega-\omega_r-\omega_s+\omega_q
\right),
\\[2mm]
B_{p,2h1p}^{(n)}(\Omega)
&=
\sum_{qrs}\mathcal K_{pq}^{rs}
\int d\omega_q\,d\omega_r\,d\omega_s\,
A_q^{(n)}(\omega_q)
A_r^{(n)}(\omega_r)
A_s^{(n)}(\omega_s)
\nonumber\\
&\quad\times
\left(1-f_q^{(n)}\right)
f_r^{(n)}
f_s^{(n)}
\delta\!\left(
\Omega-\omega_r-\omega_s+\omega_q
\right),
\\
B_p^{(n)}(\Omega)
&=
B_{p,2p1h}^{(n)}(\Omega)
+
B_{p,2h1p}^{(n)}(\Omega),
\end{align}
where $
f_x^{(n)}
\equiv
f^{(n)}(\omega_x)$ with $f^{(n)}(\omega)
=
\Theta\!\left(\mu^{(n)}-\omega\right)$ at zero temperature.
The associated retarded correlation self-energy is
\begin{equation}
\Sigma_{p,c}^{R,(n)}(\omega)
=
\int d\Omega\,
\frac{B_p^{(n)}(\Omega)}
{\omega-\Omega+i0^+}.
\end{equation} 
\begin{tcolorbox}
In the initial iteration, this takes on the one-shot form 
\begin{align}
\Sigma_{p,c}^{R,(0)}(\omega)
&=
\int d\Omega\,
\frac{B_p^{(0)}(\Omega)}
{\omega-\Omega+i0^+}
\\
&=
\int d\Omega\,
\frac{1}{\omega-\Omega+i0^+}
\left[
\sum_{iab}
\mathcal K_{pi}^{ab}\,
\delta\!\left(
\Omega-\tilde{\varepsilon}_a-\tilde{\varepsilon}_b
+\tilde{\varepsilon}_i
\right)
+
\sum_{ija}
\mathcal K_{ij}^{pa}\,
\delta\!\left(
\Omega-\tilde{\varepsilon}_i-\tilde{\varepsilon}_j
+\tilde{\varepsilon}_a
\right)
\right]
\\
&=
\sum_{iab}
\frac{\mathcal K_{pi}^{ab}}
{\omega+\tilde{\varepsilon}_i
-\tilde{\varepsilon}_a-\tilde{\varepsilon}_b+i0^+}
+
\sum_{ija}
\frac{\mathcal K_{ij}^{pa}}
{\omega+\tilde{\varepsilon}_a
-\tilde{\varepsilon}_i-\tilde{\varepsilon}_j+i0^+}
\\
&\equiv
\Sigma_{p,c}^{(2),2p1h}
\!\left[G^{(0)'}\right](\omega)
+
\Sigma_{p,c}^{(2),2h1p}
\!\left[G^{(0)'}\right](\omega)
.
\end{align}
\end{tcolorbox}
We are always taking 
\begin{equation}
A_p^{(n)}(\omega)
=
-\frac{1}{\pi}
\Im
G_p^{R,(n)}(\omega)
\end{equation}
and at each iteration, we have
\begin{equation}
\Sigma_k^{x,(n)} = n_{k}^{(n)}v_m-\sum_{\bm{q}\neq\bm{0}} n_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}}^{(n)}v(\bm{q}),
\end{equation}
where $v_m$ is the Madelung constant and $v(\bm{q})$ is the Fourier transform of the Coulomb potential. We have $n_k^{(n)} = \int_{-\infty}^{\mu^{(n)}} A_k^{(n)}(\omega)\,d\omega$.
\subsubsection{Dyson formalism}
Here we can write out 
\begin{align}
A_p^{(n+1)}(\omega)
&=
-\frac{1}{\pi}
\Im G_p^{R,(n+1)}(\omega)
\\
&=
{
-\frac{1}{\pi}
\frac{
\Im\Sigma_{p,c}^{R,(n)}(\omega)
}{
\left[
\omega-p^2/2-\Sigma_p^{x,{(n)}}
-\Re\Sigma_{p,c}^{R,(n)}(\omega)
\right]^2
+
\left[
\Im\Sigma_{p,c}^{R,(n)}(\omega)
\right]^2
}
}.
\end{align}
\subsubsection{Cumulant formalism }
Define 
\begin{equation}
    \xi_p^{(n)}
    =
p^2/2 + \Sigma_p^{x,{(n)}} + \Lambda_p^{(n)},
    % \epsilon_p^{{\rm HF},(n)}
    % +
    % \Lambda_p^{(n)},
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
\Lambda_p^{(n)}
=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\frac{\alpha_1}{2}\,
\Re\Sigma_{p,c}^{R,(n),2p1h}
\!\left(\xi_p^{(n)}\right),
& p\in\mathcal O,
\\[3mm]
\displaystyle
\frac{\alpha_2}{2}\,
\Re\Sigma_{p,c}^{R,(n),2h1p}
\!\left(\xi_p^{(n)}\right),
& p\in\mathcal V,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align}
\Re\Sigma_{p,c}^{R,(n),2p1h}(\omega)
&=
\mathcal{P}\!\int d\Omega\,
\frac{B_{p,2p1h}^{(n)}(\Omega)}
{\omega-\Omega},
\\
\Re\Sigma_{p,c}^{R,(n),2h1p}(\omega)
&=
\mathcal{P}\!\int d\Omega\,
\frac{B_{p,2h1p}^{(n)}(\Omega)}
{\omega-\Omega}.
\end{align}
This gives 
\begin{align}
C_p^{(2),(n)}(t)
&=
\int d\Omega\,
B_p^{(n)}(\Omega)
\frac{
e^{-i(\Omega-\xi_p^{(n)})t}
+i(\Omega-\xi_p^{(n)})t
-1
}{
(\Omega-\xi_p^{(n)})^2
}\\
&= \int d\Omega\,\frac{B_p^{(n)}(\Omega + \xi_p^{(n)})}{\Omega^2}
\left(
e^{-i\Omega t}
+i\Omega t
-1
\right).
\end{align}
Then, we get the Green's function via 
\begin{align}
G_p^{R,(n+1)}(t)
&=
-i\Theta(t)
e^{-i(p^2/2+\Sigma_p^{x,{(n)}})t}
e^{C_p^{(2),(n)}(t)}
\end{align}
\begin{tcolorbox}
    In the initial iteration, this takes on the one-shot form of 
\begin{align}
C_p^{(2),(0)}(t)
&= \int d\Omega\,\frac{B_p^{(0)}(\Omega+\xi_p^{(0)})}{\Omega^2}
\left(e^{-i\Omega t}+i\Omega t-1
\right)
\\
&=
\sum_{iab}\frac{\mathcal K_{pi}^{ab}}{(\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab})^2}
\left(e^{-i\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab} t}+i\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab} t-1\right)
+
\sum_{ija}\frac{\mathcal K_{ij}^{pa}}{(\tilde{\epsilon}_{pa}^{ij})^2}
\left(e^{-i\tilde{\epsilon}_{pa}^{ij} t}+i\tilde{\epsilon}_{pa}^{ij} t-1\right),
\end{align}
where $\tilde{\epsilon}_{pq}^{rs} = \tilde{\varepsilon}_r + \tilde{\varepsilon}_s - \tilde{\varepsilon}_p - \tilde{\varepsilon}_q - i0^+$ and $\mathcal{K}_{pq}^{rs}=(pr|qs)\left[2(pr|qs)-(ps|qr)\right]$.
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection{Why Garnet's HF+GW is wrong}
I was able to match Garnet's HF+GW and I think that was wrong. 
An ingredient of HF+GW is dRPA@HF, in which we use
\begin{equation}
    A_{ia,jb}(\mathbf{q}) = \delta_{ij} \delta_{ab} (\varepsilon_a^{\rm HF} - \varepsilon_i^{\rm HF}) + 2 (ia|bj)
\label{eq:dRPA_matrix}
\end{equation}
By Koopmans', $\varepsilon_a^{\rm HF}$ is the EA of the $a$-th virtual orbital, and $\varepsilon_i^{\rm HF}$ is the negative IP of the $i$-th occupied orbital. 
\subsubsection{The first thing you write }
Your paper writes that "inclusion of the Madelung constant in the Hamiltonian means the IP gets shifted by $-\frac{\xi}{2}$ and the EA gets shifted by $+\frac{\xi}{2}$". This then implies that in this shifted Ewald scheme the difference between virtual and occupied Hartree-Fock single-particle energies in equation \eqref{eq:dRPA_matrix} can be written as 
\begin{equation}
    \varepsilon_a^{\rm HF} - \varepsilon_i^{\rm HF} = \bar{\varepsilon}_a^{\rm HF} + \frac{\xi}{2} - \left(\bar{\varepsilon}_i^{\rm HF} + \frac{\xi}{2}\right) = \bar{\varepsilon}_a^{\rm HF} - \bar{\varepsilon}_i^{\rm HF},
\end{equation}
where $\bar{\varepsilon}_a^{\rm HF}$ and $\bar{\varepsilon}_i^{\rm HF}$ are the single-particle energies that one would get from standard Ewald summation, where all Madelung contribution is neglected.  So, computation of screening via dRPA@HF should never see a Madelung contribution.
\subsubsection{The second thing you write}
However, we know that the HF poles on the spectral function are not at $\bar{\varepsilon}_i^{\rm HF}+\frac{\xi}{2}$ and $\bar{\varepsilon}_a^{\rm HF}+\frac{\xi}{2}$, respectively, but rather at $\bar{\varepsilon}_i^{\rm HF} +\xi$ and $\bar{\varepsilon}_a^{\rm HF}$. This is because you then go on to write that "regardless of whether the Madelung constant is included in the Hamiltonian, there is an additional correction of $-\frac{\xi}{2}$ for both the IP and EA to remove spurious image interactions coming from the excess of charge created". 
\subsubsection{Why both things can be true}
The dRPA calculation computes neutral excitation energies, which never see an excess of charge. So it must deal with $\bar{\varepsilon}_i^{\rm HF}+\frac{\xi}{2}$ and $\bar{\varepsilon}_a^{\rm HF}+\frac{\xi}{2}$. Meanwhile, the HF spectral pole occurs at the frequency at which the excess of charge is created. So it must deal with $\bar{\varepsilon}_i^{\rm HF}+\xi$ and $\bar{\varepsilon}_a^{\rm HF}$.
\emph{So the IPs and EAs of the HF reference are not the same as the image-corrected HF poles of the physical spectral function.}
\subsection{Insights from the UEG Hamiltonian }
We begin from
\begin{align}
\hat H
&=
\sum_{\mathbf k}\epsilon_{\mathbf k}^{(0)}\hat a_{\mathbf k}^\dagger \hat a_{\mathbf k}
+
\underbrace{\frac{1}{2\Omega}
\sum_{\mathbf q\neq\mathbf 0}v_q
\sum_{\mathbf k\mathbf k'}
a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf k'}
a_{\mathbf k}}_{\hat V'},
\end{align}
where $\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}'$ run over all valid momenta, and $\mathbf{q}$ runs over all nonzero momentum transfers. $\epsilon_{\mathbf k}^{(0)}=\frac{k^2}{2}+\frac{v_M}{2}$ is the bare single-particle energy with $v_M=-2.837297 \times\left(\frac{3}{4 \pi}\right)^{1 / 3} N^{-1 / 3} r_s^{-1}$ the Madelung constant for the cubic simulation cell. We now normal order the two-body interaction $\hat V'$ with respect to
the HF reference $|\Phi_0\rangle$. Wick's theorem gives
\begin{align}
&a^\dagger a^\dagger a a
=
:a^\dagger a^\dagger a a:
+
\sum_{\text{single contractions}}
(\text{contraction})
:\!a^\dagger a\!:
+
\sum_{\text{complete contractions}}
(\text{contractions})
\\
\implies&
a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf k'}
a_{\mathbf k}\\
&=
:
a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf k'}
a_{\mathbf k}
:
\nonumber\\
&\quad
+
\wick{
\c1 a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf k'}
\c1 a_{\mathbf k}
}
:
a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf k'}
:
-
\wick{
\c1 a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf q}^\dagger
\c1 a_{\mathbf k'}
a_{\mathbf k}
}
:
a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf k}
:
\nonumber\\
&\quad
-
\wick{
a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q}^\dagger
\c1 a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf k'}
\c1 a_{\mathbf k}
}
:
a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf k'}
:
+
\wick{
a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q}^\dagger
\c1 a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf q}^\dagger
\c1 a_{\mathbf k'}
a_{\mathbf k}
}
:
a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf k}
:
\nonumber\\
&\quad
+
\wick{
\c1 a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q}^\dagger

\c2 a_{\mathbf k'}
\c1 a_{\mathbf k}
}
-
\wick{
\c1 a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q}^\dagger
\c2 a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf q}^\dagger
\c1 a_{\mathbf k'}
\c2 a_{\mathbf k}
}
\\
&=
:
a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf k'}
a_{\mathbf k}
:
\nonumber\\
&\quad
+
{
\delta_{\mathbf q,\mathbf 0}
n_{\mathbf k}
:

a_{\mathbf k'}
:
}

{

a_{\mathbf k}

\nonumber\\
&\quad
+

\delta_{\mathbf q,\mathbf 0}

\delta_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q,\mathbf k'}

\\[1ex]

&=

&=

\implies

&=
\sum_{\mathbf k}

+

\\

+

with ${

}

+

}

+
