$ and $E_{\rm HF} = \sum_{\mathbf k}
\epsilon_{\mathbf k}^{(0)}
n_{\mathbf k}
+
E_{\rm x}$.
\subsubsection{MP partitioning}
Partition the normal-ordered Hamiltonian into a zero-order part $\hat F$ and a perturbation $\hat V_{\mathrm{res}}$. Also, define the Madelung-free Fock operator $\hat F'=\hat F-\frac{v_M}{2}\hat N$ with eigenvalues $\epsilon'^{\rm HF}_{\mathbf k} = \frac{k^2}{2}-\frac{1}{\Omega}\sum_{\mathbf q\neq\mathbf 0}v_q n_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q}$. RSPT gives the second-order correlation energy as
\begin{align}
  E^{(2)}&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ijab}
  \frac{|\langle\Phi_{ij}^{ab}|\hat V_{\mathrm{res}}|\Phi_0\rangle|^2}{E^{(0)}_0-E^{(0)}_{ij,ab}}\\
&\overset{\text{RHF}}{=} \sum_{ijab}
  \frac{\mathcal K_{ij}^{ab}}{E'^{(0)}_0-E'^{(0)}_{ij,ab}}\\
&= E'^{(2)},
\end{align}
since, in the restricted formalism $\frac{1}{2}|\langle\Phi_{ij}^{ab}|\hat V_{\mathrm{res}}|\Phi_0\rangle|^2 \equiv \mathcal K_{ij}^{ab} =
(ia|jb)
\left[
2(ia|jb)-(ib|ja)
\right]$ and
\begin{align}
  E^{(0)}_{ij,ab}-E^{(0)}_0
  &=\langle\Phi_{ij}^{ab}|\hat F|\Phi_{ij}^{ab}\rangle-\langle\Phi_0|\hat F|\Phi_0\rangle
\\
  &=\langle\Phi_{ij}^{ab}|\hat F'|\Phi_{ij}^{ab}\rangle-\langle\Phi_0|\hat F'|\Phi_0\rangle
   +\frac{v_M}{2}\,\langle\Phi_{ij}^{ab}|\hat N-N|\Phi_{ij}^{ab}\rangle
\\
&= \underbrace{\langle\Phi_{ij}^{ab}|\hat F'|\Phi_{ij}^{ab}\rangle}_{E'^{(0)}_{ij,ab}}-\underbrace{\langle\Phi_0|\hat F'|\Phi_0\rangle}_{E'^{(0)}_0}
%   &=\ebar_a+\ebar_b-\ebar_i-\ebar_j .
  \label{eq:mp2denom}
\end{align}
So, we have shown that the MP2 correlation energy does not depend on the Madelung potential.
\subsubsection{Second-order self-energy}
The first iteration of BWs2 must satisfy the constraint $\operatorname{Tr}(\mathbf{W}^{O}) - \operatorname{Tr}(\mathbf{W}^{V}) = E^{(2)}_N$. To see this, consider the definitions
\begin{align}
{W}^{O}_{ii} &\equiv \frac{1}{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{ii}^{(2),2p1h}[\hat F](\omega = \epsilon_i^{\rm HF}) = \frac{1}{2}\sum_{a b j} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{ab}}{\epsilon^{\rm HF}_i + \epsilon^{\rm HF}_j - \epsilon^{\rm HF}_a - \epsilon^{\rm HF}_b}\\
&= \frac{1}{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{ii}^{(2),2p1h}[\hat F'](\omega = \epsilon'^{\rm HF}_i) =\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a b j} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{ab}}{\epsilon'^{\rm HF}_i + \epsilon'^{\rm HF}_j - \epsilon'^{\rm HF}_a - \epsilon'^{\rm HF}_b},\\
{W}^{V}_{aa} &\equiv \frac{1}{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{aa}^{(2),2h1p}[\hat F](\omega = \epsilon^{\rm HF}_a)= \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i j b} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{ab}}{\epsilon^{\rm HF}_a + \epsilon^{\rm HF}_b - \epsilon^{\rm HF}_i - \epsilon^{\rm HF}_j}\\
&= \frac{1}{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{aa}^{(2),2h1p}[\hat F'](\omega = \epsilon'^{\rm HF}_a) = \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i j b} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{ab}}{\epsilon'^{\rm HF}_a + \epsilon'^{\rm HF}_b - \epsilon'^{\rm HF}_i - \epsilon'^{\rm HF}_j}.
\end{align}
This implies that the second-order self-energy can be written as
\begin{align}
  \Sigma^{(2)}[F](\omega)&\equiv \bm{\Sigma}^{(2),2p1h}[F](\omega) + \bm{\Sigma}^{(2),2h1p}[F](\omega)  \\
&= \sum_{ia b} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{pi}^{ab}}{\omega - \epsilon^{\rm HF}_a - (\epsilon^{\rm HF}_b-\epsilon^{\rm HF}_i)} + \sum_{i j a} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{pa}}{\omega-\epsilon^{\rm HF}_i + (\epsilon^{\rm HF}_a - \epsilon^{\rm HF}_j)}\\
&= \sum_{ia b} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{pi}^{ab}}{\omega' - \epsilon'^{\rm HF}_a - (\epsilon'^{\rm HF}_b-\epsilon'^{\rm HF}_i)} + \sum_{i j a} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{pa}}{\omega'-\epsilon'^{\rm HF}_i + (\epsilon'^{\rm HF}_a - \epsilon'^{\rm HF}_j)}\label{eq:second_order_self_energy}\\
&\equiv \bm{\Sigma}^{(2),2p1h}[F'](\omega') + \bm{\Sigma}^{(2),2h1p}[F'](\omega'),
\end{align}
where $\omega' = \omega - \frac{v_M}{2}$.
Then, the following quasiparticle equations are the same:
\begin{align}
\omega - \epsilon^{\rm HF}_p - \bm{\Sigma}^{(2)}_{pp}[\hat F](\omega) &= 0\\
\omega' - \epsilon'^{\rm HF}_p - \bm{\Sigma}^{(2)}_{pp}[\hat F'](\omega') &= 0
\end{align}
The same expressions should hold when solving for HF+G0W0 quasiparticle energies, where one just makes the substitution $\bm{\Sigma}^{(2)} \to \bm{\Sigma}^{\rm GW}$, where the spectral representation is
\begin{align}
\bm{\Sigma}^{\rm G 0W0}_{pp}(\omega) &= \sum_{i \nu_{\bm{q}}} \frac{|g_{\nu}^{\rm pl}(\bm{k},\bm{q})|^2}{\omega - \epsilon^{\rm HF}_i - \omega_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}^{\rm pl} + i0^+} + \sum_{a \nu_{\bm{q}}} \frac{|g_{\nu}^{\rm pl}(\bm{k},\bm{q})|^2}{\omega - \epsilon^{\rm HF}_a + \omega_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}^{\rm pl} - i0^+},
\end{align}
with the plasmonic frequencies $\omega_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}^{\rm pl}$ and couplings $g_{\nu}^{\rm pl}(\bm{k},\bm{q})$ are computed from a Madelung-free polarizability for reasons discussed below.
\section{Deriving the electron-boson Hamiltonian}
We start from
\begin{align}
\hat H
&=
E_{\rm HF}
+
{\sum_{\mathbf k}
\epsilon_{\mathbf k}^{\rm HF}
:
\hat a_{\mathbf k}^\dagger \hat a_{\mathbf k}
:}
+
\hat V_{\mathrm{res}},
\end{align}
where $\hat V_{\mathrm{res}}=\frac{1}{2\Omega}
\sum_{\mathbf q\neq\mathbf 0}v_q
\sum_{\mathbf k\mathbf k'}
a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf q}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf k'}
a_{\mathbf k}$.
\subsubsection{Quadratic bosonic bath from orbital rotations}
Define the particle--hole excitation and de-excitation operators  $
    E_{i\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
    =
    a_{\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf i}$ and $
    E_{i\mathbf -q}
    =
    a_{\mathbf i}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q}$, respectively,
where $\mathbf i\in\mathcal O$ and
$\mathbf{i}\pm\mathbf q\in\mathcal V$. Define a vector of orbital-rotation amplitudes
$
    \boldsymbol{\kappa}_{\mathbf q}
    =
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \kappa_{1\mathbf q}\\
        \kappa_{2\mathbf q}\\
        \vdots
    \end{pmatrix}
$ and the doubled coordinate vector $
    {
    \mathbf z_{\mathbf q}
    =
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \boldsymbol{\kappa}_{\mathbf q}\\
        \boldsymbol{\kappa}_{-\mathbf q}^{*}
    \end{pmatrix}
    }
$. Then, the anti-Hermitian orbital-rotation generator is
\begin{align}
    \hat\kappa[\mathbf z]
    &=
    \sum_{i\mathbf q}
    \left[
        \kappa_{i\mathbf q}
        E_{i\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
        -
        \kappa_{i\mathbf q}^{*}
        E_{i\mathbf q}
    \right] \implies
    \hat\kappa^{\dagger}
    =
    -\hat\kappa.
\end{align}
Taking the single best Slater determinant in the HF $|\Phi_0\rangle$ as our equilibrium point, nearby Slater determinants are parameterized as
\begin{equation}
    |\Phi(\mathbf z)\rangle
    =
    e^{\hat\kappa[\mathbf z]}
    |\Phi_0\rangle,
\end{equation}
where $|\Phi_0\rangle$ is the Hartree--Fock determinant. The corresponding energy functional is
\begin{align}
    E(\mathbf z)
    &=
    \langle\Phi(\mathbf z)|
    \hat H
    |\Phi(\mathbf z)\rangle
    \\
    &=
    \langle\Phi_0|
    e^{-\hat\kappa[\mathbf z]}
    \hat H
    e^{\hat\kappa[\mathbf z]}
    |\Phi_0\rangle\\
    &=
    E_{\rm HF}
    +
    \langle\Phi_0|
    [\hat H,\hat\kappa[\mathbf z]]
    |\Phi_0\rangle
+
    \frac{1}{2}
    \langle\Phi_0|
    [[\hat H,\hat\kappa[\mathbf z]],\hat\kappa[\mathbf z]]
    |\Phi_0\rangle
    +
    \mathcal O(\kappa^3[\mathbf z]),
    \label{eq:energy_expansion_kappa}
\end{align}
where we used the BCH expansion $e^{-\hat\kappa[\mathbf z]}\hat H e^{\hat\kappa[\mathbf z]} = \hat H + [\hat H,\hat\kappa[\mathbf z]] + \frac{1}{2}[[\hat H,\hat\kappa[\mathbf z]],\hat\kappa[\mathbf z]] + \mathcal O(\kappa^3[\mathbf z])$. The generalized gradient of the energy is
\begin{align}
    \nabla_{\mathbf z}E(\mathbf z)
    &=
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \dfrac{\partial E(\mathbf z)}
        {\partial\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\\
        \\
        \dfrac{\partial E(\mathbf z)}
        {\partial\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{*}}
    \end{pmatrix} \implies    \left.
    \nabla_{\mathbf z}E(\mathbf z)
    \right|_{\mathbf z=0}
    =
    0.
\end{align}
since for all $i\mathbf q$, $
    \left.
    \frac{\partial E(\mathbf z)}
    {\partial\kappa_{i\mathbf q}}
    \right|_{\mathbf z=0}
    =
    \langle\Phi_0|
    [\hat H,E_{i\mathbf q}^{\dagger}]
    |\Phi_0\rangle=0
$ and $
    \left.
    \frac{\partial E(\mathbf z)}
    {\partial\kappa_{i,-\mathbf q}^{*}}
    \right|_{\mathbf z=0}
    =
    -
    \langle\Phi_0|
    [\hat H,E_{i,-\mathbf q}]
    |\Phi_0\rangle=0$ by the Brillouin theorem.
The Hessian is
\begin{align}
    \mathcal H_{\rm HF}
    =
    \left.
    \frac{\partial^2 E(\mathbf z)}
    {\partial
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \boldsymbol{\kappa}^{*}\\
        \boldsymbol{\kappa}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \partial
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \boldsymbol{\kappa}&
        \boldsymbol{\kappa}^{*}
    \end{pmatrix}}
    \right|_{\mathbf z=0}&=
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \displaystyle
        \frac{\partial^2E}
        {\partial\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{*}
         \partial\boldsymbol{\kappa}}
        &
        \displaystyle
        \frac{\partial^2E}
        {\partial\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{*}
         \partial\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{*}}
        \\[5mm]
        \displaystyle
        \frac{\partial^2E}
        {\partial\boldsymbol{\kappa}
         \partial\boldsymbol{\kappa}}
        &
        \displaystyle
        \frac{\partial^2E}
        {\partial\boldsymbol{\kappa}
         \partial\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{*}}
    \end{pmatrix}_{\mathbf z=0}
    \\
    &={
    \begin{pmatrix}
        A & B\\
        B^{*} & A^{*}
    \end{pmatrix}.
    }
\end{align}
The energy functional then becomes
\begin{align}
        E(\mathbf \kappa, \mathbf \kappa^{*})
    &= E_{\rm HF}
    +
    \frac{1}{2}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \boldsymbol{\kappa}_{\mathbf q}^{*} &
        \boldsymbol{\kappa}_{-\mathbf q}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        A & B\\
        B^{*} & A^{*}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \boldsymbol{\kappa}_{\mathbf q}\\
        \boldsymbol{\kappa}_{-\mathbf q}^{*}
    \end{pmatrix}
    +
    \mathcal O(\mathbf \kappa^3)\\
\implies E^{2}(\mathbf \kappa, \mathbf \kappa^{*})
    &=
    \frac{1}{2}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \boldsymbol{\kappa}_{\mathbf q}^{*} &
        \boldsymbol{\kappa}_{-\mathbf q}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        A & B\\
        B^{*} & A^{*}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \boldsymbol{\kappa}_{\mathbf q}\\
        \boldsymbol{\kappa}_{-\mathbf q}^{*}
    \end{pmatrix}
\end{align}
Quantizing the space as $\bm{\kappa}^{*}_{\mathbf q} \to \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger}$ and $\bm{\kappa}_{-\mathbf q} \to \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q}$, where $\hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger}$ and $\hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q}$ are bosonic creation and annihilation operators, respectively, leads to
\begin{align}
\hat{H}^{(2)}&= \frac{1}{2}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} &
        \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        A & B\\
        B^{*} & A^{*}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q}\\
        \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
    \end{pmatrix}
% &= \frac{1}{2}
%     \begin{pmatrix}
%         \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} &
%         \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q}
%     \end{pmatrix}
%     \begin{pmatrix}
%         A \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q} + B \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}\\
%         B^{*} \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q} + A^{*} \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
%     \end{pmatrix}\\
% &= \frac{1}{2}
%     \left[
%         \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} A \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q}
%         +
%         \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} B \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
%         +
%         \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q} B^{*} \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q}
%         +
%         \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q} A^{*} \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
%     \right]\\
% &= \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} A \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q}+\frac{1}{2}
%     \left[ \operatorname{Tr}(A) 
%         +
%         \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} B \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
%         +
%         \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q} B^{*} \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q}
%     \right],
\end{align}
% where in the final step we use the fact that normal ordering of the string leads to $\hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q} A^{*} \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger} = \operatorname{Tr}(A) + \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger} A^{*} \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q}$.
Redefining the bosonic operators according to the Bogoliubov transformation as
\begin{equation}
        \begin{pmatrix}
        \hat{\bm{b}}_{\mathbf q}\\
        \hat{\bm{b}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
    \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix}
        \mathbf X_{\mathbf q} & \mathbf Y_{\mathbf q}\\
        \mathbf Y_{-\mathbf q}^{*} & \mathbf X_{-\mathbf q}^{*}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathbf q}\\
        \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
    \end{pmatrix}, \quad \begin{pmatrix} \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} & \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{-\mathbf q} \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix}
        \mathbf b_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} & \mathbf b_{-\mathbf q}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \mathbf X_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} & \mathbf Y_{-\mathbf q}^{\rm T}\\
        \mathbf Y_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} & \mathbf X_{-\mathbf q}^{\rm T}
    \end{pmatrix},
\end{equation}
yields
\begin{align}
    \hat{H}^{(2)}
    &=
    \frac{1}{2}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} &
        \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{-\mathbf q}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \mathbf X_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} & \mathbf Y_{-\mathbf q}^{\rm T}\\
        \mathbf Y_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} & \mathbf X_{-\mathbf q}^{\rm T}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        A & B\\
        B^{*} & A^{*}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \mathbf X_{\mathbf q} & \mathbf Y_{\mathbf q}\\
        \mathbf Y_{-\mathbf q}^{*} & \mathbf X_{-\mathbf q}^{*}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathbf q}\\
        \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
    \end{pmatrix}\\
    &=
    \frac{1}{2}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} &
        \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{-\mathbf q}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\mathbf q} & 0\\
        0 & \boldsymbol{\Omega}_{-\mathbf q}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathbf q}\\
        \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
    \end{pmatrix}\\
    &=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}
        \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} &
        \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{-\mathbf q}
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\mathbf q} \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathbf q}\\
        \boldsymbol{\Omega}_{-\mathbf q} \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
    \end{pmatrix}\\
    &=
    \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathbf q}^{\dagger} \boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\mathbf q} \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathbf q}
    +
    \frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\mathbf q}-A)+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(A)\\
&= \sum_{\nu_{\mathbf q}} \omega_{\nu_{\mathbf q}} \hat{\beta}_{\nu_{\mathbf q}}^{\dagger} \hat{\beta}_{\nu_{\mathbf q}} + E_{\rm RPA} + \frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(A)
\end{align}
where $\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\mathbf q}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{-\mathbf q}$ are diagonal matrices of the RPA excitation energies and we once again used the fact that normal ordering of the string leads to $\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{-\mathbf q} \boldsymbol{\Omega}_{-\mathbf q} \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger} = \operatorname{Tr}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\mathbf q}) + \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger} \boldsymbol{\Omega}_{-\mathbf q} \hat{\bm{\beta}}_{-\mathbf q}$. 
\subsubsection{Fermionic one-hole Hamiltonian}
We can write
\begin{equation}
    \hat H_{\rm h}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})
    =
    \sum_{\mathbf k,\mathbf k'\in\mathcal O}
    h^{\rm h}_{\mathbf k'\mathbf k}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})
    h_{\mathbf k'}^\dagger h_{\mathbf k}.
    \label{eq:hole_hamiltonian_kappa}
\end{equation} Expanding to first order about the
Hartree--Fock reference gives
\begin{align}
    h^{\rm h}_{\mathbf k'\mathbf k}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})
    &=
    h^{\rm h}_{\mathbf k'\mathbf k}(0)+
    \sum_{i\mathbf q}
    \left.
    \frac{\partial
    h^{\rm h}_{\mathbf k'\mathbf k}}
    {\partial\kappa_{i\mathbf q}}
    \right|_{0}
    \kappa_{i\mathbf q}+
    \sum_{i\mathbf q}
    \left.
    \frac{\partial
    h^{\rm h}_{\mathbf k'\mathbf k}}
    {\partial\kappa_{i\mathbf q}^{*}}
    \right|_{0}
    \kappa_{i\mathbf q}^{*}
    +
    \mathcal O(\kappa^2),
    \label{eq:hole_hamiltonian_expansion}
\end{align}
% Because $\mathbf l\in\mathcal O$ while
% $\mathbf l+\mathbf q\in\mathcal V$,
% \begin{equation}
%     \langle \mathbf l+\mathbf q|
%     P_0
%     |\mathbf l\rangle
%     =
%     0.
% \end{equation}
% Using orthonormality,
% \begin{align}
%     \langle \mathbf l+\mathbf q
%     |\mathbf j+\mathbf Q\rangle
%     \langle \mathbf j|\mathbf l\rangle
%     &=
%     \delta_{\mathbf l,\mathbf j}
%     \delta_{\mathbf q,\mathbf Q},
% \end{align}
% while the conjugate term vanishes for this matrix element. Hence
% \begin{align}
%     P_{\mathbf l+\mathbf q,\mathbf l}
%     &=
%     \sum_{j\mathbf Q}
%     \kappa_{j\mathbf Q}
%     \delta_{\mathbf l,\mathbf j}
%     \delta_{\mathbf q,\mathbf Q}
%     +
%     \mathcal O(\kappa^2)
%     \\
%     &=
%     \kappa_{\mathbf l\mathbf q}
%     +
%     \mathcal O(\kappa^2).
% \end{align}

% Therefore,
% \begin{equation}
%     \boxed{
%     \left.
%     \frac{\partial
%     P_{\mathbf l+\mathbf q,\mathbf l}}
%     {\partial\kappa_{i\mathbf q}}
%     \right|_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}=0}
%     =
%     \delta_{\mathbf l,\mathbf i}.
%     }
%     \label{eq:density_kappa_derivative_delta}
% \end{equation}

% The derivative therefore vanishes for
% $\mathbf l\neq\mathbf i$ and equals unity for
% $\mathbf l=\mathbf i$. Consequently,
% \begin{align}
%     \left.
%     \frac{\partial
%     h^{\rm h}_{\mathbf k+\mathbf q,\mathbf k}}
%     {\partial\kappa_{i\mathbf q}}
%     \right|_0
%     &=
%     -
%     \sum_{\mathbf l}
%     \left.
%     \frac{\partial
%     F_{\mathbf k,\mathbf k+\mathbf q}}
%     {\partial
%     P_{\mathbf l+\mathbf q,\mathbf l}}
%     \right|_0
%     \left.
%     \frac{\partial
%     P_{\mathbf l+\mathbf q,\mathbf l}}
%     {\partial\kappa_{i\mathbf q}}
%     \right|_0
%     \\
%     &=
%     -
%     \sum_{\mathbf l}
%     \frac{v(\mathbf q)}{\Omega}
%     \delta_{\mathbf l,\mathbf i}
%     \\
%     &=
%     \boxed{
%     -\frac{v(\mathbf q)}{\Omega}.
%     }
% \end{align}
% where $h^{\rm h}_{\mathbf k'\mathbf k}(0)
    % = -\epsilon_{\mathbf k}^{\rm HF}\delta_{\mathbf k'\mathbf k}$.
 Consider
\begin{align}
    \left.
    \frac{\partial
    h^{\rm h}_{\mathbf k+\mathbf q,\mathbf k}}
    {\partial\kappa_{i\mathbf q}}
    \right|_0
    &=
    -
    \left.
    \frac{\partial
    F_{\mathbf k,\mathbf k+\mathbf q}}
    {\partial\kappa_{i\mathbf q}}
    \right|_0
    \\
    &=
    -
    \sum_{\mathbf l}
    \frac{\partial
    F_{\mathbf k,\mathbf k+\mathbf q}}
    {\partial P_{\mathbf l + \mathbf q,\mathbf l}}
    \left.
    \frac{\partial
    P_{\mathbf l + \mathbf q,\mathbf l}}
    {\partial\kappa_{i\mathbf q}}
    \right|_0 \\
&= 
    -
\frac{v(\mathbf q)}{\Omega} \sum_{\mathbf l}
    \left.
    \frac{\partial
    P_{\mathbf l + \mathbf q,\mathbf l}}
    {\partial\kappa_{i\mathbf q}}
    \right|_0\\
&= 
    -
\frac{v(\mathbf q)}{\Omega}
\end{align}
because, by definition 
\begin{align}
    P(\boldsymbol{\kappa})
    &=
    P_0
    +
    \sum_{j\mathbf Q}
    \left[
        \kappa_{j\mathbf Q}
        |j+\mathbf Q\rangle\langle j|
        +
        \kappa_{j\mathbf Q}^{*}
        |j\rangle\langle j+\mathbf Q|
    \right]
    +
    \mathcal O(\kappa^2)\\
\implies     P_{\mathbf l+\mathbf q,\mathbf l}
    &\equiv
    \langle \mathbf l+\mathbf q|
    P(\boldsymbol{\kappa})
    |\mathbf l\rangle
    \\
    &=
    \langle \mathbf l+\mathbf q|
    P_0
    |\mathbf l\rangle+
    \sum_{j\mathbf Q}
    \kappa_{j\mathbf Q}
    \langle \mathbf l+\mathbf q
    |\mathbf j+\mathbf Q\rangle
    \langle \mathbf j|\mathbf l\rangle+
    \sum_{j\mathbf Q}
    \kappa_{j\mathbf Q}^{*}
    \langle \mathbf l+\mathbf q
    |\mathbf j\rangle
    \langle \mathbf j+\mathbf Q|\mathbf l\rangle
    +
    \mathcal O(\kappa^2)\\
    \implies
    \left.
    \frac{\partial
    P_{\mathbf l+\mathbf q,\mathbf l}}
    {\partial\kappa_{i\mathbf q}}
    \right|_0
    &=
        \delta_{\mathbf l,i} + \mathcal O(\kappa).
\end{align}
where we truncated at linear order and $P_0=\sum_{i\in\mathcal O}|i\rangle\langle i|$ is the Hartree--Fock density matrix. Similar arguments will hold for the other derivative. Thus, keeping only terms linear in the particle--hole displacement yields
\begin{align}
    \hat H_{\rm h}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})
    &= -
    \sum_{\mathbf k\in\mathcal O}
    \epsilon_{\mathbf k}^{\rm HF}
    h_{\mathbf k}^{\dagger}h_{\mathbf k}
-
    \sum_{\mathbf k\mathbf q}
    \sum_i
    \frac{v(\mathbf q)}{\Omega}
    h_{\mathbf k+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}h_{\mathbf k}
    \left(
        \kappa_{i\mathbf q}
        +
        \kappa_{i,-\mathbf q}^{*}
    \right)
    +
    \mathcal O(\kappa^2).
    \label{eq:hole_hamiltonian_linear_kappa}
\end{align}
Again quantizing as $\kappa_{i\mathbf q} \to \hat b_{i\mathbf q}$ and $\kappa_{i,-\mathbf q}^{*} \to \hat b_{i,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}$, 
the linear coupling becomes
\begin{equation}
    \hat H_{\rm h-pl}
    =-\frac{1}{\Omega}
    \sum_{\mathbf k\mathbf q}
    \sum_i
    v(\mathbf q)
    h_{\mathbf k+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}h_{\mathbf k}
    \left(
        \hat b_{i\mathbf q}
        +
        \hat b_{i,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
    \right).
\end{equation}
Applying the same Bogoliubov transformation as above yields
\begin{align}
    \hat{\mathbf b}_{\mathbf q}
    +
    \hat{\mathbf b}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
    &=
    \left(
        X_{\mathbf q}
        +
        Y_{\mathbf q}
    \right)
    \left(
        \hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mathbf q}
        +
        \hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
    \right).
    \label{eq:x_plus_y_origin}
\end{align}
Substitution into $\hat H_{\rm h-ph}$ gives
\begin{align}
    \hat H_{\rm h-pl}
    &=
    \sum_{\mathbf k\mathbf q}
    \sum_{\nu_{\mathbf q}}
    g_{\nu}^{\rm pl}(\mathbf k,\mathbf q)
    h_{\mathbf k+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
    h_{\mathbf k}
    \left(
        \hat\beta_{\nu_{\mathbf q}}
        +
        \hat\beta_{\nu_{-\mathbf q}}^{\dagger}
    \right),
    \label{eq:hole_plasmon_coupling}
\end{align}
where the coupling to the collective RPA mode is
\begin{equation}
    {
    g_{\nu}^{\rm pl}(\mathbf k,\mathbf q)
    =
    -\frac{v(\mathbf q)}{\Omega}
    \sum_{\mathbf i}
    \left[
        X_{i\nu}(\mathbf q)
        +
        Y_{i\nu}(\mathbf q)
    \right].
    }
    \label{eq:screened_hole_plasmon_coupling}
\end{equation}
So overall, we have obtained (omitting constant terms)
\begin{equation}
    \hat H_{\rm eb}
    =
    -\sum_{\mathbf k\in\mathcal O}
    \epsilon_{\mathbf k}^{\rm HF}
    h_{\mathbf k}^{\dagger}h_{\mathbf k}
    +
    \sum_{\nu_{\mathbf q}}
    \omega_{\nu_{\mathbf q}}^{\rm pl}
    \hat\beta_{\nu_{\mathbf q}}^{\dagger}
    \hat\beta_{\nu_{\mathbf q}}
    +
    \sum_{\mathbf k\mathbf q}
    \sum_{\nu_{\mathbf q}}
    g_{\nu}^{\rm pl}(\mathbf k,\mathbf q)
    h_{\mathbf k+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
    h_{\mathbf k}
    \left(
        \hat\beta_{\nu_{\mathbf q}}
        +
        \hat\beta_{\nu_{-\mathbf q}}^{\dagger}
    \right).
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{A single charge carrier}
The ionization of a single electron has turned the previously neutral UEG into one with a single hole, which is our fermionic charge carrier. The hole may be created in any occupied state, so the fermionic contribution to the total Hamiltonian is \begin{equation}
    \hat{H}_{\mathrm{h}} = \sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{O}}
\epsilon_{\bm{k}}^{h}\,
h_{\bm{k}}^\dagger h_{\bm{k}},
\end{equation}
where $\epsilon_{\bm{k}}^{h}$ is the hole energy and $h_{\bm{k}}^\dagger$ (h$_{\bm{k}}$) creates (annihilates) a hole in the occupied state $\bm{k}$.

% \hat{H}_{\mathrm{h-pl}}
% &=

% +
% \hat{H}_{\mathrm{pl}}
% +

% \\
% &=

% +
% \sum_{\nu_{\bm{q}}\in\mathcal{M}}

% b_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}^{\dagger}

% \nonumber\\

% +

% g_{\nu}^{\rm pl}(\bm{k},\bm{q})

% +
% b_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}
% \right)
% \label{eq:eb_hamiltonian}
% \end{align}
% and $\hat H_N=\hat F + \hat V_{\mathrm{res}}$.
\subsubsection{Charge-neutral particle-hole fluctuations up to quadratic order}
A second contribution to the total Hamiltonian is through a bath of neutral particle-hole fluctuations. 
A way to think about this is that at each wavevector accessible to the hole, we place a harmonic oscillator in the N-dimensional Hilbert space, whose equilibrium is at the single best Slater determinant, or the HF state. We want to consider fluctuations about this equilibrium, as generated by OV rotation, to lowest non-trivial order, which is quadratic. 
Specifically, with $\Phi_0$ the HF reference, we define a particle-hole excited state as
\begin{align}
    |\Phi(\kappa)\rangle &= e^{\sum_{ia}\kappa_{ia}a_a^\dagger a_i}|\Phi_0\rangle \\
    &\approx \left(1 + \sum_{ia}\kappa_{ia}a_a^\dagger a_i + \frac{1}{2}\sum_{ia,jb}\kappa_{ia}\kappa_{jb}a_a^\dagger a_i a_b^\dagger a_j\right)|\Phi_0\rangle,
\end{align}
\begin{align}
    \hat H_{\rm ph}^{(2)}
    &=
    E_{\rm n}
    +
    \sum_{\mathbf q}\sum_{ij}
    A_{ij}(\mathbf q)
    E_{i\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
    E_{j\mathbf q}
    \nonumber\\
    &\quad
    +
    \frac{1}{2}
    \sum_{\mathbf q}\sum_{ij}
    \left[
        B_{ij}(\mathbf q)
        E_{i\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
        E_{j,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
        +
        B_{ij}^{*}(\mathbf q)
        E_{j,-\mathbf q}
        E_{i\mathbf q}
    \right],
    \label{eq:quadratic_neutral_hamiltonian}
\end{align}
Define the particle--hole excitation and de-excitation operators  $
    E_{i\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
    =
    a_{\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf i}$ and $
    E_{i,-\mathbf q}
    =
    a_{\mathbf i}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q}$, respectively,
where $\mathbf i\in\mathcal O$ and
$\mathbf{i}\pm\mathbf q\in\mathcal V$. The space defined by these operators is $\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf q}=\text{span}\{E_{i\mathbf q}^{\dagger},E_{i,-\mathbf q}\}$. Introduce $\mathcal{P}$, which is a projection onto $\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf q}$, and $\mathcal{Q} = 1-\mathcal{P}$. The EOM for the particle--hole operators is
\begin{align}
-i\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}
E_{i\mathbf q}^{\dagger}\\
E_{i,-\mathbf q}
\end{pmatrix}
&= \begin{pmatrix}
\left[\hat H_N,E_{i\mathbf q}^{\dagger}\right]\\
\left[\hat H_N,E_{i,-\mathbf q}\right]
\end{pmatrix}\\
&= \begin{pmatrix}
\mathcal{P}\left[\hat H_N,E_{i\mathbf q}^{\dagger}\right]\\
\mathcal{P}\left[\hat H_N,E_{i,-\mathbf q}\right]
\end{pmatrix}
+
\begin{pmatrix}
\mathcal{Q}\left[\hat H_N,E_{i\mathbf q}^{\dagger}\right]\\
\mathcal{Q}\left[\hat H_N,E_{i,-\mathbf q}\right]
\end{pmatrix}\\
&\approx \begin{pmatrix}
\mathcal{P}\left[\hat H_N,E_{i\mathbf q}^{\dagger}\right]\\
\mathcal{P}\left[\hat H_N,E_{i,-\mathbf q}\right]
\end{pmatrix}\\
&= \begin{pmatrix}
\sum_{j} \left[A_{ji}(\mathbf q) E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger} + B_{ji}(\mathbf q) E_{j,-\mathbf q}\right]\\
-\sum_{j} \left[A_{ji}^{*}(\mathbf q) E_{j,-\mathbf q} + B_{ji}^{*}(\mathbf q) E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger}\right]
\end{pmatrix} \label{eq:ph_eom_final}
\end{align}
We can always write equation \eqref{eq:ph_eom_final} because the projected commutators belong to
$\mathcal S_{\mathbf q}$, so they must be expressible as linear
combinations of the basis operators spanning that space. The second equation follows from Hermitian conjugation of the first.

Since for $\mathbf{q} \neq {0}$, $\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
\left[
E_{i\mathbf q},
E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\right]
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle
=
\delta_{ij}$ 
\footnote{\begin{align}
\left[
E_{i\mathbf q},
E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\right]
&=
\left[
a_{\mathbf i}^{\dagger}
a_{\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q},
a_{\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
a_{\mathbf j}
\right]
\\
&=
\delta_{ij}
\left(
a_{\mathbf i}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf i}
-
a_{\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
a_{\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q}
\right)
\end{align}
because $\mathbf i$ is occupied while
$\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q$ is virtual.}
and $\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
\left[
E_{i\mathbf q},
E_{j,-\mathbf q}
\right]\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle
=
0$, 
\begin{align}
A_{ij}(\mathbf q)&=\sum_k
A_{kj}(\mathbf q)\delta_{ik}\\
&= \sum_k
A_{kj}(\mathbf q)
\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
\left[
E_{i\mathbf q},
E_{k\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\right]
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle
+
\sum_k
B_{kj}(\mathbf q)
\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
\left[
E_{i\mathbf q},
E_{k,-\mathbf q}
\right]
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle\\
&=
\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
\left[
E_{i\mathbf q},
\mathcal P[\hat H_N,E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger}]
\right]
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle\\
&=
\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
\left[
E_{i\mathbf q},
[\hat F,E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger}]
\right]
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle
+
\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
\left[
E_{i\mathbf q},
[\hat V_{\rm res},E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger}]
\right]
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle\\
&= \left( \epsilon^{\rm HF}_{\mathbf i+\mathbf{q}} - \epsilon^{\rm HF}_{\mathbf i} \right)\delta_{ij}
+ \langle aj||ib\rangle\\
&= \left( \epsilon^{\rm HF}_{\mathbf i+\mathbf{q}} - \epsilon^{\rm HF}_{\mathbf i} \right)\delta_{ij}
+ \frac{1}{\Omega}
\Big[
v_q
-
\left(1-\delta_{ij}\right)v_{|\mathbf i-\mathbf j|}
\Big]
\label{eq:A_Fock_plus_interaction}
\end{align}
where $a=\mathbf{i}+\mathbf{q}$ and $b=\mathbf{j}+\mathbf{q}$ and in the penultimate step, we simplified the Fock operator contribution
\footnote{
\begin{align}
[\hat F,E_{i\mathbf q}^{\dagger}]
&=
\left[
\sum_{\mathbf p}
\epsilon_{\mathbf p}^{\rm HF}
:\!a_{\mathbf p}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf p}\!:,
\,
a_{\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf i}
\right]
\\
% &=
% \sum_{\mathbf p}
% \epsilon_{\mathbf p}^{\rm HF}
% \left[
% a_{\mathbf p}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf p},
% \,
% a_{\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf i}
% \right]
% \\
&=
\sum_{\mathbf p}
\epsilon_{\mathbf p}^{\rm HF}
\left(
\left[
a_{\mathbf p}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf p},
a_{\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\right]
a_{\mathbf i}
+
a_{\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\left[
a_{\mathbf p}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf p},
a_{\mathbf i}
\right]
\right)
\\
&=
\sum_{\mathbf p}
\epsilon_{\mathbf p}^{\rm HF}
\left(
\delta_{\mathbf p,\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q}
a_{\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf i}
-
\delta_{\mathbf p,\mathbf i}
a_{\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf i}
\right)
\\
&=
\left(
\epsilon_{\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q}^{\rm HF}
-
\epsilon_{\mathbf i}^{\rm HF}
\right)
a_{\mathbf{i}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf i}.
\end{align}}
, and we used the Slater-Condon rule.
Similarly, since
$\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
\left[
E_{i,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger},
E_{k,-\mathbf q}
\right]
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle
=
-\delta_{ik}$
and
$\langle \Phi_0|\left[
E_{i,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger},
E_{k\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\right]|\Phi_0\rangle
=
0$,
\begin{align}
B_{ij}(\mathbf q)
&=
-\sum_k
B_{kj}(\mathbf q)
\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
\left[
E_{i,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger},
E_{k,-\mathbf q}
\right]
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle
-
\sum_k
A_{kj}(\mathbf q)
\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
\left[
E_{i,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger},
E_{k\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\right]
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle
\\
&=
-\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
\left[
E_{i,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger},
\mathcal P[\hat H_N,E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger}]
\right]
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle
\\
&=
-\left( \epsilon^{\rm HF}_{\mathbf{j}+\mathbf{q}} - \epsilon^{\rm HF}_{\mathbf j} \right)
\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
\left[
E_{i,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger},
E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\right]
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle
-
\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
\left[
E_{i,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger},
[\hat V_{\rm res},E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger}]
\right]
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle
\\
&=
-
\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
E_{i,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}\hat V_{\rm res}E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
-
E_{i,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger}\hat V_{\rm res}
-
\hat V_{\rm res}E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger}E_{i,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
+
E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger}\hat V_{\rm res}E_{i,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle
\\
&=
\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
\hat V_{\rm res}\,
E_{j\mathbf q}^{\dagger}\,
E_{i,-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle\\
&=
\frac{1}{2\Omega}
\sum_{\mathbf p\neq\mathbf 0}v_p
\sum_{\mathbf k\mathbf k'}
\left\langle\Phi_0\left|
:
a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf p}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf p}^\dagger
a_{\mathbf k'}
a_{\mathbf k}
:
a_{\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
a_{\mathbf j}
a_{\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
a_{\mathbf i}
\right|\Phi_0\right\rangle
\\
&=
\frac{1}{2\Omega}
\sum_{\mathbf p\neq\mathbf 0}v_p
\sum_{\mathbf k\mathbf k'}
\left[
\wick{
\c1 a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf p}^\dagger
\c2 a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf p}^\dagger
\c3 a_{\mathbf k'}
\c4 a_{\mathbf k}
\c3 a_{\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\c2 a_{\mathbf j}
\c4 a_{\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\c1 a_{\mathbf i}
}
+
\wick{
\c1 a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf p}^\dagger
\c2 a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf p}^\dagger
\c3 a_{\mathbf k'}
\c4 a_{\mathbf k}
\c4 a_{\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\c1 a_{\mathbf j}
\c3 a_{\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\c2 a_{\mathbf i}
}
\right.
\nonumber\\
&\qquad\left.
-
\wick{
\c1 a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf p}^\dagger
\c2 a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf p}^\dagger
\c3 a_{\mathbf k'}
\c4 a_{\mathbf k}
\c4 a_{\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\c2 a_{\mathbf j}
\c3 a_{\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\c1 a_{\mathbf i}
}
-
\wick{
\c1 a_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf p}^\dagger
\c2 a_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf p}^\dagger
\c3 a_{\mathbf k'}
\c4 a_{\mathbf k}
\c3 a_{\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\c1 a_{\mathbf j}
\c4 a_{\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q}^{\dagger}
\c2 a_{\mathbf i}
}
\right]
\\
&=
\frac{1}{2\Omega}
\sum_{\mathbf p\neq\mathbf 0}v_p
\sum_{\mathbf k\mathbf k'}
\Big[
\delta_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf p,\mathbf i}\,n_{\mathbf i}\;
\delta_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf p,\mathbf j}\,n_{\mathbf j}\;
\delta_{\mathbf k',\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q}(1-n_{\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q})\;
\delta_{\mathbf k,\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q}(1-n_{\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q})
\nonumber\\
&\qquad
+
\delta_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf p,\mathbf j}\,n_{\mathbf j}\;
\delta_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf p,\mathbf i}\,n_{\mathbf i}\;
\delta_{\mathbf k',\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q}(1-n_{\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q})\;
\delta_{\mathbf k,\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q}(1-n_{\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q})
\nonumber\\
&\qquad
-
\delta_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf p,\mathbf i}\,n_{\mathbf i}\;
\delta_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf p,\mathbf j}\,n_{\mathbf j}\;
\delta_{\mathbf k',\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q}(1-n_{\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q})\;
\delta_{\mathbf k,\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q}(1-n_{\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q})
\nonumber\\
&\qquad
-
\delta_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf p,\mathbf j}\,n_{\mathbf j}\;
\delta_{\mathbf{k}'-\mathbf p,\mathbf i}\,n_{\mathbf i}\;
\delta_{\mathbf k',\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q}(1-n_{\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q})\;
\delta_{\mathbf k,\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q}(1-n_{\mathbf{i}-\mathbf q})
\Big]
\\[1ex]
&\overset{\substack{\mathbf i,\mathbf j\in\mathcal O\\ \mathbf{i}-\mathbf q,\,\mathbf{j}+\mathbf q\in\mathcal V}}{=}
\frac{1}{2\Omega}
\Big[
\underbrace{v_{\mathbf q}}_{\mathbf p=\mathbf q}
+
\underbrace{v_{-\mathbf q}}_{\mathbf p=-\mathbf q}
-
\underbrace{v_{\mathbf i-\mathbf j-\mathbf q}}_{\mathbf p=\mathbf i-\mathbf j-\mathbf q}
-
\underbrace{v_{\mathbf j-\mathbf i+\mathbf q}}_{\mathbf p=\mathbf j-\mathbf i+\mathbf q}
\Big]
\\
&=
\frac{1}{\Omega}
\Big[
v_q
-
v_{|\mathbf i-\mathbf j-\mathbf q|}
\Big].
\end{align}
\subsubsection{Coupling of the hole to particle-hole fluctuations up to linear order}
\subsection{Electron-boson Hamiltonian}
Given a set of hole energies $\{\epsilon_{\bm{k}}^{h}=-\epsilon_{\bm{k}}^{\rm HF}\}$, as well as plasmonic frequencies $\{\omega_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}^{\rm pl}\}$ and couplings $\{g_{\nu}^{\rm pl}(\bm{k},\bm{q})\}$, we can write the effective electron--boson Hamiltonian as
\begin{align}
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eb}}
&=
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{el}}
+
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{b}}
+
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eb,int}}
\\
&=
\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{O}}
\epsilon_{\bm{k}}^{h}\,
h_{\bm{k}}^\dagger h_{\bm{k}}
+
\sum_{\nu_{\bm{q}}\in\mathcal{M}}
\omega_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}^{\rm pl}
b_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}^{\dagger}
b_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}
\nonumber\\
&\quad
+
\sum_{\bm{k},\bm{k}+\bm{q}\in\mathcal{O}}
\sum_{\nu_{\bm{q}}\in\mathcal{M}}
g_{\nu}^{\rm pl}(\bm{k},\bm{q})
h_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}}^{\dagger}
h_{\bm{k}}
\left(
b_{\nu_{-\bm{q}}}^{\dagger}
+
b_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}
\right).
\label{eq:eb_hamiltonian}
\end{align}
The Brillouin--Wigner construction chooses to repartition
\begin{equation}
    \hat{H}_{\mathrm{eb}}
    =
    \hat{H}_{\mathrm{BW}}^{(0)}
    +
    \hat{H}_{\mathrm{BW}}^{(1)}
\end{equation}
with
\begin{align}
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{BW}}^{(0)}
&=
\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{O}}
\underbrace{\left(\epsilon_{\bm{k}}^{h} + W_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}\right)}_{\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}}}
h_{\bm{k}}^\dagger h_{\bm{k}}
+
\sum_{\nu_{\bm{q}}\in\mathcal{M}}
\omega_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}^{\rm pl}
b_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}^{\dagger}
b_{\nu_{\bm{q}}},
\end{align}
where our fermionic space just consists of occupied states so
\begin{equation}
    {W}_{\bm{k}\bm{k}} = \alpha \Sigma^{(2)}_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}(\omega=\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}}) = \alpha \sum_{\substack{\bm{k}+\bm{q}\in\mathcal{O}\\\nu_{\bm{q}}\in\mathcal{M}}} \frac{\left|g_{\nu}^{\rm pl}(\bm{k},\bm{q})\right|^2}{\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}}-\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}}-\omega_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}^{\rm pl}}.
\end{equation}
{The upshot is that, before we do anything, we need to solve for this modified BW reference. }This implies that we must start by finding a self-consistent solution to the matrix equation
\begin{equation}
    \left(\mathbf{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{HF}}+\alpha \mathbf{W}[\bm{\tilde{\varepsilon}}_h]\right) \bm{U} = \bm{\tilde{\varepsilon}}_h \bm{U},
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{HF}}=\begin{pmatrix}
\epsilon_{\bm{k}_1}^{h} & 0 & \cdots & 0 \\
0 & \epsilon_{\bm{k}_2}^{h} & \cdots & 0 \\
\vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\
0 & 0 & \cdots & \epsilon_{\bm{k}_N}^{h}
\end{pmatrix}$ is the Fock matrix in the occupied space, and $\bm{\tilde{\varepsilon}}_h = \begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}_1} & 0 & \cdots & 0 \\
0 & \tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}_2} & \cdots & 0 \\
\vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\
0 & 0 & \cdots & \tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}_N}
\end{pmatrix}$ is the diagonal matrix of the modified BW reference energies. 
To leave the full Hamiltonian unchanged, the perturbation is defined as
\begin{align}
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{BW}}^{(1)}
&=
\sum_{\bm{k},\bm{k}+\bm{q}\in\mathcal{O}}
\sum_{\nu_{\bm{q}}\in\mathcal{M}}
g_{\nu}^{\rm pl}(\bm{k},\bm{q})
h_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}}^{\dagger}
h_{\bm{k}}
\left(
b_{\nu_{-\bm{q}}}^{\dagger}
+
b_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}
\right)
-
\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathcal{O}}
W_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}
h_{\bm{k}}^\dagger h_{\bm{k}}.
\end{align}
\subsubsection{BWs2+C for the electron-boson Hamiltonian}
The derivation remains the same until the step
\begin{align}
C_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}^{\mathrm{BWs2}}(t)
&=
i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi}
e^{-i(\omega - \tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}})t}
\frac{\Sigma^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}(\omega)}
     {(\omega - \tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}})^2} \\
&=
i \int \frac{d\omega'}{2\pi}
e^{-i\omega' t}
\frac{\Sigma^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}(\omega' + \tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}})}
     {(\omega')^2}. %\\
% &=
% \int d\omega\,
% \frac{\beta_{pp}(\omega)}{\omega^2}
% \left(
% e^{-i \omega t}
% + i \omega t
% - 1
% \right),
\end{align}
% where
% \[
% \beta_{pp}(\omega)
% =
% \frac{1}{\pi}
% \operatorname{Im}
% \Sigma_{\mathrm{eff},pp}
% (\omega + \tilde{\varepsilon}_p)
% \]
% is the cumulant kernel for the BWs2+C method.
Now, we have 
\begin{align}
\Sigma^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}(\omega) &=
\Sigma_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}^{(2)}\!\left[ G^{(0)'} \right](\omega)
-  W_{\bm{k}\bm{k}} \\
&=
\Sigma_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}^{(2)}\!\left[ G^{(0)'} \right](\omega)
- \alpha \Sigma_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}^{(2)}\!\left[ G^{(0)'} \right](\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}})\\
\implies \Sigma^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}(\omega + \tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}}) &=
\Sigma_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}^{(2)}\!\left[ G^{(0)'} \right](\omega + \tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}})
- \alpha \Sigma_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}^{(2)}\!\left[ G^{(0)'} \right](\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}})\\
&= \sum_{\substack{\bm{k}+\bm{q}\in\mathcal{O}\\\nu_{\bm{q}}\in\mathcal{M}}} \frac{\left|g_{\nu}^{\rm pl}(\bm{k},\bm{q})\right|^2}{\omega+\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}}-\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}}-\omega_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}^{\rm pl}} - \alpha \sum_{\substack{\bm{k}+\bm{q}\in\mathcal{O}\\\nu_{\bm{q}}\in\mathcal{M}}} \frac{\left|g_{\nu}^{\rm pl}(\bm{k},\bm{q})\right|^2}{\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}}-\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}}-\omega_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}^{\rm pl}}.
\end{align}
where we identify $\tilde{\epsilon}^\nu(\bm{k},\bm{q}) = -\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}}+\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}}+\omega_{\nu_{\bm{q}}}^{\rm pl}$.
%  Putting this together, the effective self-energy is
% \begin{equation}
%     \Sigma_{\mathrm{eff},pp}(\omega + \tilde{\varepsilon}_p) = 
%     \frac{1}{2} \sum_{iab} \langle p i \| a b\rangle^2 \frac{1}{\omega - \tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab}} 
%     + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{ija} \langle p a \| i j\rangle^2 \frac{\omega}{\tilde{\epsilon}_{pa}^{ij}(\omega - \tilde{\epsilon}_{pa}^{ij})}.
% \end{equation}
Using the partial fraction decomposition\footnote{
\begin{equation}
    \frac{1}{\omega^2(\omega - \epsilon)} = \frac{1}{\epsilon^2} \frac{1}{\omega - \epsilon} - \frac{1}{\epsilon^2} \frac{1}{\omega} - \frac{1}{\epsilon} \frac{1}{\omega^2}
\end{equation}}
and the time-domain integral identities\footnote{
\begin{align}
    i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t} \frac{1}{\omega - \epsilon} &= e^{-i\epsilon t} \\
    i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t} \frac{1}{\omega} &= 1 \\
    i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t} \frac{1}{\omega^2} &= -it,
\end{align}}
we find:
% Now, integral to be solved for the 2p1h term is:
\begin{align}
    I^{\alpha}(t) &= i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} \frac{e^{-i\omega t}}{\omega^2} \left( \frac{1}{\omega - \tilde{\epsilon}^\nu(\bm{k},\bm{q})} + \frac{\alpha}{\tilde{\epsilon}^\nu(\bm{k},\bm{q})} \right) \\
&= \frac{e^{-i\tilde{\epsilon}^\nu(\bm{k},\bm{q}) t} + i \left( 1-\alpha \right) \tilde{\epsilon}^\nu(\bm{k},\bm{q}) t - 1}{(\tilde{\epsilon}^\nu(\bm{k},\bm{q}))^2}.
%     &= i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t} \left( \frac{1}{(\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab})^2} \frac{1}{\omega - \tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab}} - \frac{1}{(\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab})^2} \frac{1}{\omega} - \frac{1}{\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab}} \frac{1}{\omega^2} \right) \\
%  &= \frac{1}{(\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab})^2} e^{-i\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab}t} - \frac{1}{(\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab})^2}(1) - \frac{1}{\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab}}(-it) \nonumber \\
%     &= \frac{e^{-i\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab} t} + i\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab} t - 1}{(\tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab})^2}
\end{align}
This yields the final form:
\begin{equation}
    C_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}^{(2,\alpha)}(t) = \sum_{\substack{\bm{k}+\bm{q}\in\mathcal{O}\\\nu_{\bm{q}}\in\mathcal{M}}} \frac{\left|g_{\nu}^{\rm pl}(\bm{k},\bm{q})\right|^2}{(\tilde{\epsilon}^\nu(\bm{k},\bm{q}))^2} \left( e^{-i \tilde{\epsilon}^\nu(\bm{k},\bm{q}) t} + i \left( 1-\alpha \right) \tilde{\epsilon}^\nu(\bm{k},\bm{q}) t - 1 \right)
\end{equation}
Thus, the retarded Green's function in the BWs2+C method will read
\begin{equation}
G_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}^{\mathrm{(2,\alpha)+C}}(t) = -i \theta(t) e^{-i\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}} t} \exp\left\{C_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}^{(2,\alpha)}(t)\right\}.
\end{equation}
\subsubsection{Why BWs2 doesn't diverge in the TDL of the UEG}
To show this property, it is sufficient to show that the BWs2 nonlinear eigenvalue equation of
\begin{equation}
    \left(\mathbf{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{HF}}+\alpha \mathbf{W}[\bm{\tilde{\varepsilon}}_h]\right) \bm{U} = \bm{\tilde{\varepsilon}}_h \bm{U}
\end{equation}
has a well-defined solution in the TDL.% for infinitely separated particles 
 Since we know that Hartree-Fock does not diverge in the TDL, we just need to show this same property for the regularizer. For the arbitrary hole state $\bm{k}$, the
 regularizer $\bm{W}\propto \bm{\Sigma}^{(2)}$ has the form
\begin{align}
\Sigma^{(2)}_{\bm{k}\bm{k}}(\omega&=\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}}) = \int^{\infty}_0 \frac{d^3 \bm{q}}{(2 \pi)^3} \frac{|g(\bm{q})|^2}{\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}}-\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}}-\omega_{\mathrm{pl}}(\bm{q})}\\
&=\underbrace{\int^{\bm{q}_c}_{0} \frac{d^3 \bm{q}}{(2 \pi)^3} \frac{|g(\bm{q})|^2}{\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}}-\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}}-\omega_{\mathrm{pl}}(\bm{q})}}_{\bm{W}_{\bm{k}}^{\rm IR}}+\int^{\infty}_{\bm{q}_c} \frac{d^3 \bm{q}}{(2 \pi)^3} \frac{|g(\bm{q})|^2}{\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}}-\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}}-\omega_{\mathrm{pl}}(\bm{q})},
\label{eq:W_regularizer}
\end{align} where we have introduced the infitesimally small plasma frequency cutoff $\bm{q}_c$. We are confident that the second term does not diverge, so it suffices to show that $\bm{W}_{\bm{k}}^{\rm IR}$ does not diverge.
For a fixed hole momentum $\bm k$, define
\begin{equation}
D_{\bm k}(\bm q)
=
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm k}
-
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{\bm{k}+\bm q}
-
\omega_{\rm pl}(q).
\end{equation}
We consider the contribution to the regularizer from an arbitrarily
small neighborhood $|\bm{q}_c|<\delta$ of the origin,
\begin{equation}
W_{\bm k}^{\rm IR}(\delta)
=
\int_{\substack{|\bm q_c|<\delta\\
\bm{k}+\bm q_c\in\mathcal O}}
\frac{d^3q}{(2\pi)^3}
\frac{|g(q)|^2}{D_{\bm k}(\bm q)}.
\end{equation}
In the long-wavelength limit of the 3D UEG,
the plasmon-pole representation, which is exact in the TDL, gives
\begin{equation}
|g(q)|^2
=
\frac{2\pi e^2\omega_p}{q^2},
\qquad q\rightarrow0,
\end{equation}
while continuity of the fermionic dispersion and
$\omega_{\rm pl}(q)\rightarrow\omega_p$ imply
\begin{equation}
\lim_{\bm q\rightarrow0}
D_{\bm k}(\bm q)
=
-\omega_p.
\end{equation}
Since the bulk Sommerfeld plasma frequency $\omega_p>0$, the definition of this limit guarantees the
existence of a $\delta_0>0$ such that, for
$0<q<\delta_0$,
\begin{align}
\left|
D_{\bm k}(\bm q)+\omega_p
\right|
&<
\frac{\omega_p}{2}\\
\implies 
|D_{\bm k}(\bm q)|
&>
\frac{\omega_p}{2}\\
\implies
\frac{1}{|D_{\bm k}(\bm q)|}
&<
\frac{2}{\omega_p}.
\end{align}
For any $\delta<\delta_0$, it therefore follows that
\begin{align}
\left|W_{\bm k}^{\rm IR}(\delta)\right|
&\leq
\int_{|\bm q_c|<\delta}
\frac{d^3q}{(2\pi)^3}
\frac{|g(q)|^2}{|D_{\bm k}(\bm q)|}
\\
&<
\frac{2}{\omega_p}
\int_{|\bm q_c|<\delta}
\frac{d^3q}{(2\pi)^3}
\frac{2\pi e^2\omega_p}{q^2}.
\end{align}
Using $d^3q=4\pi q^2dq$ gives
\begin{align}
\left|W_{\bm k}^{\rm IR}(\delta)\right|
&<
\frac{2e^2}{\pi}
\int_0^\delta dq
\\
&=
\frac{2e^2}{\pi}\delta.
\end{align}
Therefore,
\begin{equation}
{
\lim_{\delta\rightarrow0^+}
W_{\bm k}^{\rm IR}(\delta)=0.
}
\end{equation}
and there is no infrared
divergence of the Brillouin--Wigner regularizer in the TDL of the 3D UEG.


\subsubsection{Numerator}
The plasmon-pole representation of the
correlation part of the screened Coulomb interaction, which is exact in the TDL, is
\begin{align}
W_c(q,\omega)
&=
\frac{|g(q)|^2}
{\omega-\omega_p+i0^+}
-
\frac{|g(q)|^2}
{\omega+\omega_p-i0^+}\\
&=
\frac{
2\omega_p |g(q)|^2
}{
\omega^2-\omega_p^2
}.
\label{eq:ppm_bosonic}
\end{align}
Now, formally, the correlation part of the screened Coulomb interaction is defined as
\begin{align}
W_c(q,\omega)
&=
\frac{v(q)}{\epsilon(q,\omega)}-v(q)
\nonumber\\
&=
v(q)
\frac{\omega_p^2}
{\omega^2-\omega_p^2}
\qquad
(q\rightarrow0),
\label{eq:ppm_screened}
\end{align}
because the long-wavelength dielectric function satisfies
$
\lim_{q\rightarrow0}
\epsilon(q,\omega)=
1-\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2}.
$
Setting Eqs.~\eqref{eq:ppm_bosonic} and
\eqref{eq:ppm_screened} equal to each other gives
\begin{align}
2\omega_p|g(q)|^2
&=
v(q)\omega_p^2 \\
\implies |g(q)|^2
&=
\frac{2\pi e^2\omega_p}{q^2},
\label{eq:g_long_wavelength}
\end{align}
since $v(q)=4\pi e^2/q^2$ in three dimensions.

\subsubsection{Comparison with the ordinary spectral function }
Defining
\begin{equation}
    \tilde{\Sigma}_p^{(2), R}(\omega)= \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i a b} \frac{|\langle p i \| a b\rangle|^2}{\omega+\tilde{\varepsilon}_i-\tilde{\varepsilon}_a-\tilde{\varepsilon}_b+i \eta_{\Sigma}} + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i j a} \frac{|\langle p a \| i j\rangle|^2}{\omega+\tilde{\varepsilon}_a-\tilde{\varepsilon}_i-\tilde{\varepsilon}_j+i \eta_{\Sigma}}
\end{equation}
where the single-particle energies in the denominator are due to the ground state BWs2 calculation. The spectral function is then given by
\begin{align}
    A_p(\omega)&=-\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im} G_p^R(\omega)\\
    &= -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im} \frac{1}{\omega+i \eta_A-\varepsilon_p^{\mathrm{HF}}-\tilde{\Sigma}_p^{(2), R}(\omega)} \\
&= \frac{1}{\pi} \frac{\eta_A+\Gamma_p(\omega)}{\left[\omega-\varepsilon_p^{\mathrm{HF}}-\operatorname{Re} \tilde{\Sigma}_p^{(2), R}(\omega)\right]^2+\left[\eta_A+\Gamma_p(\omega)\right]^2}
\end{align}
where $\Gamma_p(\omega)=|\operatorname{Im} \tilde{\Sigma}_p^{(2), R}(\omega)|$. We can take $\eta_A = \eta_{\Sigma}= 0.2 \text{eV}$ to facilitate comparison with my BWs2+C spectral function. Let us also put a vertical line for the frequency of the quasi-particle energy of 
\begin{equation}
    \varepsilon_p^{\text{BWs2}} = \varepsilon_p^{\mathrm{HF}}+ Z_p(\tilde{\varepsilon}_p)
\operatorname{Re} \tilde{\Sigma}_p^{(2), R}(\varepsilon_p^{\text{BWs2}})
\end{equation}
where $Z_p(\tilde{\varepsilon}_p) = \left(1-\frac{\partial \operatorname{Re} \tilde{\Sigma}_p^{(2), R}(\omega)}{\partial \omega}\big|_{\omega = \tilde{\varepsilon}_p}\right)^{-1}$ is the renormalization factor.
\subsubsection{Double check on my previous results}
An equivalent way to compute the BWs2 cumulant Greens function should be via 
\begin{equation}
\bar{G}_{pp}^{\mathrm{BWs2+C}}(t) = -i \theta(t) e^{-i{\varepsilon}^{HF}_p t} \exp\left\{\bar{C}_{pp}^{\mathrm{BWs2}}(t)\right\}.
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
    \bar{C}_{pp}^{\mathrm{BWs2}}(t) =     \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i a b}\frac{\langle p i \| a b\rangle^2 }{\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b}\right)^2}\left(e^{-i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b} t} +i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b} t -1 \right) 
    + \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i j a} \frac{\langle p a \| i j\rangle^2}{\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{p a}^{i j}\right)^2} \left(e^{-i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p a}^{i j} t} - 1 \right).
\end{equation}
We should verify numerically for some small system size (maybe 14e19o) that $G_{pp}^{\mathrm{BWs2+C}}(t) = \bar{G}_{pp}^{\mathrm{BWs2+C}}(t)$ To numerical precision, given that they should be the same.
\subsubsection{Frequency domain version}
Now, if we plug in our derived expression for $C^{\text{BWs2}}_{pp}(t)$ into the cumulant ansatz for the retarded Green's function:
\begin{align}
G_{pp}(t) & = G_{pp}^{0, BWs2}(t) e^{C_{pp}^{BWs2}(t)} \\
& = -i \Theta(t) e^{-i \tilde\epsilon_pt + C_{pp}^{BWs2}(t)} \\
& = -i \Theta(t) e^{-i \tilde\epsilon_pt} \exp\left\{ \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i a b}\frac{\langle p i \| a b\rangle^2 }{\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b}\right)^2}\left(e^{-i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b} t} +i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b} t -1 \right) + \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i j a} \frac{\langle p a \| i j\rangle^2}{\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{p a}^{i j}\right)^2} \left(e^{-i \tilde{\epsilon}_{p a}^{i j} t} - 1 \right) \right\} \\
&= -i \Theta(t) \underbrace{e^{-\sum_{i a b} \zeta_{pi}^{ab} - \sum_{i j a} \zeta_{pa}^{ij}}}_{Z_p^{QP}} e^{-i\overbrace{\left(\tilde\epsilon_p - \sum_{i a b} \zeta_{pi}^{ab} \tilde{\epsilon}_{pi}^{ab}\right)}^{\epsilon_p^{QP}} t} \exp\left\{ \sum_{i a b} \zeta_{pi}^{ab} e^{-i\tilde\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} t} + \sum_{i j a} \zeta_{pa}^{ij} e^{-i\tilde\epsilon_{pa}^{ij} t} \right\}
\end{align}
where $\zeta_{pi}^{ab} = \frac{1}{2} \frac{\langle p i \| a b\rangle^2}{\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{p i}^{a b}\right)^2}$ and $\zeta_{pa}^{ij} = \frac{1}{2} \frac{\langle p a \| i j\rangle^2}{\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_{p a}^{i j}\right)^2}$ are the bosonic coupling coefficients.
 Next, we want to perform a Fourier transform.
\begin{align}
G_{pp}(\omega) & = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} dt e^{i \omega t} G_{pp}(t)
\label{Gpp1}
\\
& = -i Z_p^{QP} \int_0^{\infty} dt e^{i(\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP}) t} \exp\left\{ \sum_{i a b} \zeta_{pi}^{ab} e^{-i\tilde\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} t} + \sum_{i j a} \zeta_{pa}^{ij} e^{-i\tilde\epsilon_{pa}^{ij} t} \right\}
\label{Gpp2}
\\
& = -i Z_p^{QP} \int_0^{\infty} dt\, e^{i(\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP}) t} \left(1 + \sum_{i a b} \zeta_{pi}^{ab} e^{-i\tilde\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} t} + \sum_{i j a} \zeta_{pa}^{ij} e^{-i\tilde\epsilon_{pa}^{ij} t} + \ldots \right) \\
&= -i Z_p^{QP} \int_0^{\infty} dt\, e^{[-\eta + i(\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP}) t]}\label{Gpp3} \\
&\quad - i Z_p^{QP} \sum_{iab} \zeta_{pi}^{ab} \int_0^{\infty} dt\, e^{[-\eta + i(\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP} - \tilde\epsilon_{pi}^{ab}) t]}\\
&\quad - i Z_p^{QP} \sum_{a\nu} \zeta_{pa}^{ij} \int_0^{\infty} dt\, e^{[-\eta + i(\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP} - \tilde\epsilon_{pa}^{ij}) t]} + \ldots\\
&= \frac{Z_p^{QP}}{\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP} + i\eta} + \sum_{i a b} \frac{Z_{piab}^{sat}}{\omega - \epsilon_{piab}^{sat} + i\eta} + \sum_{i j a} \frac{Z_{pija}^{sat}}{\omega - \epsilon_{pija}^{sat} + i\eta} + \ldots
\end{align}
where we defined the satellite energies $\epsilon_{piab}^{sat} = \epsilon_p^{QP} + \tilde\epsilon_{pi}^{ab}$ and $\epsilon_{pija}^{sat} = \epsilon_p^{QP} + \tilde\epsilon_{pa}^{ij}$, and the corresponding spectral weights $Z_{piab}^{sat} = Z_p^{QP} \zeta_{pi}^{ab}$ and $Z_{pija}^{sat} = Z_p^{QP} \zeta_{pa}^{ij}$.
The diagonal elements of the spectral function are obtained as (the virtual satellites spectral function, whose derivation will mirror that of the occupied satellites, are omitted for the sake of brevity):
\begin{align}
A_{pp}^{MP2+C}(\omega) & = -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im} G_{pp}(\omega) \\
& = -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im}\left[\frac{Z_p^{QP}}{\omega - \epsilon_p^{QP} + i\eta} + \sum_{i a b} \frac{Z_{piab}^{sat}}{\omega - \tilde\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} + i\eta} + \sum_{i j a} \frac{Z_{pija}^{sat}}{\omega - \tilde\epsilon_{pa}^{ij} + i\eta} + \ldots \right] \\
&= -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im}\left[\frac{\operatorname{Re} Z_p^{QP} + i \operatorname{Im} Z_p^{QP}}{\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP} + i\left(\eta - \operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)} + \sum_{i a b} \frac{\operatorname{Re} Z_{piab}^{sat} + i \operatorname{Im} Z_{piab}^{sat}}{\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{piab}^{sat} + i\left(\eta - \operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{piab}^{sat}\right)} + \ldots \right] \\
&= -\frac{1}{\pi} \operatorname{Im}\left[\frac{\left(\operatorname{Re} Z_p^{QP} + i \operatorname{Im} Z_p^{QP}\right)\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP} - i\left(\eta - \operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)\right)}{\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)^2 + \left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)^2}\right.\\
& \left. + \sum_{i a b} \frac{\left(\operatorname{Re} Z_{piab}^{sat} + i \operatorname{Im} Z_{piab}^{sat}\right)\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{piab}^{sat} - i\left(\eta - \operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{piab}^{sat}\right)\right)}{\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{piab}^{sat}\right)^2 + \left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{piab}^{sat}\right)^2} + \ldots \right] \\
&= -\frac{1}{\pi}\left[\frac{\left(\operatorname{Re} Z_p^{QP}\right)\left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right) + \left(\operatorname{Im} Z_p^{QP}\right)\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)}{\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)^2 + \left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_p^{QP}\right)^2}\right.\\
& \left. + \sum_{i a b} \frac{\left(\operatorname{Re} Z_{piab}^{sat}\right)\left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{piab}^{sat}\right) + \left(\operatorname{Im} Z_{piab}^{sat}\right)\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{piab}^{sat}\right)}{\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{piab}^{sat}\right)^2 + \left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{piab}^{sat}\right)^2} + \ldots \right]
% & \left. + \sum_{i\nu} \frac{\left(\operatorname{Re} Z_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)\left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right) + \left(\operatorname{Im} Z_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)}{\left(\omega - \operatorname{Re} \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)^2 + \left(\operatorname{Im} \epsilon_{pi\nu}^{sat}\right)^2} + \ldots \right]
\end{align}
Everything I present in the plots is one-shot. When I speak of GF2+C with analytic frequency integration, I am building the cumulant as
\begin{align}
    C_{pp}(t) &=\sum_{iab} \frac{(pa|ib)\left(2(pa|ib)- (pb|ia)\right)}{\left(\epsilon_{pi}^{ab}\right)^2}
\left(e^{-i \epsilon_{pi}^{ab} t} +i \epsilon_{pi}^{ab} t -1 \right)
\\&+\sum_{ija} \frac{(pi|aj)\left(2(pi|aj)- (pj|ai)\right)}{\left(\epsilon_{pa}^{ij}\right)^2} \left(e^{-i \epsilon_{pa}^{ij} t} +i \epsilon_{pa}^{ij} t -1 \right) \nonumber
\end{align}
with $\epsilon_{pi}^{ab} = \epsilon_{a}+\epsilon_{b}-\epsilon_{p}-\epsilon_{i}$ and
$\epsilon_{pa}^{ij} = \epsilon_{i}+\epsilon_{j}-\epsilon_{p}-\epsilon_{a}$.
 As can be seen, this gives qualitatively the same result as GF2+C with numerical frequency integration, where I instead build the cumulant as
\begin{equation}
    C_{pp}(t) = \int d \omega \frac{\beta_{pp}(\omega)}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i \omega t} + i \omega t -1 \right)
\label{eq:cumulant_beta}
\end{equation}
with $\beta_{pp}(\omega) = \frac{1}{\pi} |\operatorname{Im} \Sigma_{pp}(\omega + \epsilon_p)|$ with the self-energy defined in chemists notation as
\begin{align}
    \Sigma^{(2)}_{pq}(\omega)
    & = \sum_{iab} \frac{(pa|ib)\Big(2(qa|ib) - (qb|ia)\Big)}{\omega - \epsilon_a - \epsilon_b + \epsilon_i + i \eta} + \sum_{ija} \frac{\left( (pi|aj)(2(qi|aj) - (qj|ai)) \right)}{\omega - \epsilon_i - \epsilon_j + \epsilon_a + i \eta} 
\end{align}
or in physicists notation as
\begin{align}
    \Sigma^{(2)}_{pq}(\omega)
    & = \sum_{iab} \frac{\left<pi|ab\right>\left(2\left<qi|ab\right> - \left<qi|ba\right>\right)}{\omega - \epsilon_a - \epsilon_b + \epsilon_i + i \eta} + \sum_{ija} \frac{\left<pa|ij\right>\left(2\left<qa|ij\right> - \left<qa|ji\right>\right)}{\omega - \epsilon_i - \epsilon_j + \epsilon_a + i \eta}
\end{align}
If I remember correctly, our idea for a self consistent scheme was to solve equation \ref{eq:cumulant_beta} using
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma_{pp}(\omega) = \frac{1}{G^0_{pp}(\omega)} - \frac{1}{G_{pp}(\omega)}
% \left(G^0_{pp}(\omega)\right)^{-1} - \left(G_{pp}(\omega)\right)^{-1}
\end{equation}
In the initial trials, I have not been getting any useful results with the above. However, I notice that I only use under 1e4 frequency grid points. Should I try to get this scheme to work with something like 1e6 grid points or were you thinking of something different?
\subsection{BW-PEOM(2)+C}
The second-order self-energy can be written as
\begin{align}
\Sigma_{pq}^{(2)}(\omega)
=&
\frac{1}{2}
\sum_{iab}
\frac{
\langle pi || ab \rangle
\langle ab || qi \rangle
}{
\omega + \epsilon_i - \epsilon_a - \epsilon_b + i\eta
}
\nonumber\\
&+
\frac{1}{2}
\sum_{ija}
\frac{
\langle pa || ij \rangle
\langle ij || qa \rangle
}{
\omega + \epsilon_a - \epsilon_i - \epsilon_j + i\eta
}.
\end{align}
The P-EOM-MBPT(2) self-energy may instead be written as
\begin{align}
\Sigma_{ij}^{\text{PEOM(2)}}(\omega)
=
\underbrace{
\frac{1}{2}
\sum_{l,c,d}
\frac{
\langle il || cd \rangle
\langle cd || jl \rangle
}{
\epsilon_j+\epsilon_l-\epsilon_c-\epsilon_d
}
}_{2p1h}
-
\underbrace{
\frac{1}{2}
\sum_{k,l,d}
\frac{
\tilde{V}_{id[kl]}
\langle kl || jd \rangle
}{
\epsilon_k+\epsilon_l-\epsilon_d-\omega
}
}_{2h1p}.
\end{align}
Here,
\begin{align}
\tilde{V}_{kb[ij]}
=&\;
\langle kb || ij \rangle
+
\sum_{l,d}
\langle kl || jd \rangle
t_{il}^{bd}
-
\sum_{l,d}
\langle lk || jd \rangle
t_{il}^{bd}
\nonumber\\
&+
\sum_{c,d}
\langle bk || dc \rangle
t_{ji}^{dc}
-
\sum_{l,c}
\langle lk || jc \rangle
t_{li}^{bc},
\end{align}
with
\begin{equation}
t_{ij}^{ab}
=
\frac{
\langle ab || ij \rangle
}{
\epsilon_i+\epsilon_j-\epsilon_a-\epsilon_b
}.
\end{equation}
Defining
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{S}
=
\bm{\Sigma}^{\text{PEOM(2)},2p1h},
\end{equation}
the Dyson equation may be partitioned as
\begin{align}
G^{-1}(\omega)
&\approx
\omega
-
\left(
\mathbf{F}
+
\alpha \mathbf{S}
\right)
-
\left[
\bm{\Sigma}^{\text{PEOM(2)}}[G^{(0)'}](\omega)
-
\alpha \mathbf{S}
\right]
\\
&=
\left(
G^{(0)'}(\omega)
\right)^{-1}
-
\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\omega),
\end{align}
where
\begin{equation}
G^{(0)'}(\omega)
=
\left[
\omega
-
\left(
\mathbf{F}
+
\alpha \mathbf{S}
\right)
\right]^{-1}
\end{equation}
is the BWS2 reference Green's function. Expanding the Dyson equation to first order in the residual
self-energy gives
\begin{align}
G(t)
\approx
G^{(0)'}(t)
+
\int dt' dt''\,
G^{(0)'}(t-t')
\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{eff}}(t'-t'')
G^{(0)'}(t'').
\label{eq:Dyson_BWs2_corrected}
\end{align}
The BW-PEOM(2) cumulant ansatz for state $p$, within the diagonal
approximation, is
\begin{align}
G_{pp}^{\mathrm{BW-PEOM(2)}+C}(t)
&=
G_{pp}^{(0)'}(t)
\exp\left[
C_{pp}^{\mathrm{BW-PEOM(2)}}(t)
\right]
\\
&\approx
G_{pp}^{(0)'}(t)
+
G_{pp}^{(0)'}(t)
C_{pp}^{\mathrm{BW-PEOM(2)}}(t).
\label{eq:BWs2_cumulant_ansatz_corrected}
\end{align}
Equating
Eqs.~\eqref{eq:Dyson_BWs2_corrected} and
\eqref{eq:BWs2_cumulant_ansatz_corrected}
gives
\begin{align}
G_{pp}^{(0)'}(t)
C_{pp}^{\mathrm{BW-PEOM(2)}}(t)
=
\int dt' dt''\,
G_{pp}^{(0)'}(t-t')
\Sigma_{\mathrm{eff},pp}(t'-t'')
G_{pp}^{(0)'}(t''),
\end{align}
which implies
\begin{align}
C_{pp}^{\mathrm{BW-PEOM(2)}}(t)
&=
i
\int
\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}
e^{-i(\omega-\tilde{\varepsilon}_p)t}
\frac{
\Sigma_{\mathrm{eff},pp}(\omega)
}{
(\omega-\tilde{\varepsilon}_p)^2
}
\\
&=
i
\int
\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}
e^{-i\omega t}
\frac{
\Sigma_{\mathrm{eff},pp}
(\omega+\tilde{\varepsilon}_p)
}{
\omega^2
}.
\label{eq:peom_cumulant_general}
\end{align}
Since
\begin{equation}
\Sigma_{pp}^{\mathrm{PEOM(2)}}(\omega)
=
S_p
+
\Sigma_{pp}^{\mathrm{PEOM(2)},2h1p}(\omega),
\end{equation}
the effective self-energy becomes
\begin{align}
\Sigma_{\mathrm{eff},pp}
(\omega+\tilde{\varepsilon}_p)
&=
(1-\alpha)S_p
+
\Sigma_{pp}^{\mathrm{PEOM(2)},2h1p}
(\omega+\tilde{\varepsilon}_p)\\
&\overset{\alpha=1}{=}
\Sigma_{pp}^{\mathrm{PEOM(2)},2h1p}
(\omega+\tilde{\varepsilon}_p)
.
\end{align}
Introducing
\begin{equation}
A_{p;kld}
\equiv
\tilde{V}_{pd[kl]}
\langle kl \Vert pd \rangle
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\tilde{\Omega}_{p;kld}
\equiv
\tilde{\varepsilon}_k
+
\tilde{\varepsilon}_l
-
\tilde{\varepsilon}_p
-
\tilde{\varepsilon}_d
-
i\eta,
\end{equation}
the $2h1p$ self-energy may be written as
\begin{align}
\Sigma_{pp}^{\mathrm{PEOM(2)},2h1p}
(\omega+\tilde{\varepsilon}_p)
=
-\frac{1}{2}
\sum_{kld}
\frac{
A_{p;kld}
}{
\tilde{\Omega}_{p;kld}-\omega
}.
\label{eq:peom_2h1p_self_energy}
\end{align}
Substituting into
Eq.~\eqref{eq:peom_cumulant_general} gives
\begin{align}
C_{pp}^{\mathrm{BW-PEOM(2)}}(t)
&\overset{\alpha=1}{=}
\frac{1}{2}
\sum_{kld}
\frac{
\tilde{V}_{pd[kl]}
\langle kl\Vert pd\rangle
}{
\tilde{\Omega}_{p;kld}^{\,2}
}
\left[
e^{-i\tilde{\Omega}_{p;kld}t}
+
i\tilde{\Omega}_{p;kld}t
-
1
\right]
.
\label{eq:bw_peom_cumulant_alpha1}
\end{align}
\section{Self-consistent cumulant}
The scheme involves multiple junctures at which we are free to make different choices. A few parameters should always stay the same. Specifically, we need to use a consistent set of $\left\{\omega_i, t_j, \Delta t, \eta, k\right\}$, where $\omega_i$ and $t_j$ are the frequency and time grids related by the discrete Fourier transform. $\eta$ is the broadening parameter. We should experiment with a range of different values for this. What should we try, and what kind of dependence should this have on my time grid? , and $k$ is the momentum index. We are most in the deep core state. The other parameters can be varied to explore different self-consistent cumulant schemes.
\subsection{Solving for the retarded Green's function initially}
We solve for the Green's function via
\begin{align} G^{R}_{k}(t) &= G_{0,k}^R(t) e^{C_{k}^{R}(t)}
\label{eq:cumulant_G}\end{align}
where the non-interacting cumulant takes the form 
\begin{equation}
   G_{0,k}^R(t) = -i \Theta(t) e^{-i\epsilon_k t}.
\end{equation}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item If we use a HF reference, $\epsilon_k=\epsilon_k^{\text{HF}}$.
    \item If we use an LDA reference, $\epsilon_k = \epsilon_k^{\text{LDA}} + \left(\Sigma^x - V_{xc}\right)_{kk}$, where $\Sigma^x$ is the Fock exchange operator and $V_{xc}$ is the LDA exchange-correlation potential.
\end{enumerate}
The Landau formula for the cumulant is given by
\begin{equation}
C_{k}^R(t) = \int d\omega \frac{\beta_{k}(\omega)}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right)
\label{eq:cumulant_beta}
\end{equation}
where 
\begin{equation}
\beta_{k}(\omega) = \frac{1}{\pi} |\text{Im} \Sigma^R_{k}(\omega + \tilde{\epsilon}_k)|    
\end{equation}
is proportional to the retarded self-energy. In the past, we have always used the proper form, But given that document showed the different exact relationship between the improper self-energy and the retarded cumulant, we also try using that. In the initial iteration, the improper prescription is 

\begin{enumerate} 
    \item If we are using a HF or LDA reference , $\tilde{\epsilon}_k = \epsilon_k^{\text{HF}}$ or $\tilde{\epsilon}_k = \epsilon_k^{\text{LDA}}$, respectively.
    % \item At the initial iteration, try $\Sigma^R=\Sigma^{R,(2)}$, $\Sigma^R = \Sigma^{R,G_0W_0}$, $\Sigma^R = \Sigma^{R,G_0W}$, $\Sigma^R = \Sigma^{R,GW_0}$ and $\Sigma^R = \Sigma^{R,GW}$ proper self energies. In order to form the improper self energies, consider   To solve for the initial self-energy, because when you form the Green's fn via a DFT, the time grid already dictates what the frequency grid on which we're doing this self-consistent procedure should be. 
\end{enumerate}
We have already investigated the one-shot form where we utilize the proper self-energy. Relative to this, the improper self-energy is defined as 

Then, do the one-sided causal transform:
\begin{equation}
    G^R_{k}(\omega) = \int_0^{\infty} dt e^{i\omega t} G^R_{k}(t).
\end{equation}
We can do this via the discrete Fourier transform. Here we should use the trick that forms the periodic signal.

\subsection{Updating the self-energy }
We can try one of two schemes to update the self-energy after the initial iteration $n=0$.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $\Sigma_{k, n>0}^{P,R}(\omega) = \frac{1}{G^{R}_{0,k}(\omega)} - \frac{1}{G^R_{k,n-1}(\omega)}$ is the appropriate choice for a proper self-energy.
    \item $\Sigma_{k, n>0}^{I,R}(\omega) = \frac{G_{k,n-1}^R(\omega)-G_{ 0,k}^{R}(\omega)}{\left[G_{0,k}^{R}(\omega)\right]^2}$ is the appropriate choice for an improper self-energy.
\end{enumerate}
In both schemes, we deal with the complex scalar at the frequency point $\omega$. $G_{0,k}^R(\omega) = \frac{1}{\omega - \epsilon_k + i\eta}$ is the noninteracting Green's function, so $\left[G_{0,k}^R(\omega)\right]^{-1} = \omega - \epsilon_k + i\eta$.
\subsubsection{Seeing if we can get an analytic expression for Hartree-Fock reference}
After G0W0+C, the resulting Green's function can be shown to be 
\begin{align}
    G_k^R(\omega )&= -i Z_k \int_0^\infty dt\, e^{i(\omega - \epsilon_k^{QP}) t} \exp\left\{\sum_{i\nu} \zeta_{ki\nu} e^{-i\Delta_{ki\nu} t} + \sum_{a\nu} \zeta_{ka\nu} e^{-i\Delta_{ka\nu} t}\right\} \\
&\approx  \frac{Z_k}{\omega - \epsilon_k^{QP} + i\eta} + \sum_{i\nu} \frac{\overbrace{Z_k \zeta_{ki\nu}}^{Z^{\text{sat}_{ki\nu}}}}{\omega - \underbrace{\left(\epsilon_k^{QP} + \Delta_{ki\nu}\right)}_{\epsilon^{\text{sat}}_{ki\nu}} + i\eta} + \sum_{a\nu} \frac{\overbrace{Z_k \zeta_{ka\nu}}^{Z^{\text{sat}}_{ka\nu}}}{\omega - \underbrace{\left(\epsilon_k^{QP} + \Delta_{ka\nu}\right)}_{\epsilon^{\text{sat}}_{ka\nu}} + i\eta}.
\end{align}
where 
% &= -i\theta(t)\, \underbrace{\exp\left\{-\sum_{i\nu} \zeta_{ki\nu} - \sum_{a\nu} \zeta_{ka\nu} \right\}}_{Z_k} \exp\left\{-i \underbrace{\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_k - \sum_{i\nu} \zeta_{ki\nu} \Delta_{ki\nu} - \sum_{a\nu} \zeta_{ka\nu} \Delta_{ka\nu}\right)}_{\epsilon_k^{QP}} t\right\}\\ 
\begin{align}
    Z_k &= \exp\left\{-\sum_{i\nu} \zeta_{ki\nu} - \sum_{a\nu} \zeta_{ka\nu} \right\} \\
    \epsilon_k^{QP} &= {\epsilon}_k^{\text{HF}} - \sum_{i\nu} \zeta_{ki\nu} \Delta_{ki\nu} - \sum_{a\nu} \zeta_{ka\nu} \Delta_{ka\nu}.
\end{align}
with the definitions $\zeta_{ki\nu} = \left(\frac{M_{ki\nu}}{\Delta_{ki\nu}}\right)^2$ and $\zeta_{ka\nu} = \left(\frac{M_{ka\nu}}{\Delta_{ka\nu}}\right)^2$, $M$ are transition densities, and $\Delta_{ki\nu} = \epsilon_i - \epsilon_k^{HF} - \Omega_\nu - i \eta$ and $\Delta_{ka\nu} = \epsilon_a - \epsilon_k^{HF} + \Omega_\nu - i \eta$.
% &= i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} \frac{1}{(\omega + i \eta)^2} e^{-i \omega t} \left[ \sum_{i\nu} \frac{M_{pi\nu}^2}{\omega + \underbrace{\epsilon_p^{HF} - \epsilon_i + \Omega_\nu + i \eta}_{-\Delta_{pi\nu}}} + \sum_{a\nu} \frac{M_{pa\nu}^2}{\omega + \underbrace{\epsilon_p^{HF} - \epsilon_a - \Omega_\nu + i \eta}_{-\Delta_{pa\nu}}} \right] \\
The approximation that we have made to get the final expression, which comes from performing a Taylor expansion of the outer exponential of the first expression, has been shown to generate the main quasiparticle peak along with the first two satellites.
After some algebra, we can derive 
\begin{align}
    \Sigma_k^{I, R}(\omega) \approx & \frac{Z_k\left(x_k-\epsilon_k\right)^2}{x_k-\epsilon_k^{Q P}} +\sum_{i \nu} \frac{Z_{k i \nu}^{\text {sat }}\left(x_k-\epsilon_k\right)^2}{x_k-\epsilon_{k i \nu}^{\text {sat }}}+\sum_{a \nu} \frac{Z_{k a \nu}^{\text {sat }}\left(x_k-\epsilon_k\right)^2}{x_k-\epsilon_{k a \nu}^{\text {sat }}} -\left(x_k-\epsilon_k\right)
\end{align}
and

\end{align}
where $x_k = \omega + i\eta$.

\subsection{Self-consistency condition}
We can monitor the Green's function norm at a given iteration $n>0$ as
% $$
% \Delta_G^{(n)}=\frac{\left[\sum_i w_i\left|G_p^{(n)}\left(\omega_i\right)-G_p^{(n-1)}\left(\omega_i\right)\right|^2\right]^{1 / 2}}{\left[\sum_i w_i\left|G_p^{(n-1)}\left(\omega_i\right)\right|^2\right]^{1 / 2}}
% $$
\begin{equation}
    \Delta_G^{(n)} = \frac{\sqrt{\sum_i w_i |G_{k}^{(n)}(\omega_i) - G_{k}^{(n-1)}(\omega_i)|^2}}{\sqrt{\sum_i w_i |G_{k}^{(n-1)}(\omega_i)|^2}}
\end{equation}
where $w_i$ are the quadrature weights for the frequency grid chosen above, stopping once $\Delta_G^{(n)} < \epsilon$ for some small threshold $\epsilon$.

    \item Experiment with how different parameters, including the broadening $\eta$, effect what choice of $\epsilon$ is realistic.

\subsection{Problems with this formulation}
To arrive at the Landau form in the first place, we expanded the cumulant ansatz alongside the Dyson equation as 
\begin{equation}
    G_0 e^{C(t)} \equiv G_0 [ 1 + C(t) + \frac{C(t)^2}{2} + \ldots ] = G_0 + G_0 \Sigma_{GW} G_0 + G_0 \Sigma_{GW} G_0 \Sigma_{GW} G_0 + \ldots \equiv G_0 + G_0 \Sigma_{GW} G.
\end{equation}
Then, to the first order in the screened interaction, with which we will associate the parameter $\lambda$, we have 
\begin{align}
    G_0 C(t) &= G_0 \Sigma_{GW} G_0 \label{eq:gw_match} \\
\implies  C_{k}^R(t) &= \frac{1}{\pi}\int d\omega \frac{|\Sigma_{GW}^R(\omega+\epsilon_k)|}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right) \label{eq:ce_land}
\end{align}
Exponentiating introduces additional powers of $\lambda$:
\begin{align}
  G^{R}_{k}(t) &= G_{0,k}^R(t) e^{C_{k}^{R}(t)} \propto \lambda + \lambda^2 + \lambda^3 + \ldots\\
\implies  \Sigma^R_k(\omega ) &= \left[G_0^R(\omega )\right]^{-1} - \left[G^R(\omega )\right]^{-1} \propto \lambda + \lambda^{2} + \lambda^{3} + \ldots
\label{eq:sig_lam}
\end{align}
where \eqref{eq:sig_lam} is the proposed self-energy update. But then, we shouldn't plug back into \eqref{eq:ce_land}, because deriving this expression relied on $\Sigma$ being correct up to first order in $\lambda$, which was true in the initial G0W0 case but is no longer true during our update. If instead we follow Holms' formulation, where they just update G self-consistently, this would also be some sort of approximate formulation since technically we would be including powers of $\lambda$ into G, but at least now it would always be the case that W stays controlled at the RPA level. To understand if this scheme is actually effective would require numerical experimentation. 

\subsection{}
In this scheme, we envisioned we would plug the $\Sigma$ from equation \eqref{eq:sig_lam} into equation \eqref{eq:ce_land} to get a new cumulant. But this would violate the order matching of equation \eqref{eq:gw_match} that gave us the Landau form in the first place.
Recall that in order to derive the Landau form for the cumulant of
\begin{equation}
    C_{k}^R(t) = \frac{1}{\pi}\int d\omega \frac{|\Sigma_{GW}^R(\omega+\epsilon_k)|}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right),
% \label{eq:ce_land}
\end{equation}
We can start by  performing the expansion in powers of the screened Coulomb interaction, associating with it the parameter $\lambda$. It is clear that at the one-shot level 
\begin{equation}
    C_{k}^R(t) = \frac{1}{\pi}\int d\omega \frac{|\Sigma_{GW}^R(\omega+\epsilon_k)|}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right) \propto GW \propto \lambda
% \label{eq:ce_land}
\end{equation}
So then
\begin{equation}
     G^{R}_{CE,k}(t) = G_{0,k}^R(t) e^{C_{k}^{R}(t)} \propto \lambda + \lambda^2 + \lambda^3 + \ldots.
\end{equation}
Then, the self-energy update is 
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma^R_k(\omega ) = \left[G_0^R(\omega )\right]^{-1} - \left[G^R(\omega )\right]^{-1} \propto \lambda^{-1} + \lambda^{-2} + \lambda^{-3} + \ldots.
% \label{eq:sig_lam}
\end{equation}
To arrive at the Landau form in the first place, we expanded the cumulant ansatz alongside the Dyson equation as 
\begin{equation}
    G_0 e^{C(t)} \equiv G_0 [ 1 + C(t) + \frac{C(t)^2}{2} + \ldots ] = G_0 + G_0 \Sigma G_0 + G_0 \Sigma G_0 \Sigma G_0 + \ldots \equiv G_0 + G_0 \Sigma G.
\end{equation}
Then, to the first order in $\lambda$, it is true that 
\begin{align}
    G_0 C(t) &= G_0 \Sigma G_0\\% \label{eq:gw_match} \\
\implies C_{k}^R(t) &= \frac{1}{\pi}\int d\omega \frac{|\Sigma_{GW}^R(\omega+\epsilon_k)|}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right)
\end{align}
In this scheme, we envisioned we would plug the $\Sigma$ from equation \eqref{eq:sig_lam} into equation \eqref{eq:ce_land} to get a new cumulant. But this would violate the order matching of equation \eqref{eq:gw_match} that gave us the Landau form in the first place.
Recall that in order to derive the Landau form for the cumulant of
\begin{equation}
    C_{k}^R(t) = \frac{1}{\pi}\int d\omega \frac{|\Sigma_{GW}^R(\omega+\epsilon_k)|}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right),
% \label{eq:ce_land}
\end{equation}

which we have used successfully for one-shot calculations, we equated the cumulant ansatz to the Dyson equation in a way that was correct up to first-order (second-order) in the screened (bare) Coulomb interaction, using
\begin{equation}
  G_0C=G_0\Sigma G_0 ,
\end{equation}
 for a $\Sigma$ due to $GW$ (BWs2). But when we go on to form
\begin{equation}
     G^{R}_{CE,k}(t) = G_{0,k}^R(t) e^{C_{k}^{R}(t)},
\end{equation}
it is known that the resulting interacting Green's function can no longer be understood as just being just correct up to a finite order of the interaction strength (this the same reason why we say that $G_{CE}$ contains an approximate infinity of diagrams). In the current self-consistent scheme, when we want to update $\Sigma$, we use the \emph{exact} relation 
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma^R_k(\omega ) = \left[G_0^R(\omega )\right]^{-1} - \left[G^R(\omega )\right]^{-1},
\end{equation}
where now it is unclear to which order of interaction strength $G^R(\omega )$ is correct.
Therefore it no longer makes sense to utilize equation \eqref{eq:ce_land} again, since the derivation of this relation relied on the assumption that we were going to stay at a certain order in the interaction strength.

%     \item Getting the cumulant from the Landau form in order to get the interacting Green's function.
%     \item Integrating over the spectral function to form a new sum over states expression for the GW or BWs2 self energg, keeping the interaction constant.
%     \item Iterating until convergence.
