\subsection{Results from our scheme}
As before, we can do either 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $\beta_k(\omega)= \left|\operatorname{Im} \Sigma^{c,R}(\mathbf{k}, \omega+E^{\text{GW}}_k)\right|$ with $E^{\text{GW}}_k$ the GW quasiparticle energy, which I call epep@qp.
    \item $\beta_k(\omega)= \left|\operatorname{Im} \Sigma^{c,R}(\mathbf{k}, \omega+\varepsilon_{\mathbf{k}}^{\text{MF}})\right|$ with $\varepsilon_{\mathbf{k}}^{\text{MF}}$ the non-interacting single-particle energy, which I call epep@mf.
\end{enumerate}
I just present results for epep@mf, as that is what Loos suggested, seems more theoretically sound, and falls closer to the CCSD curve for 114e485o. I show the results for the initial iteration (labeled as LDA+G0W0+C) and then, for the two following iterations after that, which gives a good idea as to what the solution is converging to.
\subsubsection{Proper self-energy}
Here we start with the standard one-shot retarded cumulant (obtained from the retarded proper G0W0 self-energy $\Sigma^R_{GW}$) I have presented before, and then we update the self energy via 
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma^R_k(\omega ) = \left[G_0^R(\omega )\right]^{-1} - \left[G^R(\omega )\right]^{-1}.
\end{equation}
I have plotted both the spectral function and the self-energy obtained from subsequent iterations. \emph{As we can see, after the one-shot cumulant, the spectral function just becomes featureless because the self-energy loses its features in this update scheme. }
\subsubsection{Improper self-energy}
Here we start with the retarded G0W0 improper self-energy $\Sigma^R_{\mathrm{imp}}$, which is related to the proper one by 
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma^R_{\mathrm{imp}}=\Sigma^R_{G W}\left(1-G_0^R \Sigma^R_{G W}\right)^{-1}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, our self-energy update scheme instead uses
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma^R_k(\omega ) = \frac{G^R(\omega)-G_0^R(\omega)}{\left[G_0^R(\omega)\right]^2}.
\end{equation}
I have plotted both the spectral function and the self-energy obtained from subsequent iterations. Clearly, the scheme isn't working for the GW case. \emph{Recall that Reichman used this improper retarded, cumulant scheme to get one-shot spectral functions in the TDL, but the results weren't that good there either. }
\subsection{Results from the Holm-inspired SC-CE(GW0)}
As before, we start with the standard one-shot cumulant (obtained from the proper G0W0 self-energy $\Sigma^R_{GW}$). But then to do our self-energy update we build
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma_i^R(t)=i \sum_j\left[G_j^R(t) W_{i j}^{0,>}(t)+G_j^{<}(t) W_{i j}^{0, R}(t)\right].
\end{equation}
$G_j^R(t)$ and $G_j^{<}(t)$ are the retarded and lesser cumulant Green's functions for the state $j$. $W_{ij}^{0,R}(t)$ and $W_{ij}^{0,>}(t)$ are the retarded and greater matrix elements of the bare RPA screened interaction in the time domain. I obtain $W(t)$ by first forming the frequency domain sum-over-states expression for the screened interaction via a Casida diagonalization and then doing a DFT to get it into the time domain. Once we have built $\Sigma_i^R(t)$, we can then Fourier transform to get $\Sigma_i^R(\omega)$, then form a new cumulant via the Landau form, and iterate until convergence.
\subsection{Choosing the correct pole for our self-consistent cumulant}
In a self-consistent cumulant, we update the pole along with the cumulant. But this is nuanced and to see why, we juxtapose how we do the one-shot cumulant starting from Hartree-Fock versus LDA references. We always have $G^{R}_{CE,k}(t) = G_{0,k}^R(t) e^{C_{k}^{R}(t)} = -i \Theta(t) e^{-i\tilde{\epsilon}_k t} e^{C_{k}^{R}(t)}$, where $C_{k}^R(t) = \frac{1}{\pi}\int d\omega \frac{|\Sigma_{GW}^{c,R}(\omega+\epsilon_k)|}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right)$. 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item HF chooses $\tilde{\epsilon}_k = \epsilon_k = \frac{k^2}{2} + \Sigma_{kk}^x \equiv \epsilon^{HF}_k$
    \item LDA chooses $\tilde{\epsilon}_k = \epsilon^{LDA}_k + \Sigma_{kk}^x - v^{xc}_{kk} = \frac{k^2}{2} + \Sigma_{kk}^x$ and $\epsilon_k = \epsilon^{LDA}_k$
\end{enumerate}
Therefore, it becomes clear that one always builds the Green function from three main ingredients. The first two are static, constituting the completely non-interacting piece and the bare exchange self-energy. The third is the dynamic correlation piece, which is taken care of by the cumulant. A self-consistent scheme must respect the same split. Therefore, if one started from a quasiparticle $G_0$, the correlation component would have to be contained in the cumulant, so we would do $\tilde{\epsilon}_k = \epsilon^{QP}_k - \Sigma_{kk}^c(\epsilon^{QP}_k)= \frac{k^2}{2} + \Sigma_{kk}^x$ and $\epsilon_k = \epsilon^{QP}_k$. So SC-CE(GW0) should use $\tilde{\epsilon}_k = \frac{k^2}{2} + \Sigma_{kk}^x$ and $\epsilon_k = \epsilon^{QP}_k$.
\subsection{A New Scheme for Self-Consistent Cumulant}
We can start with a one-shot cumulant computation as 
\begin{equation}
    C_{k}^R(t) = \frac{1}{\pi}\int d\omega \frac{|\Sigma_{GW}^R(\omega+\epsilon_k)|}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right),
\label{eq:ce_landau}
\end{equation}
leading to a time-domain Green's function
\begin{equation}
     G^{R}_{CE,k}(t) = G_{0,k}^R(t) e^{C_{k}^{R}(t)}.
\end{equation}
Keeping the screened interaction constant at the RPA level, we can then iterate and build a new self-energy using 
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma_i^R(t)=i \sum_j\left[G_j^R(t) W_{i j}^{0,>}(t)+G_j^{<}(t) W_{i j}^{0, R}(t)\right] .
\end{equation}
In order to do this, note that we have to get the cumulant Green's function for each state $j$. Now, since we are working with the electron gas, we know that there are abundant degeneracies for states with the same K-vector, so we can exploit that to do fewer computations of G. Also, we already have the spectral representation of the screened interaction $W_{ij}^{0,R}(\omega)$ from the initial Casida diagonalization, so we can just Fourier transform that initially to get to what we need, which is the screened interaction in the time domain. With these quantities, we can form a new GW self-energy, plug it into equation \eqref{eq:ce_landau} to get a new cumulant, and iterate until convergence.
\subsubsection{A scheme for doing self-consistency in G and W}
Hedin's equations tell us that we can form the polarizability as 
\begin{equation}
    P_{ij}(t) = -i \sum_k G_{ik}(t) G_{kj}(-t).
\end{equation}
Then, we perform a Fourier transform to get this into frequency space yielding $P_{ij}(\omega)$. Then, we can form the screened interaction as 
\begin{equation}
    W_{ij}(\omega) = v_{ij} + \sum_{kl} v_{ik} P_{kl}(\omega) W_{lj}(\omega),
\end{equation}
where $v_{ij}$ is the bare Coulomb interaction. Then, we can Fourier transform this back into the time domain to get $W_{ij}(t)$, which we can then use to form a new self-energy as 
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma_i^R(t)=i \sum_j\left[G_j^R(t) W_{i j}^{>}(t)+G_j^{<}(t) W_{i j}^{R}(t)\right] .
\end{equation}
\subsubsection{Self-consistency condition}
We can monitor the Green's function norm at a given iteration $n>0$ as
% $$
% \Delta_G^{(n)}=\frac{\left[\sum_i w_i\left|G_p^{(n)}\left(\omega_i\right)-G_p^{(n-1)}\left(\omega_i\right)\right|^2\right]^{1 / 2}}{\left[\sum_i w_i\left|G_p^{(n-1)}\left(\omega_i\right)\right|^2\right]^{1 / 2}}
% $$
\begin{equation}
    \Delta_G^{(n)} = \frac{\sqrt{\sum_i |G_{k}^{(n)}(\omega_i) - G_{k}^{(n-1)}(\omega_i)|^2}}{\sqrt{\sum_i |G_{k}^{(n-1)}(\omega_i)|^2}}
\end{equation}
where $\omega_i$ are the frequency grid points chosen, stopping once $\Delta_G^{(n)} < \epsilon$ for some small threshold $\epsilon$.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Experiment with how different parameters, including the broadening $\eta$, effect what choice of $\epsilon$ is realistic.
\end{enumerate}
\subsection{Parameters of interest}
We can obtain the spectral function $A_{k}(\omega)$ by generating a periodic signal from the converged retarded Green's function. Then, we can compute the total energies $E$ from Galitskii-Migdal as:
\begin{equation}
    E=\sum_k \int_{-\infty}^\mu\left(\omega+\epsilon_k\right) A_k(\omega) d \omega.
\end{equation}
where $\mu$ is the chemical potential, which we can take to be equivalent to the Fermi energy $\epsilon_F$, and $\epsilon_k=\frac{k^2}{2}$ is the bare single-particle energy. This allows us to compute the correlation energy per particle as 
\begin{equation}
    \epsilon_{\text{corr}} = \frac{E-E_{\text{HF}}}{N}
\end{equation}
where $N$ is the number of particles. Finally, we can obtain occupation numbers as \begin{equation}
    n_k = \int_{-\infty}^\mu A_k(\omega) d\omega.
\end{equation}
\section{Update notes}
 I compute the LDA+G0W0 spectral functions via
\begin{equation}
    A(\mathbf{k}, \omega) = \frac{1}{\pi}|\operatorname{Im}G^R(\mathbf{k}, \omega)| =
\frac{1}{\pi} |\frac{ \operatorname{Im} \Sigma^{c,R}(\mathbf{k}, \omega)[\eta_s]+\eta_P}{\left[\omega - \left(\varepsilon_{\mathbf{k}}^0+\Sigma_k^{\mathrm{x}}+ \operatorname{Re} \Sigma^{c,R}(\mathbf{k}, \omega)\right)\right]^2 + \left[\operatorname{Im} \Sigma^{c,R}(\mathbf{k}, \omega)[\eta_s]+\eta_P\right]^2}|,
\end{equation}
where I used $\eta_s=\eta_P=0.8 \text{ eV }$, computing $\operatorname{Im} \Sigma^{c,R}(\mathbf{k}, \omega)[\eta_s]$ via the exact sum over states expression. In computing LDA+G0W0+C, I use
\begin{equation}
    C_k(t) = \pi^{-1} \int d \omega\left|\operatorname{Im} \beta_k(\omega)\right|\frac{e^{-i \omega t}+i \omega t-1}{\omega^2}
\end{equation}
and then build the Green's function as 
\begin{equation}
    G^R(\mathbf{k}, t) = -i \theta(t) e^{-i \varepsilon_{\mathbf{k}}^{\text{eff}} t} e^{C_k(t)},
\end{equation}
where $\varepsilon_{\mathbf{k}}^{\text{eff}}=\varepsilon_{\mathbf{k}}^{\text{LDA}}+\Sigma_k^{\mathbf{x}}-V^{xc}_{\mathbf{k}}=\varepsilon^0_{\mathbf{k}}+\Sigma_k^{\mathbf{x}}$ with $\varepsilon^0_{\mathbf{k}}=\frac{k^2}{2}$. Two variants have been explored. 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $\beta_k(\omega)= \left|\operatorname{Im} \Sigma^{c,R}(\mathbf{k}, \omega+E^{\text{GW}}_k)[\eta_s]\right|$, which I call epep@qp.
    \item $\beta_k(\omega)= \left|\operatorname{Im} \Sigma^{c,R}(\mathbf{k}, \omega+\varepsilon_{\mathbf{k}}^{\text{LDA}})[\eta_s]\right|$, which I call epep@mf.
\end{enumerate}
\section{Insights from the independent boson model}
Here we work with the independent Boson model, which has the Hamiltonian:
\begin{equation}
H=\epsilon_c c^{\dagger} c+\sum_\nu \Omega_{c\nu} b_\nu^{\dagger} b_\nu+c^{\dagger} c \sum_\nu V_\nu\left(b_\nu+b_\nu^{\dagger}\right) .
\end{equation}
with $\Omega_{c\nu}= \tilde{\epsilon_c}-\epsilon_c+\Omega_\nu$, where $\Omega_\nu$ and $V_\nu$ are the excitation energies and couplings for the QB mode $\nu$. $\epsilon_c$ and $\tilde{\epsilon_c}$ are noninteracting single-particle energies of the core state at $\mathbf{k}_c=[0,0,0]$.  Note that for $G_{c,0}@\mathrm{HF}$, $\epsilon_c=\epsilon_c^{\mathrm{HF}}=\tilde{\epsilon_c}$; for $G_{c,0}@\mathrm{LDA}$, $\epsilon_c=\epsilon_c^{\mathrm{LDA}}+\Sigma_{cc}^{x}-v_{cc}^{xc}
=\epsilon_c^{\mathrm{HF}}$ while $\tilde{\epsilon_c}=\epsilon_c^{\mathrm{LDA}}$;
and for $G_{c,0}@\mathrm{BWs2}$, $\epsilon_c=\epsilon_c^{\mathrm{BWs2}}=\tilde{\epsilon_c}$. We have been able to compute QB quantities for dRPA@HF, dRPA@LDA, and BWs2 with $\alpha_1=2$ and $\alpha_2=-1,0,1$ for a variety of system sizes; they are all stored in
\begin{itemize}
    \item \texttt{qcpbc/libpbc/gw\_tests/useful\_tests\_all/ueg/7/quasiboson/quasiboson\_hf\_rpa\_rs4\_29492127.h5},
    \item \texttt{qcpbc/libpbc/gw\_tests/useful\_tests\_all/ueg/7/quasiboson/quasiboson\_lda\_rpa\_rs4\_29492515.h5},
    \item \texttt{qcpbc/libpbc/gw\_tests/useful\_tests\_all/ueg/7/quasiboson/quasiboson\_hf\_bws2\_a1\_2\_a2\_-1\_rs4\_29796407.h5},
    \item \texttt{qcpbc/libpbc/gw\_tests/useful\_tests\_all/ueg/7/quasiboson/quasiboson\_hf\_bws2\_a1\_2\_a2\_0\_rs4\_29796408.h5},
    \item \texttt{qcpbc/libpbc/gw\_tests/useful\_tests\_all/ueg/7/quasiboson/quasiboson\_hf\_bws2\_a1\_2\_a2\_1\_rs4\_29796365.h5}.
\end{itemize}
The single-particle energies are Exposed immediately within the h5s, whereas the QB quantities for a given momentum transfer vector $\mathbf{q}$ are nested, where each momentum transfer vector $\bm{q}$ belies a set of modes $\nu$. Should I just enumerate all possible modes $\nu$ in all possible $\bm{q}$ and store them in a long dictionary along with their couplings? Because right now the layout is such that we have separate groups for each $\bm{q}$ with the modes $\nu$ belonging to that $\bm{q}$ inside. Or is it imperative that once I have this long dictionary, I then sort it, placing degenerate modes together? The latter is more faithful to the equations, but would take an extra step to implement. I guess a useful check that we could do is once we have our long dictionary of modes $\nu$ with their excitation energies and couplings, we could then scan the excitation energies to see which modes are degenerate, and then we can demand the couplings should be identical.  For the 114e485o system, there are 3911 $\bm{q}$ vectors, each with potentially multiple modes $\nu$. Will the performance of the code suffer greatly if we don't organize before we run? The exact retarded Green's function for this model is given by
\begin{equation}
    G^R_{c,\text {exact }}(\omega)=\sum_{\left\{n_\nu\right\}} \frac{e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}} \prod_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu} / n_{\nu}!}{\omega-\epsilon_c+\sum_\nu (\zeta_{c\nu}-n_\nu )\Omega_{c\nu}+i \eta}, 
\label{eq:exact_g}
\end{equation}
with  $\zeta_{c\nu} = \frac{|V_\nu|^2}{\Omega_{c\nu}^2}$ and $n_\nu$ is the occupation number of the boson mode $\nu$.
\subsection{Landau cumulant}
The Landau cumulant is given by
\begin{equation}
    C_c^R(t) = \frac{1}{\pi}\int d\omega \frac{|\operatorname{Im} \Sigma^R(\omega+\tilde{\epsilon_c})|}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right),
\end{equation}
where for $G_{c,0}@\mathrm{HF}$, $\tilde{\epsilon_c} = \epsilon_c^{\mathrm{HF}}$; for $G_{c,0}@\mathrm{LDA}$, $\tilde{\epsilon_c} = \epsilon_c^{\mathrm{LDA}}$; and for $G_{c,0}@\mathrm{BWs2}$, $\tilde{\epsilon_c} = \epsilon_c^{\mathrm{BWs2}}$. Then we plug this into the cumulant ansatz for the Green's function as 
\begin{equation}
    G^R_c(t) = G_{c,0}^R(t) e^{C_c^R(t)},
\end{equation}
where $G_{c,0}^R(t) = -i \Theta(t) e^{-i\epsilon_c t}$.
\subsubsection{Putting the GW self-energy into the Landau cumulant}
 It is known that plugging in the GW form of the self-energy into the Landau cumulant and then using it in the cumulant ansatz for the Green's function reproduces the exact solution of \ref{eq:exact_g}. The GW self-energy for this model is given by
\begin{align}
    \Sigma^R_{c,GW}(\omega) &= \sum_\nu \frac{|V_\nu|^2}{\omega - \epsilon_c + \Omega_\nu + i\eta}\\
\implies \Sigma^R_{c,GW}(\omega+\tilde{\epsilon_c}) &= \sum_\nu \frac{|V_\nu|^2}{\omega + \Omega_{c\nu} + i\eta} \\
\implies \operatorname{Im} \Sigma^R_{c,GW}(\omega+\tilde{\epsilon_c}) &= -\pi \sum_\nu |V_\nu|^2 \frac{\eta}{(\omega + \Omega_{c\nu})^2 + \eta^2} \\
\implies C^R_{c,GW}(t) &= \int d\omega \frac{\sum_\nu |V_\nu|^2 \frac{\eta}{(\omega + \Omega_{c\nu})^2 + \eta^2}}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right)\\
&= \sum_{\nu} \zeta_{c\nu} \left[ e^{-i\Omega_{c\nu} t} - 1 + i\Omega_{c\nu} t \right]
\label{eq:Cpp_2} \\
\implies G^R_{c,GW}(t) &= G_{c,0}^R(t) e^{C_{c,GW}^R(t)} \\
&=
-i\Theta(t)e^{-i\epsilon_c t}
e^{\sum_{\nu}\zeta_{c\nu}
\left[
e^{-i\Omega_{c\nu}t}
-1
+i\Omega_{c\nu}t
\right]}
\\
&=
-i\Theta(t)e^{-i\epsilon_c t}
e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}}
e^{+i\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}\Omega_{c\nu}t}
\prod_\nu
\exp\left[
\zeta_{c\nu}e^{-i\Omega_{c\nu}t}
\right]
\\
&=
-i\Theta(t)e^{-i\epsilon_c t}
e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}}
e^{+i\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}\Omega_{c\nu}t}
\prod_\nu
\sum_{n_\nu=0}^{\infty}
\frac{
\left(
\zeta_{c\nu}e^{-i\Omega_{c\nu}t}
\right)^{n_\nu}
}{n_\nu!}
\\
&=
-i\Theta(t)e^{-i\epsilon_c t}
e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}}
e^{+i\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}\Omega_{c\nu}t}
\sum_{\{n_\nu\}}
\prod_\nu
\frac{\zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu}}{n_\nu!}
e^{-i\sum_\nu n_\nu\Omega_{c\nu}t}
\\
&=
-i \Theta(t)
\sum_{\{n_\nu\}}
e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}}
\prod_\nu
\frac{\zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu}}{n_\nu!}
e^{-i\left[
\epsilon_c
-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}\Omega_{c\nu}
+\sum_\nu n_\nu\Omega_{c\nu}
\right]t}\\
% &= -i \Theta(t) e^{-i\epsilon_c t} e^{\sum_{\nu} \zeta_{c\nu} \left[ e^{-i\Omega_{c\nu} t} - 1 + i\Omega_{c\nu} t \right]} \\
% &= -i \Theta(t) \sum_{\left\{n_\nu\right\}} e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}} \prod_\nu \frac{\zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu}}{n_{\nu}!} e^{-i\left[\epsilon_c-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu} \Omega_{c\nu}+\sum_\nu n_\nu \Omega_{c\nu}\right] t} \\
\implies G^R_{c,GW}(\omega) &= \sum_{\left\{n_\nu\right\}} \frac{e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}} \prod_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu} / n_{\nu}!}{\omega-\epsilon_c+\sum_\nu (\zeta_{c\nu} - n_\nu) \Omega_{c\nu}+i \eta}
\end{align}
\subsubsection{Putting the exact self-energy into the Landau cumulant}
In order to observe the ramifications of our SCCE procedure, we can compute the exact self-energy from the exact Green's function as
\begin{align}
    \Sigma^R_{c,\text{Dyson}}(\omega) &= \left[G_{c,0}^R(\omega)\right]^{-1} - \left[G^R_{c,\text{exact}}(\omega)\right]^{-1} \\
% \implies \Sigma^R_{c,\text{Dyson}}(\omega) &= \omega - \epsilon_c + i\eta - \left[\sum_{\left\{n_q\right\}} \frac{e^{-\sum_q a_q} \prod_q a_q^{n_q} / n_{q}!}{\omega-\epsilon_c+\Delta-\sum_q n_q \Omega_q+i \eta}\right]^{-1}\\
\implies \Sigma^R_{c,\text{Dyson}}(\omega) &= \omega - \epsilon_c + i\eta - \left[\sum_{\left\{n_\nu\right\}} \frac{e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}} \prod_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu} / n_{\nu}!}{\omega-\epsilon_c+\sum_\nu (\zeta_{c\nu} - n_\nu) \Omega_{c\nu}+i \eta}\right]^{-1}\\
\implies \Sigma^R_{c,\text{Dyson}}(\omega+\tilde{\epsilon_c}) &= 
\begin{cases}
    \omega + i\eta - \left[\sum_{\left\{n_\nu\right\}} \frac{e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}} \prod_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu} / n_{\nu}!}{\omega+\sum_\nu (\zeta_{c\nu} - n_\nu) \Omega_{c\nu}+i \eta}\right]^{-1}, & G_0@\text{HF or } G_0@\text{BWs2}\\
    \omega + v^{xc}_{cc} - \Sigma_{cc}^x + i\eta - \left[\sum_{\left\{n_\nu\right\}} \frac{e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}} \prod_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu} / n_{\nu}!}{\omega+v^{xc}_{cc} - \Sigma_{cc}^x+\sum_\nu (\zeta_{c\nu} - n_\nu) \Omega_{c\nu}+i \eta}\right]^{-1}, & G_0@\text{LDA}
\end{cases}
\end{align}
We should be able to back-out the value of $v^{xc}_{cc} - \Sigma_{cc}^x$ by taking $\epsilon_c^{\text{LDA}} - \epsilon_c^{\text{HF}}$.
Then, we can plug this into the Landau cumulant to get a $C^R_{c,\text{Dyson}}(t)$, which we can then use to get a $G^R_{c,\text{Dyson}}(t)$ and then Fourier transform to get $G^R_{c,\text{Dyson}}(\omega)$.
\subsection{Results for the independent boson model}
All results are at $r_s = 4$. The independent Boson model has the Hamiltonian:
\begin{equation}
H=\tilde{\epsilon_c} c^{\dagger} c+\sum_\nu \Omega_{\nu} b_\nu^{\dagger} b_\nu+c^{\dagger} c \sum_\nu V_\nu\left(b_\nu+b_\nu^{\dagger}\right) .
\end{equation}
where $\Omega_\nu$ and $V_\nu$ are quasiboson (QB) frequencies and couplings, which can be taken from dRPA@HF, dRPA@LDA, and BWs2 ($\alpha_1=2, \alpha_2=0$). The latter two are the ones which were found to give competitive spectral functions with respect to CCSD for 114e485o. $\tilde{\epsilon_c}$ is the noninteracting single-particle energy of the core state at $\mathbf{k}_c=[0,0,0]$. This is a model for which we can get the exact retarded Green's function, which is given by
\begin{equation}
    G^R_{c,\text {exact }}(\omega)= \frac{e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{\nu}} \prod_\nu \zeta_{\nu}}{\omega-\tilde{\epsilon_c}+\sum_\nu (\zeta_{\nu})\Omega_{\nu}+i \eta}, 
\label{eq:exact_g}
\end{equation}
with  $\zeta_{\nu} = \frac{|V_\nu|^2}{\Omega_{\nu}^2}$.

\subsubsection{Putting the exact self-energy into the Landau cumulant}
We can compute the exact self-energy from the exact Green's function as
\begin{align}
    \Sigma^R_{c,\text{Dyson}}(\omega) &= \left[G_{c,0}^R(\omega)\right]^{-1} - \left[G^R_{c,\text{exact}}(\omega)\right]^{-1} \\
% \implies \Sigma^R_{c,\text{Dyson}}(\omega) &= \omega - \epsilon_c + i\eta - \left[\sum_{\left\{n_q\right\}} \frac{e^{-\sum_q a_q} \prod_q a_q^{n_q} / n_{q}!}{\omega-\epsilon_c+\Delta-\sum_q n_q \Omega_q+i \eta}\right]^{-1}\\
% \implies \Sigma^R_{c,\text{Dyson}}(\omega) &= \omega - \epsilon_c + i\eta - \left[\sum_{\left\{n_\nu\right\}} \frac{e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}} \prod_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu} / n_{\nu}!}{\omega-\epsilon_c+\sum_\nu (\zeta_{c\nu} - n_\nu) \Omega_{c\nu}+i \eta}\right]^{-1}\\
\implies \Sigma^R_{c,\text{Dyson}}(\omega+\tilde{\epsilon_c}) &= \omega + i\eta - \left[ \frac{e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{\nu}} \prod_\nu \zeta_{\nu}}{\omega+\sum_\nu (\zeta_{\nu}) \Omega_{\nu}+i \eta}\right]^{-1}
% \begin{cases}
%     \omega + i\eta - \left[\sum_{\left\{n_\nu\right\}} \frac{e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}} \prod_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu} / n_{\nu}!}{\omega+\sum_\nu (\zeta_{c\nu} - n_\nu) \Omega_{c\nu}+i \eta}\right]^{-1}, & G_0@\text{HF or } G_0@\text{BWs2}\\
%     \omega + v^{xc}_{cc} - \Sigma_{cc}^x + i\eta - \left[\sum_{\left\{n_\nu\right\}} \frac{e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}} \prod_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu} / n_{\nu}!}{\omega+v^{xc}_{cc} - \Sigma_{cc}^x+\sum_\nu (\zeta_{c\nu} - n_\nu) \Omega_{c\nu}+i \eta}\right]^{-1}, & G_0@\text{LDA}
% \end{cases}
\end{align}
% We should be able to back-out the value of $v^{xc}_{cc} - \Sigma_{cc}^x$ by taking $\epsilon_c^{\text{LDA}} - \epsilon_c^{\text{HF}}$.
Getting the self-energy this way defines our self-consistent cumulant scheme (SC-CE). The way to read the plots is that I am doing $\Sigma_{\text{Dyson}}[G_{\text{exact}}] \rightarrow \text{SC-CE (Iter 1)} \rightarrow \Sigma_{\text{Dyson}}[G_1] \rightarrow \text{SC-CE (Iter 2)}$. This is where I terminate my iteration because it has become clear that the results I will get past this point are not useful. The exact spectral function and the GW self-energy are shown for reference. 
\subsection{Dyson versus cumulant expansion for the independent boson model (IBM)}
Let $\lambda$ count powers of $W$. The exact cumulant $C$ and exact self-energy $\Sigma$ admit expansions as 
\begin{equation}
    C=\lambda C^{(1)}+\lambda^2 C^{(2)}+\cdots
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma=\lambda \Sigma^{(1)}+\lambda^2 \Sigma^{(2)}+\cdots,
\end{equation}
respectively. 
% Therefore, the single-particle Green's function can be expanded via its cumulant ansatz as 
% \begin{equation}
%     G=G_0 e^C = G_0+\lambda G_0 C^{(1)}+\lambda^2 G_0\left(C^{(2)}+\frac{1}{2}\left[C^{(1)}\right]^2\right)+\cdots,
% \end{equation}
% or via the Dyson equation as
% \begin{equation}
%     G=G_0+\lambda G_0 \Sigma^{(1)} G_0+\lambda^2\left(G_0 \Sigma^{(2)} G_0+G_0 \Sigma^{(1)} G_0 \Sigma^{(1)} G_0\right)+\cdots.
% \end{equation}
Matching the cumulant and Dyson expansions of $G$ to first order in $W$ gives
\begin{equation}
    G_0 C^{(1)}=G_0 \Sigma^{(1)} G_0,
\end{equation}
 from which the Landau form for $C^{(1)}$ is derived. For the IBM, we can get the exact Green's function $G$ via
\begin{equation}
    G=G_0 \exp(C^{(1)}[\Sigma^{(1)}]), \quad \text{where } \Sigma^{(1)}\equiv \Sigma_{GW}.
\end{equation}
From this, we can get
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma = G_0^{-1} - G^{-1} = \lambda \Sigma^{(1)} + \lambda^2 \Sigma^{(2)} + \cdots.
\end{equation}
\subsubsection{SCCE(GW)}
Our self-consistent cumulant scheme then proposes to recumulate as
\begin{equation}
    \tilde{G}=G_0 \exp(C^{(1)}[\Sigma^{(1)} + \Sigma^{(2)} + \cdots]).
\end{equation}
Assuming high-order terms of the self-energy are non-zero (this is reasonable because we expect the self energy to decay slowly), we then have $\tilde{G}\neq G$. In other words, our scheme incorpates vertex corrections in an uncontrolled manner. Since it fails for the simple model system of the IBM, it cannot be trusted to deliver reliable results for more complex systems, such as the electron gas.
\subsubsection{SCCE($GW_0$)}
In Holms' scheme, at iteration $n\geq 1$ we update the self-energy for occupied state $i$ as
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma_i^{n,R}(t)=i \sum_j\left[G_j^{n-1,R}(t) W_{i j}^{0,>}(t)+G_j^{n-1,<}(t) W_{i j}^{0, R}(t)\right],
\end{equation}
where $G_j^{n-1}$ is the cumulant Green's function of state $j$ from the previous iteration. Then, we do a DFT to get $\Sigma$ into the frequency domain and stick it into the Landau form. Since $G_j^{n-1}$, for all $n\geq 0$, contains all orders of $W$, the IBM spectral functions past one-shot cannot give the exact result, assuming $C\neq0\implies G\neq G_0\implies \Sigma^n \neq \underbrace{\Sigma^{(1)}}_{\Sigma_{G_0W_0}}$, since we know that $C^{(1)}[\Sigma^{(1)}]$ gives the exact result for the IBM. My IBM plots support this conclusion.
\begin{tcolorbox}[title=Moving to more complex models ]
I understand that even if SCCE($GW_0$) is bad for the IBM, it may still be useful for the UEG. However, theoretically speaking, in order to use the Landau form, the cumulant and Dyson expansions of $G$ should be matched up to first order in $W$, but as argued above SCCE($GW_0$) violates this for all $n\geq 1$.
\end{tcolorbox}
\begin{tcolorbox}[title=Results for the UEG ]
 My UEG plot for 114e485o supports this conclusion that SCCE($GW_0$) doesn't improve results past one-shot.
\end{tcolorbox}
\begin{tcolorbox}[title=Results for the finite-size Holstein model]

\subsection{Holms' \(GW_0\) self-consistent cumulant scheme for the independent boson model}
Here we work with the independent Boson model, which has the Hamiltonian:
\begin{equation}
H=\epsilon_c c^{\dagger} c+\sum_\nu \Omega_{c\nu} b_\nu^{\dagger} b_\nu+c^{\dagger} c \sum_\nu V_\nu\left(b_\nu+b_\nu^{\dagger}\right) .
\end{equation}
with $\Omega_{c\nu}= \tilde{\epsilon_c}-\epsilon_c+\Omega_\nu$, where $\Omega_\nu$ and $V_\nu$ are the excitation energies and couplings for the QB mode $\nu$. $\epsilon_c$ and $\tilde{\epsilon_c}$ are noninteracting single-particle energies of the core state at $\mathbf{k}_c=[0,0,0]$.  Note that for $G_{c,0}@\mathrm{HF}$, $\epsilon_c=\epsilon_c^{\mathrm{HF}}=\tilde{\epsilon_c}$; for $G_{c,0}@\mathrm{LDA}$, $\epsilon_c=\epsilon_c^{\mathrm{LDA}}+\Sigma_{cc}^{x}-v_{cc}^{xc}
=\epsilon_c^{\mathrm{HF}}$ while $\tilde{\epsilon_c}=\epsilon_c^{\mathrm{LDA}}$. We have been able to compute QB quantities for dRPA@HF, dRPA@LDA for a variety of system sizes; they are all stored in
\begin{itemize}
    \item \texttt{qcpbc/libpbc/gw\_tests/useful\_tests\_all/ueg/7/quasiboson/quasiboson\_hf\_rpa\_rs4\_29492127.h5},
    \item \texttt{qcpbc/libpbc/gw\_tests/useful\_tests\_all/ueg/7/quasiboson/quasiboson\_lda\_rpa\_rs4\_29492515.h5},
\end{itemize}
The single-particle energies are Exposed immediately within the h5s, whereas the QB quantities for a given momentum transfer vector $\mathbf{q}$ are nested, where each momentum transfer vector $\bm{q}$ belies a set of modes $\nu$. Also note that for this independent Boson model, my h5 files give all of the possible momentum transfers starting from occupied states. Since in this model I am only considering the excitations that can arise after the core state, the subset of allowed excitations is indeed smaller. I think this is already taken into account in the pricing script, but I just want you to be aware of this issue.  The exact retarded Green's function for this model is given by
\begin{equation}
    G^R_{c,\text {exact }}(\omega)=\sum_{\left\{n_\nu\right\}} \frac{e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}} \prod_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu} / n_{\nu}!}{\omega-\epsilon_c+\sum_\nu (\zeta_{c\nu}-n_\nu )\Omega_{c\nu}+i \eta}, 
\label{eq:exact_g}
\end{equation}
with  $\zeta_{c\nu} = \frac{|V_\nu|^2}{\Omega_{c\nu}^2}$ and $n_\nu$ is the occupation number of the boson mode $\nu$. 
\subsection{Landau cumulant}
The Landau cumulant is given by
\begin{equation}
    C_c^R(t) = \frac{1}{\pi}\int d\omega \frac{|\operatorname{Im} \Sigma^R(\omega+\tilde{\epsilon_c})|}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right),
\end{equation}
where for $G_{c,0}@\mathrm{HF}$, $\tilde{\epsilon_c} = \epsilon_c^{\mathrm{HF}}$; for $G_{c,0}@\mathrm{LDA}$, $\tilde{\epsilon_c} = \epsilon_c^{\mathrm{LDA}}$. Then we plug this into the cumulant ansatz for the Green's function as 
\begin{equation}
    G^R_c(t) = G_{c,0}^R(t) e^{C_c^R(t)},
\end{equation}
where $G_{c,0}^R(t) = -i \Theta(t) e^{-i\epsilon_c t}$.
\subsubsection{Putting the GW self-energy into the Landau cumulant}
 It is known that plugging in the GW form of the self-energy into the Landau cumulant and then using it in the cumulant ansatz for the Green's function reproduces the exact solution of \ref{eq:exact_g}. The GW self-energy for this model is given by
\begin{align}
    \Sigma^R_{c,GW}(\omega) &= \sum_\nu \frac{|V_\nu|^2}{\omega - \epsilon_c + \Omega_\nu + i\eta}\\
\implies \Sigma^R_{c,GW}(\omega+\tilde{\epsilon_c}) &= \sum_\nu \frac{|V_\nu|^2}{\omega + \Omega_{c\nu} + i\eta} \\
\implies \operatorname{Im} \Sigma^R_{c,GW}(\omega+\tilde{\epsilon_c}) &= -\pi \sum_\nu |V_\nu|^2 \frac{\eta}{(\omega + \Omega_{c\nu})^2 + \eta^2} \\
\implies C^R_{c,GW}(t) &= \int d\omega \frac{\sum_\nu |V_\nu|^2 \frac{\eta}{(\omega + \Omega_{c\nu})^2 + \eta^2}}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right)\\
&= \sum_{\nu} \zeta_{c\nu} \left[ e^{-i\Omega_{c\nu} t} - 1 + i\Omega_{c\nu} t \right]
\label{eq:Cpp_2} \\
\implies G^R_{c,GW}(t) &= G_{c,0}^R(t) e^{C_{c,GW}^R(t)} \\
&=
-i\Theta(t)e^{-i\epsilon_c t}
e^{\sum_{\nu}\zeta_{c\nu}
\left[
e^{-i\Omega_{c\nu}t}
-1
+i\Omega_{c\nu}t
\right]}
\\
&=
-i\Theta(t)e^{-i\epsilon_c t}
e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}}
e^{+i\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}\Omega_{c\nu}t}
\prod_\nu
\exp\left[
\zeta_{c\nu}e^{-i\Omega_{c\nu}t}
\right]
\\
&=
-i\Theta(t)e^{-i\epsilon_c t}
e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}}
e^{+i\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}\Omega_{c\nu}t}
\prod_\nu
\sum_{n_\nu=0}^{\infty}
\frac{
\left(
\zeta_{c\nu}e^{-i\Omega_{c\nu}t}
\right)^{n_\nu}
}{n_\nu!}
\\
&=
-i\Theta(t)e^{-i\epsilon_c t}
e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}}
e^{+i\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}\Omega_{c\nu}t}
\sum_{\{n_\nu\}}
\prod_\nu
\frac{\zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu}}{n_\nu!}
e^{-i\sum_\nu n_\nu\Omega_{c\nu}t}
\\
&=
-i \Theta(t)
\sum_{\{n_\nu\}}
e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}}
\prod_\nu
\frac{\zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu}}{n_\nu!}
e^{-i\left[
\epsilon_c
-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}\Omega_{c\nu}
+\sum_\nu n_\nu\Omega_{c\nu}
\right]t}\\
% &= -i \Theta(t) e^{-i\epsilon_c t} e^{\sum_{\nu} \zeta_{c\nu} \left[ e^{-i\Omega_{c\nu} t} - 1 + i\Omega_{c\nu} t \right]} \\
% &= -i \Theta(t) \sum_{\left\{n_\nu\right\}} e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}} \prod_\nu \frac{\zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu}}{n_{\nu}!} e^{-i\left[\epsilon_c-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu} \Omega_{c\nu}+\sum_\nu n_\nu \Omega_{c\nu}\right] t} \\
\implies G^R_{c,GW}(\omega) &= \sum_{\left\{n_\nu\right\}} \frac{e^{-\sum_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}} \prod_\nu \zeta_{c\nu}^{n_\nu} / n_{\nu}!}{\omega-\epsilon_c+\sum_\nu (\zeta_{c\nu} - n_\nu) \Omega_{c\nu}+i \eta}
\end{align}
\subsubsection{Putting the exact self-energy into the Landau cumulant}

\section{Reproducing Holms numbers for G0W0+C in the TDL}
I am interested in reproducing Holms curves for the one-shot and self-consistent cumulant schemes he outlined in his paper (attached). However, they formulated their theory in the TDL and CBS, but I am interested in what happens with the finite basis. I think they are getting the spectral function from the time-ordered Green function at zero temperature. A way to do this is described in Reining's paper (attached). They instruct to build the Greens function as 
\begin{equation}
G_k^T(t)=G_k^{T, 0}(t) e^{\tilde{C}_k^T(t)}
\end{equation}
where the non-interacting part is $G_k^{T, 0}(t)=\mp i \theta( \pm t) \theta\left( \pm\left(\epsilon_k-\epsilon_{k F}\right)\right) e^{-i \epsilon_k^{\text{eff}} t}$, where the upper (lower) sign refers to particles with $k>k_F$ (holes $k<k_F$ ), and $\theta$ is the unit step function. Our reference is an LDA calculation and we are at zero temperature, so we can take $\epsilon_k=\epsilon_k^{\text{LDA}}$, $\epsilon_k^{\text{eff}}=\epsilon_k^{\text{LDA}}+\Sigma_k^x - v_k^{xc}$, and $\epsilon_{k_F}=\epsilon_{k_F}^{\text{LDA}}=\frac{\epsilon_{k\equiv\text{HOMO}}^{\text{LDA}} + \epsilon_{k\equiv\text{LUMO}}^{\text{LDA}}}{2}$.
The dynamical cumulant is defined as 
\begin{equation}
    \tilde{C}_k^T(t)=\int d \omega \frac{\beta_k^{ \pm}(\omega)}{\omega^2}\left(e^{-i \omega t}+i \omega t-1\right) .
\end{equation}
Note that $\beta_k(\omega)=\beta_k^{+}(\omega)+\beta_k^{-}(\omega)$, where $\beta_k^{ \pm}(\omega)= \frac{1}{\pi}\left|\operatorname{Im} \Sigma_k^R\left(\omega+\epsilon_k\right)\right| \theta\left( \pm\left(\epsilon_{k_F}-\epsilon_k-\omega\right)\right)$. Then we can Generate a periodic signal from the time-ordered Green's function like we did before
at which point we get the 1shot spectral function as $A_k(\omega) = \frac{1}{\pi} |\operatorname{Im} G^T_k(\omega)|$.
\section{Efficiently reaching the TDL with G0W0+C}
In order to form the self-energy, which is proportional to the cumulant kernel, we need to build the retarded GW self-energy. The formal expression is
\begin{equation}
\Sigma_c^R(\mathbf{k}, \omega)=\sum_{\mathbf{q} \nu}\left|M_{\mathbf{k}, \mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}}^\nu\right|^2\left[\frac{f_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}}}{\omega-\xi_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}}+\Omega_{\mathbf{q} \nu}+i \eta}+\frac{1-f_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}}}{\omega-\xi_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}}-\Omega_{\mathbf{q} \nu}+i \eta}\right] .
\end{equation}
where $\xi_{\mathrm{k}}=\frac{k^2}{2}-\mu_0$ with the chemical potential $\mu_0=\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{HOMO}}+\epsilon_{\mathrm{LUMO}}}{2}$.
% \begin{equation}
%     \Sigma^R_{\text{corr}}(\bm{k},\omega) = \sum_{\bm{q}}\sum_{\nu }^{\text{RPA}} \frac{4\pi}{|\bm{q}|^2} |\rho_\nu(\bm{q})|^2 \left[ \frac{f_{\bm{k} - \bm{q}}}{\omega + \Omega_\nu(\bm{q}) - \epsilon_{\bm{k} - \bm{q}} + i\eta} + \frac{1 - f_{\bm{k} - \bm{q}}}{\omega - \Omega_\nu(\bm{q}) - \epsilon_{\bm{k} - \bm{q}} + i\eta} \right].
% \end{equation}
In order to evaluate this expression efficiently, we start by summing over all valid momentum transfer vectors $\bm{q}$. This step is embarrassingly parallel.
\subsection{Casida eigenproblem for a momentum transfer}
We can generate the RPA quantities at each momentum transfer vector $\bm{q}$ by solving its Casida eigenproblem. The Casida eigenproblem is given by
\begin{equation}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \bm{A}(\bm{q}) & \bm{B}(\bm{q}) \\
        -\bm{B}(\bm{q}) & -\bm{A}(\bm{q})
    \end{pmatrix}
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \bm{X}(\bm{q}) & \bm{Y}(\bm{q}) \\
        \bm{Y}(\bm{q}) & \bm{X}(\bm{q})
    \end{pmatrix}
=
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \bm{\Omega}(\bm{q}) & \bm{0} \\
        \bm{0} & -\bm{\Omega}(\bm{q})
    \end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}
        \bm{X}(\bm{q}) & \bm{Y}(\bm{q}) \\
        \bm{Y}(\bm{q}) & \bm{X}(\bm{q})
    \end{pmatrix}.
    \label{eqn:casida_eq}
\end{equation}
In practice, we can solve an eigenproblem of half the size by exploiting the symmetric formulation of the Casida eigenproblem, which is already implemented.
The $\bm{A}(\bm{q})$ and $\bm{B}(\bm{q})$ matrices are defined as
\begin{align}
    A_{\bm{k}_i \bm{k}_a,\, \bm{k}_b \bm{k}_j }(\bm{q}) &= \left(\xi_{\bm{k}_a}-\xi_{\bm{k}_i}\right) \delta_{\bm{k}_i \bm{k}_j} \delta_{\bm{k}_a \bm{k}_b} + \frac{4\pi}{|\bm{q}|^2} \\
    B_{\bm{k}_i \bm{k}_a,\, \bm{k}_j \bm{k}_b}(\bm{q}) &= \frac{4\pi}{|\bm{q}|^2}
\end{align}
In which we consider all occupied and virtual wave vectors connected by the momentum transfer $\bm{q}$, i.e. $\bm{k}_a = \bm{k}_i + \bm{q}$ and $\bm{k}_b = \bm{k}_j + \bm{q}$. 
After solving the Casida eigenvalue problem, I perform the bioorthogonalization procedure, which ensures that
\begin{equation}
    \left(\bm{X}(\bm{q}) - \bm{Y}(\bm{q})\right)^\dagger \left(\bm{X}(\bm{q}) + \bm{Y}(\bm{q})\right) = \bm{I}
    % \left(\bm{X} - \bm{Y}\right)^\dagger \left(\bm{X} + \bm{Y}\right) = \bm{I}
\end{equation}
Then, I have a list of excitation energies $\bm{\Omega}$ and corresponding right eigenvectors $\bm{X}, \bm{Y}$ for each momentum transfer $\bm{q}$. But for the self energy, we actually need rather the transition densities $M_{\bm{k}, \bm{k}-\bm{q}}^\nu$ for each mode $\nu$ at each momentum transfer $\bm{q}$. These are given by
\begin{equation}
M_{\mathrm{k}, \mathrm{k}-\mathrm{q}}^\nu=\frac{v_q}{\mathcal{V}} \sum_{i a \in \mathrm{q}}\left(X_{i a}^\nu+Y_{i a}^\nu\right),
\end{equation}
where $v_q = 4\pi/|\bm{q}|^2$ is the bare Coulomb interaction and $\mathcal{V}$ is the volume of the simulation cell. $f_{\bm{k}}$ is the occupation number of state $\bm{k}$. We are at zero temperature, so this should be 1 for occupied states and 0 for unoccupied states. \emph{Should we devote 1/32 CPUs in OpenMPI to each momentum transfer vector $\bm{q}$ to parallelize the self-energy evaluation?}
\subsubsection{Verification}
With these ingredients, we can construct the screened-interaction pole expansion directly:
$$
W_{0, c}^R(\mathbf{q}, \omega)=\sum_\nu\left|M_{\mathbf{q} \nu}\right|^2\left[\frac{1}{\omega-\Omega_{\mathbf{q} \nu}+i \eta}-\frac{1}{\omega+\Omega_{\mathbf{q} \nu}+i \eta}\right],
$$
and numerically verify that it reproduces
$$
W_{0, c}^R(\mathbf{q}, \omega)=v_q\left[\frac{1}{1-v_q \chi_0^R(\mathbf{q}, \omega)}-1\right] .
$$
in order to ensure that I don't have any missing factors of $v_q, \mathcal{V}$, two, or spin degeneracy.
