I was able to reproduce Garnet's reported TDL curve, using a continuum integration approach following Reining (the implementation uses the one-dimensional quadrature you had mentioned yesterday ). To get this to work, I used $\eta_{\Sigma} \approx 0^+ =$ 2e-2 eV and $\eta_G = 0.8 \text{eV}$.
\subsubsection{What I was doing in what I showed you yesterday  }
Following Loos' convention, I was choosing $\eta_G = 0$ and $\eta_{\Sigma} = 0.8 \text{eV}$, which was giving me the OLD plots.
\subsubsection{What do I do in these updated plots}
These plots are using $\eta_G = 0.8 \text{eV}$ and $\eta_{\Sigma} =$ 2e-2 eV. I chose the latter parameter for my initial test, because choosing smaller $\eta_{\Sigma}$ did not make a qualitative difference to the 114e485o spectral functions. I submitted tests to check that this is indeed the case also for larger supercells. These plots are labeled NEW.
\subsection{Quasiparticle equation}
For a given wave vector $\bm{k}$, the linearized quasiparticle equation is
\begin{equation}
    E^{GW}_k = \epsilon_k + Z_k \operatorname{Re} \Sigma_k^R(\epsilon_k)
\end{equation}
where $Z_k = \left(1 - \frac{\partial \Sigma_k^R}{\partial \omega} \big|_{\omega = \epsilon_k}\right)^{-1}$ is the renormalization factor and $\Sigma_k^R(\epsilon_k)= \Sigma^R_{\text{corr}}(\bm{k},\epsilon_k) + \Sigma_k^x$.

\section{SCCE in the finite basis}
\subsection{GW0}
% \subsubsection{Expansion around the current pole at each iteration}
In the retarded formalism, the cumulant ansatz for the interacting Green's function at the iteration $n+1$ is
\begin{align} G^{n+1,R}_{k}(t) &= G_{0,k}^{n,R}(t) e^{C_{k}^{n+1,R}(t)}
\label{eq:cumulant_G}\end{align}
where $G_{0,k}^{n,R}(t) = -i \Theta(t) e^{-i \left(k^2/2 + \Gamma_k^{n}\right) t}$ with, for $n\geq0$, $\Gamma_k^{n+1} = n_{k}^{(n)}v_m+\Sigma_k^{n+1,x(E)}[G^{(n)}]=n_{k}^{(n)}v_m-\sum_{\bm{q}\neq\bm{0}} n_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}}^{(n)}v(\bm{q})$ and $\Gamma_k^{0} = V_k^{xc}$. $\Sigma_k^{n+1,x(E)}[G^{(n)}]$ is the exchange self-energy at iteration $n+1$ computed with Ewald summation (neglecting the $\bm{q}=0$ term) and $n_{k}^{(n)}v_m$ is the Madelung potential at iteration $n$. Note that this may change during self-consistent iteration as the chemical potential and thus occupation numbers are updated. We are always expanding around the current pole $E_k^{(n)}$; at the one-shot level, $E_k^{(0)} = \varepsilon_k^{\rm LDA}$, but for $n>0$, $E_k^{(n)}=k^2/2 + \Gamma_k^{n} + \operatorname{Re}\Sigma_k^{n,c}[G^{(n-1)}](E_k^{(n-1)})$.
\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=red!5!white, colframe=red!75!black, title=Intuition behind this decision ]
In any self-consistent flavor of GW based on iteration of the Dyson equation, the scheme is to compute the quasiparticle energies and then feed that back into the self-energy to close the self-consistency loop. Therefore, an interpretation is that this scheme is doing the exact same thing, but in the cumulant formalism.
\end{tcolorbox}
Equating the Dyson and
cumulant expansions to first order in the screened interaction yields,
\begin{align}
C_k^{n+1,R}(t) &= i e^{iE_k^{(n)} t}\int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t}
\frac{k^2/2+\Gamma_k^{n+1}-\left(k^2/2 + \Gamma_k^{n}\right) +\Sigma_k^{n+1,c}[G^{(n)}](\omega) }{(\omega - E_k^{(n)} + i\eta)^2}\\
&= -i \left(\Gamma_k^{n+1}-\Gamma_k^{n}\right) t \Theta(t) + \underbrace{i
\int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t}
\frac{\Sigma_k^{n+1,c}[G^{(n)}](\omega + E_k^{(n)})}{(\omega + i\eta)^2}}_{C_k^{n+1,\text{dyn}}(t)}.
\end{align}
where $\Sigma_k^{n+1,c}[G^{(n)}](\omega)$ is the correlation self-energy at iteration $n+1$.
We can rewrite the dynamical cumulant as 
\begin{align}
C_k^{n+1,\text{dyn}}(t) &= \int d\omega\, \beta_{k}^{n+1,R}(\omega)\,
\frac{e^{-i\omega t}-1}{\omega^{2}}\\
&= \underbrace{\int\!\dd\omega\,\beta_{k}^{n+1,R}(\omega)
    \frac{e^{-i\omega t}+i\omega t-1}{\omega^2}}_{C^{\rm Landau}_k(t)}
  \;-\; i\,S_1[\beta_k]\,t
\end{align}
where $\beta_{k,c}^{n+1,R}(\omega)=\frac{1}{\pi}\left|\operatorname{Im}\Sigma_k^{n+1,c}
\!\left(\omega+E_k^{(n)}\right)\right|$ and $S_1[\beta_k]=\mathcal{P} \int\!\dd\omega\,\beta_{k}^{n+1,R}(\omega)/\omega$. At a given iteration, the correlation self-energy takes the form:
\begin{align}
\Sigma_{m, c}^{(n+1),R}[G^{(n)}](t) &= -i\theta(t)\left[\sum_{i, I}\left|M_{ m,i}^I\right|^2 G^{(n),<}_{i}(t) e^{-i\Omega_I t} + \sum_{a, I}\left|M_{ m,a}^I\right|^2 G^{(n),>}_{a}(t) e^{-i\Omega_I t}\right],
\label{eq:correlation_self_energy_gw}
\end{align}
with $M$ and $\Omega$ the transition densities and energies from the dRPA problem. 
This allows us to write the Green's function as 
\begin{align}
G_k^{n+1,R}(t) &= - i\Theta(t)\, \exp\left[-i \left(k^2/2+\Gamma_k^{n+1}\right) t \right] e^{C_k^{n+1,\text{dyn}}(t)}.
\end{align}For iterations $n \geq 1$: Assume that we have $A_k^{(n-1)}(\omega)\propto \operatorname{Im}
G_k^{(n-1)}(\omega)$. 
This gives us a recipe to update the chemical potential $\mu^{(n+1)}$, where $\mu^{(1)}=\frac{\left(k^2/2 + \Gamma^1_k\right)_{\rm HOMO}+\left(k^2/2 + \Gamma^1_k\right)_{\rm LUMO}}{2}$ as
\begin{equation}
    N/2 = \sum_k \int_{-\infty}^{\mu^{(n+1)}} A_k^{(n)}(\omega) d \omega,
\end{equation}
where we can take $N/2$ as the number of occupied bands in our spin-restricted UEG. Once we have the chemical potential, we determine the lesser and greater Green's functions as 
\begin{align}
    G_k^{<,(n)}(t)&=i \int_{-\infty}^{\mu^{(n+1)}} d \omega A_k^{(n)}(\omega) e^{-i \omega t} \\
    G_k^{>,(n)}(t)&=-i \int_{\mu^{(n+1)}}^{\infty} d \omega A_k^{(n)}(\omega) e^{-i \omega t} .
\end{align}
Then, we would do a DFT to get the real and imaginary parts of $\Sigma_{p, c}^{n+1,R}(\omega)$, which can then be used to update $G_k^{n+1,R}(t)$. This closes the self-consistency loop.
\subsubsection{Doing it with forward time integration }
The cumulant representation of the Green's function is
\begin{equation}
    G_k(t)
    =
    G_k^{(0)}(t)e^{C_k(t)}
    =
    G_k^{(0)}(t)
    \left[
        1+C_k(t)+\cdots
    \right].
\label{eq:ha_cumulant_ansatz}
\end{equation}
where to determine the cumulant, we compare this expression with the Dyson
expansion,
\begin{equation}
    G_k(\omega)
    =
    G_k^{(0)}(\omega)
    +
    G_k^{(0)}(\omega)
    \Sigma_k(\omega)
    G_k^{(0)}(\omega)
    +\cdots.
\label{eq:ha_dyson_expansion}
\end{equation}
Equating the terms first order in the coupling and Fourier transforming
the Dyson contribution to the time domain gives
\begin{equation}
    C_k(t)
    =
    \frac{1}{G_k^{(0)}(t)}
    \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}
    \frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\,
    e^{-i\omega t}
    \Sigma_k(\omega)
    \left[
        G_k^{(0)}(\omega)
    \right]^2.
\label{eq:ha_cumulant_frequency}
\end{equation}
For the electron gas, the GW self-energy in the time domain is given as 
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma_k(t)
    =
    i
    \sum_q
    G_{k+q}(t)
    W_q^{(0)}(t).
\label{eq:ha_gw_selfenergy_time}
\end{equation}
The time-domain cumulant can therefore be written as
\begin{align}
    C_k(t)
    &=
    \frac{1}{G_k^{(0)}(t)}
    \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}
    \frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\,
    e^{-i\omega t}
    \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}
    dt_1\,e^{i t_1\omega}
    \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}
    dt_2\,e^{i t_2\omega}
    \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}
    dt_3\,e^{i t_3\omega}
    \nonumber\\
    &\qquad\times
    \Sigma_k(t_1)
    G_k^{(0)}(t_2)
    G_k^{(0)}(t_3).
\label{eq:ha_cumulant_time_full}
\end{align}
Collecting the exponential factors,
\begin{equation}
    C_k(t)
    =
    \frac{1}{G_k^{(0)}(t)}
    \int\!\!\int\!\!\int\!\!\int
    \frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\,
    dt_1\,dt_2\,dt_3\,
    e^{i\omega(t_1+t_2+t_3-t)}
    \Sigma_k(t_1)
    G_k^{(0)}(t_2)
    G_k^{(0)}(t_3).
\label{eq:ha_cumulant_combined_exp}
\end{equation}
The frequency integral generates a delta function,
\begin{equation}
    \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}
    \frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\,
    e^{i\omega(t_1+t_2+t_3-t)}
    =
    \delta(t_1+t_2+t_3-t),
\end{equation}
which imposes
\begin{equation}
    t_3=t-t_1-t_2.
\end{equation}
Thus,
\begin{equation}
    C_k(t)
    =
    \frac{1}{G_k^{(0)}(t)}
    \int\!\!\int
    dt_1\,dt_2\,
    \Sigma_k(t_1)
    G_k^{(0)}(t_2)
    G_k^{(0)}(t-t_1-t_2).
\label{eq:ha_cumulant_delta}
\end{equation}
We now specialize to the retarded Green's function,
\begin{equation}
    G_k^{(0)}(t)
    =
    -i\theta(t)e^{-i\epsilon_k^{\rm HF} t}.
\label{eq:ha_retarded_g0}
\end{equation}
Substituting this into Eq.~\eqref{eq:ha_cumulant_delta} gives
\begin{align}
    C_k(t)
    &=
    -i e^{i\epsilon_k t}
    \int\!\!\int
    dt_1\,dt_2\,
    \Sigma_k(t_1)
    \theta(t_1)
    \theta(t_2)
    \theta(t-t_1-t_2)
    \nonumber\\
    &\qquad\times
    e^{-i\epsilon_k^{\rm HF} t_2}
    e^{-i\epsilon_k^{\rm HF}(t-t_1-t_2)}.
\label{eq:ha_cumulant_retarded}
\end{align}
The step functions restrict the integration domain, yielding
\begin{equation}
    C_k(t)
    =
    -i
    \int_0^t dt_2
    \int_0^{t_2} dt_1\,
    \Sigma_k(t_1)
    e^{i\epsilon_k^{\rm HF} t_1}.
\label{eq:ha_cumulant_triangular}
\end{equation}
Since
\begin{equation}
    G_k^{(0)}(t_1)
    =
    -i \theta(t_1)e^{-i\epsilon_k^{\rm HF} t_1},
\end{equation}
we may equivalently write
\begin{equation}
    C_k(t)
    =
    -
    \int_0^t dt_2
    \int_0^{t_2} dt_1\,
    \frac{\Sigma_k(t_1)}
         {G_k^{(0)}(t_1)}.
\label{eq:ha_cumulant_final}
\end{equation}
Using
\begin{equation}
    G_k(t)
    =
    G_k^{(0)}(t)e^{C_k(t)},
\end{equation}
we differentiate to obtain
\begin{equation}
    \frac{\partial G_k(t)}{\partial t}
    =
    -i\epsilon_k G_k(t)
    -
    \int_0^t d\tau\,
    \frac{
        \Sigma_k(\tau)G_k(t)
    }{
        G_k^{(0)}(\tau)
    }.
\label{eq:ha_g_eom}
\end{equation}
Substituting in the GW self-energy,
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma_k(\tau)
    =
    i\sum_q
    G_{k+q}(\tau)W_q^{(0)}(\tau),
\end{equation}
and rearranging gives
\begin{equation}
    0
    =
    i\frac{\partial G_k(t)}{\partial t}
    -
    \epsilon_kG_k(t)
    +
    \sum_q
    \int_0^t d\tau\,
    W_q^{(0)}(\tau)
    \frac{
        G_{k+q}(\tau)G_k(t)
    }{
        G_k^{(0)}(\tau)
    }.
\label{eq:ha_eom_gw}
\end{equation}
To make contact with the time-domain Dyson equation, we may formally
multiply and divide the kernel by \(G_k(\tau)\),
\begin{equation}
    0
    =
    i\frac{\partial G_k(t)}{\partial t}
    -
    \epsilon_kG_k(t)
    +
    \sum_q
    \int_0^t d\tau\,
    W_q^{(0)}(\tau)
    \frac{
        G_{k+q}(\tau)G_k(t)
    }{
        G_k^{(0)}(\tau)G_k(\tau)
    }
    G_k(\tau).
\label{eq:ha_dyson_like}
\end{equation}
Hence, the HA-SCC construction may be interpreted as replacing the
usual self-energy kernel by the effective kernel
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma_k(t-\tau)
    \longrightarrow
    -
    W_q^{(0)}(\tau)
    \frac{
        G_{k+q}(\tau)G_k(t)
    }{
        G_k^{(0)}(\tau)G_k(\tau)
    }.
\label{eq:ha_effective_selfenergy}
\end{equation}
My hypothesis is that we would recover the self-consistent scheme carried out in the frequency domain if we froze the exchange. 
\section{Fermionic self-consistent cumulants}
\subsection{GW+C formalism}
\subsubsection{One-shot formalism}
Define the first-order cumulant as $C_p^{(1)}(t) \equiv -\mathrm{i}\,\delta_p t$, where $
\delta_p \equiv \varepsilon_p^{\rm HF}-\varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA} = \Sigma^{\rm x}_{pp}-V^{\rm xc}_{pp}$. With screening parameters $W_{pq\nu}$ and $\Omega_\nu$ computed from dRPA@LDA, the second-order cumulant is
\begin{equation}
C_p^{(2)}(t)=-\mathrm{i} \sum_{q \nu} W_{p q \nu}^2 \int_0^t \mathrm{d}\tau \int_0^\tau \mathrm{d}\tau'\,
D_\nu^{(q)}(\tau-\tau')\,
\exp\!\left[\mathrm{i} \varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA} \tau-\mathrm{i} \varepsilon_q^{\rm LDA} \tau
+\mathrm{i} \varepsilon_q^{\rm LDA} \tau'-\mathrm{i} \varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA} \tau'\right], \label{eq:cp2}
\end{equation}
using $D_\nu^{(q)}(t) \equiv -\mathrm{i}\, \Theta(t)\, e^{-\mathrm{i} s_q \Omega_\nu t}$ and
$s_q= \Theta(\varepsilon_q^{\rm LDA} - \varepsilon_F) - \Theta(\varepsilon_F - \varepsilon_q^{\rm LDA})$.
The retarded Green's function is
\begin{equation}
G_p^R(t) = -\mathrm{i}\, \Theta(t)\, e^{C_p^{(0)}(t)+C_p^{(1)}(t)+C_p^{(2)}(t)}
= -\mathrm{i}\, \Theta(t)\, e^{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_p^{\rm HF} t}\, e^{C_p^{(2)}(t)} ,
\end{equation}
where we defined $C_p^{(0)}(t) \equiv -\mathrm{i}\,\varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA} t$. This is $\mathrm{G_0W_0{+}C}$.
\subsubsection{Self-consistent resummation}
Looking at \ref{eq:cp2}, we see that the phase in the memory kernel of $C_p^{(2)}(t)$ is
$e^{-C^0_p(\tau)+C^0_q(\tau)-C^0_q(\tau')+C^0_p(\tau')}$. Taking $C^0\to C$ affects the partial resummation:
\begin{equation}
C_p(t) = -\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA} t - \mathrm{i}\delta_p t
  - \mathrm{i}\sum_{q\nu} W_{pq\nu}^2 \int_0^t \mathrm{d}\tau \int_0^\tau \mathrm{d}\tau'\,
  D^{(q)}_\nu(\tau-\tau')\, e^{-C_p(\tau)+C_q(\tau)-C_q(\tau')+C_p(\tau')} .
\end{equation}
\subsubsection{Equations of motion}
Differentiating, and using $\mathrm{d}G_p^R/\mathrm{d}t = G_p^R\,\mathrm{d}C_p/\mathrm{d}t$ since
$e^{C_p(t)} = \mathrm{i}\,G_p^R(t)$ for $t>0$, gives
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}C_p(t)}{\mathrm{d}t} &= -\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA} - \mathrm{i}\delta_p
  - \mathrm{i}\sum_{q\nu} W_{pq\nu}^2 \int_0^t \mathrm{d}\tau\,
  D^{(q)}_\nu(t-\tau)\, e^{-C_p(t)+C_q(t)-C_q(\tau)+C_p(\tau)} \\
\implies \frac{\mathrm{d}G_p^R(t)}{\mathrm{d}t} &= -\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_p^{\rm HF} G_p^R(t)
  - \mathrm{i}\sum_{q\nu} W_{pq\nu}^2 \int_0^t \mathrm{d}\tau\,
  D^{(q)}_\nu(t-\tau)\,\frac{G_p^R(\tau)\,G_q^R(t)}{G_q^R(\tau)},
  \qquad G_p^R(0^+)=-\mathrm{i} .
\end{align}
Let $y_p(t) \equiv e^{C_p(t)+\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA} t} \equiv e^{\tilde C_p(t)}$, so that
$G_p^R(t) = -\mathrm{i}\,\Theta(t)\, e^{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA} t}\, y_p(t)$. Then
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}G_p^R(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}
  &= -\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA} G_p^R(t) - \mathrm{i}\, e^{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA} t}\,\dot y_p(t), \\
\frac{G_p^R(\tau)\,G_q^R(t)}{G_q^R(\tau)}
  &= -\mathrm{i}\, e^{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA} \tau}\, e^{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_q^{\rm LDA} (t-\tau)}\,
     \frac{y_p(\tau)\,y_q(t)}{y_q(\tau)} .
\end{align}
Inserting both identities into the equation of motion, using $\varepsilon_p^{\rm HF}-\varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA}=\delta_p$, and dividing through by
$-\mathrm{i}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA} t}$, gives
\begin{equation}
\dot y_p(t) = -\mathrm{i}\delta_p\, y_p(t)
  - \mathrm{i}\sum_{q\nu} W_{pq\nu}^2 \int_0^t \mathrm{d}\tau\, D^{(q)}_\nu(t-\tau)\,
  e^{\mathrm{i}(\varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA}-\varepsilon_q^{\rm LDA})(t-\tau)}\,
  \frac{y_p(\tau)\,y_q(t)}{y_q(\tau)}, \qquad y_p(0)=1 .
\end{equation}
\begin{tcolorbox}
Writing
$\frac{y_p(\tau)\,y_q(t)}{y_q(\tau)} = y_p(t)\,
e^{-\tilde C_p(t)+\tilde C_q(t)-\tilde C_q(\tau)+\tilde C_p(\tau)}$ and setting $\tilde C\to0$ in the exponent,
\begin{equation}
\dot y_p(t) = y_p(t)\left[-\mathrm{i}\delta_p + \dot C_p^{(2)}(t)\right]
\implies y_p(t) = e^{C_p^{(1)}(t)+C_p^{(2)}(t)},
\end{equation}
which is $\mathrm{G_0W_0{+}C}$@LDA.
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsubsection{The form to implement numerically}
Define $\bar y_p(t) \equiv e^{\mathrm{i}\delta_p t}\, y_p(t) = e^{C_p(t)+\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_p^{\rm HF} t}$, so that
$G_p^R(t) = -\mathrm{i}\,\Theta(t)\, e^{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_p^{\rm HF} t}\, \bar y_p(t)$ and
\begin{align}
\dot{\bar y}_p(t) &= e^{\mathrm{i}\delta_p t}\left[\dot y_p(t) + \mathrm{i}\delta_p y_p(t)\right] \nonumber\\
&= -\mathrm{i}\sum_{q\nu} W_{pq\nu}^2 \int_0^t \mathrm{d}\tau\, D^{(q)}_\nu(t-\tau)\,
   e^{\mathrm{i}\delta_p t}\,e^{\mathrm{i}(\varepsilon_p^{\rm LDA}-\varepsilon_q^{\rm LDA})(t-\tau)}\,
   \frac{y_p(\tau)\,y_q(t)}{y_q(\tau)} \nonumber\\
&= -\mathrm{i}\sum_{q\nu} W_{pq\nu}^2 \int_0^t \mathrm{d}\tau\, D^{(q)}_\nu(t-\tau)\,
   e^{\mathrm{i}(\varepsilon_p^{\rm HF}-\varepsilon_q^{\rm HF})(t-\tau)}\,
   e^{\mathrm{i}\delta_p\tau+\mathrm{i}\delta_q(t-\tau)}\,
   \frac{y_p(\tau)\,y_q(t)}{y_q(\tau)} \nonumber\\
&= -\mathrm{i}\sum_{q\nu} W_{pq\nu}^2 \int_0^t \mathrm{d}\tau\, D^{(q)}_\nu(t-\tau)\,
   e^{\mathrm{i}(\varepsilon_p^{\rm HF}-\varepsilon_q^{\rm HF})(t-\tau)}\,
   \frac{\bar y_p(\tau)\,\bar y_q(t)}{\bar y_q(\tau)},
   \qquad \bar y_p(0)=1,
\end{align}
where we used
$\frac{y_p(\tau)\,y_q(t)}{y_q(\tau)} = e^{-\mathrm{i}\delta_p\tau-\mathrm{i}\delta_q(t-\tau)}
\frac{\bar y_p(\tau)\,\bar y_q(t)}{\bar y_q(\tau)}$.
We consider the electronic Hamiltonian with the traditional partitioning as
\begin{align}
    \hat H
    &=
    \hat H_0+\hat V\\
    &=
    \sum_p
    \epsilon_p\,
    \underbrace{\{a_p^\dagger a_p\}}_{\hat n_p} +
    \frac{1}{4}
    \sum_{pqrs}
    \overline{v}_{pqrs}\,
    \{a_p^\dagger a_q^\dagger a_s a_r\},
\end{align}
with $
    \overline{v}_{pqrs}
    \equiv
    \langle pq \Vert rs\rangle.
$
Introducing a set of time-dependent scalar
functions $\{\phi_p(t)\}$ satisfying $
    \phi_p(0)=0$, we can repartition the electronic Hamiltonian according to
\begin{equation}
    \hat H
    =
    \hat H_0^\phi(t)
    +
    \hat V^\phi(t),
\label{eq:repartitioning}
\end{equation}
with $
    \hat H_0^\phi(t)
    =
    \hat H_0
    +
    \sum_p
    \dot{\phi}_p(t)\,
    \hat n_p
$
and $
    \hat V^\phi(t)
    =
    \hat V
    -
    \sum_p
    \dot{\phi}_p(t)\,
    \hat n_p.
$
The evolution operator generated by the new reference Hamiltonian
is
\begin{align}
    \hat U_0^\phi(t,0)
    &=
    T\exp\left[
    -i
    \int_0^t
    d\tau\,
    \hat H_0^\phi(\tau)
    \right] \\
    &=
    \exp\left[
    -i\hat H_0 t
    -i\sum_p\phi_p(t)\hat n_p
    \right]
    \nonumber\\
    &=
    e^{-i\hat H_0 t}
    e^{-i\hat\Phi(t)}.
\end{align}
\subsection{Particle Green's function in the interaction picture}
The time-ordered Green's function for the addition of an electron into state $p$, with $t>0$, is:
\begin{align}
    G_c(t)
    &=
    -i\Theta(t)
    \frac{
    \Tr\left[
    \hat\rho_0\,
    T\left\{
    a_{c,I}^\phi(t)
    a_{c,I}^{\phi\dagger}(0)
    \exp\left[
    -i\int_0^t d\tau\,
    \hat V_{\phi,I}(\tau)
    \right]
    \right\}
    \right]
    }{
    \Tr\left[
    \hat\rho_0\,
    T
    \exp\left[
    -i\int_0^t d\tau\,
    \hat V_{\phi,I}(\tau)
    \right]
    \right]
    }\\
\overset{T=0}{=}&
    -i\Theta(t)
    \frac{\langle\Phi_0|     a_{c,I}^\phi(t)
    T\left\{
    \exp\left[
    -i\int_0^t d\tau\,
    \hat V_{\phi,I}(\tau)
    \right]
    \right\}    a_{c,I}^{\phi\dagger}(0)|\Phi_0\rangle
    }{
    \langle\Phi_0|
    T
    \exp\left[
    -i\int_0^t d\tau\,
    \hat V_{\phi,I}(\tau)
    \right]
    |\Phi_0\rangle
    }
    \label{eq:fermionic_green_phi_trace}
\end{align}
with the interaction-picture operators $
    \hat O_I^\phi(t)
    =
    \hat U_0^{\phi\dagger}(t,0)\,
    \hat O\,
    \hat U_0^\phi(t,0).$

\subsubsection{Interaction-picture fermionic operators}
Since
\begin{align}
    a_{c,I}^\phi(t)
    &=
    \hat U_0^{\phi\dagger}(t,0)
    a_c
    \hat U_0^\phi(t,0)
    \nonumber\\
    &=
    e^{-i\epsilon_c  t}
    e^{-i\phi_c(t)}
    a_c,
    \label{eq:a_phi_interaction}
\end{align}
while
\begin{align}
    a_{c,I}^{\phi\dagger}(t)
    &=
    e^{+i\epsilon_c  t}
    e^{+i\phi_c(t)}
    a_c^\dagger\\
\implies
    a_{c,I}^{\phi\dagger}(0)
    &=
    a_c^\dagger.
    \label{eq:adag_phi_interaction}
\end{align}
Substituting in yields:
\begin{align}
    G_c(t)
    &=
    -i\Theta(t)
    e^{-i\epsilon_c  t}
    e^{-i\phi_c(t)}
    \underbrace{\frac{\langle\Phi_0| a_c
    T\left\{
    \exp\left[
    -i\int_0^t d\tau\,
    \hat V_{\phi,I}(\tau)
    \right]
    \right\}a_c^\dagger|\Phi_0\rangle
    }{
    \langle\Phi_0|
    T
    \exp\left[
    -i\int_0^t d\tau\,
    \hat V_{\phi,I}(\tau)
    \right]
    |\Phi_0\rangle
    }}_{y_c^\phi(t)}
    \label{eq:G_phi_factored}
\end{align}
So defining the bare propagator $
    G_c^{(0)}(t)
    =
    -i\Theta(t)e^{-i\epsilon_c  t},
$
we can write
\begin{equation}
    {
    G_c(t)
    =
    G_c^{(0)}(t)
    e^{-i\phi_c(t)}
    y_c^\phi(t).
    }
\end{equation}
Comparing to the traditional parametrization of the interacting Green's function by a cumulant,
\begin{align}
    G_c(t)
    &=
    G_c^{(0)}(t)e^{C_c(t)}\\
\implies    e^{C_c(t)}
    &=
    e^{-i\phi_c(t)}
    y_c^\phi(t)\\
\implies    C_c(t)
    &=
    -i\phi_c(t)
    +
    \ln y_c^\phi(t).
    \label{eq:C_phi_y}
\end{align}
\subsubsection{Expansion of the normalized interaction functional}

Defining
\begin{equation}
    \hat S_\phi(t)
    \equiv
    -i\int_0^t d\tau\,
    \hat V_{\phi,I}(\tau),
\end{equation}
we have
\begin{align}
    y_c^\phi(t)
    &=
    \frac{
    \langle\Phi_0|
    a_c\,
    T e^{\hat S_\phi(t)}
    a_c^\dagger
    |\Phi_0\rangle
    }{
    \langle\Phi_0|
    T e^{\hat S_\phi(t)}
    |\Phi_0\rangle
    } \equiv \frac{\mathcal N_c^\phi(t)}
         {\mathcal D^\phi(t)}
% \\
% \implies     \ln y_c^\phi(t)
%     &=
%     \left[\ln y_c^\phi(t)\right]_{K}^{(1)}
%     +
%     \left[\ln y_c^\phi(t)\right]_{WW}^{(2)}
%     +
%     \mathcal O(\lambda^3).
%     % \equiv
%     % \frac{\mathcal N_p^\phi(t)}
%     %      {\mathcal D^\phi(t)}.
\end{align}
Associating the coupling parameter $\lambda$ with the
residual two-body correlation interaction (the
first-order static contribution has already been absorbed into the
HF reference Hamiltonian), the correlation cumulant begins at
second order,
\begin{equation}
    C_c(t)
    =
    \lambda^2 C_c^{(2)}(t)
    +
    \mathcal O(\lambda^3).
\end{equation}
Since the self-consistent choice in \ref{eq:C_phi_y} will take the phase to have
the same perturbative order as the correlation cumulant, we assign $
    \phi_c(t)=\mathcal O(\lambda^2) \implies
    \dot{\phi}_c(t)=\mathcal O(\lambda^2)$.

The full residual interaction in the $\phi$-interaction picture is
\begin{align}
    \hat V_{\phi,I}(\tau)
    &=
    \hat U_0^{\phi\dagger}(\tau,0)
    \hat V^\phi(\tau)
    \hat U_0^\phi(\tau,0)
    \nonumber\\
    &=
    \lambda
    \hat U_0^{\phi\dagger}(\tau,0)
    \hat V
    \hat U_0^\phi(\tau,0)
    -
    \lambda^2
    \sum_r
    \dot{\phi}_r(\tau)\hat n_r
    \nonumber\\
    &\equiv
    \lambda\hat W_{\phi,I}(\tau)
    -
    \lambda^2\hat K_\phi(\tau).
    \label{eq:Vphi_WK}\\
\implies    \hat S_\phi(t)
    &=
    -i\int_0^t d\tau\,
    \hat V_{\phi,I}(\tau)
    \nonumber\\
    &=
    \lambda
    \underbrace{
    \left[
    -i\int_0^t d\tau\,
    \hat W_{\phi,I}(\tau)
    \right]
    }_{\hat S_W(t)}
    +
    \lambda^2
    \underbrace{
    \left[
    +i\int_0^t d\tau\,
    \hat K_\phi(\tau)
    \right]
    }_{\hat S_K(t)}\\
\implies    T e^{\hat S_\phi(t)}
    &=
    1
    +
    \lambda\hat S_W(t)
    +
    \lambda^2
    \left[
        \hat S_K(t)
        +
        \frac{1}{2}
        T\hat S_W^2(t)
    \right]
    +
    \mathcal O(\lambda^3)\\
\implies
    \mathcal N_c^\phi(t)
    &=
    \langle
    a_c a_c^\dagger
    \rangle_0
    +
    \lambda \underbrace{
    \langle
    a_c\hat S_W a_c^\dagger
    \rangle_0}_{N_1}+
    \lambda^2 \underbrace{
    \left[
        \langle
        a_c\hat S_K a_c^\dagger
        \rangle_0
        +
        \frac{1}{2}
        \langle
        a_c T\hat S_W^2 a_c^\dagger
        \rangle_0
    \right]}_{N_2}
    +
    \mathcal O(\lambda^3)\\
\implies
    \mathcal D^\phi(t)
    &=
    1
    +
    \lambda \underbrace{
        \langle\hat S_W\rangle_0
    }_{D_1}+
    \lambda^2 \underbrace{
    \left[
        \langle\hat S_K\rangle_0
        +
        \frac{1}{2}
        \langle T\hat S_W^2\rangle_0
    \right]}_{D_2}
    +
    \mathcal O(\lambda^3)\\
\implies \frac{1}{\mathcal D^\phi}
&=
\frac{1}{
1+\lambda D_1+\lambda^2D_2
}
\nonumber\\
&=
1-\lambda D_1
+\lambda^2\left(D_1^2-D_2\right)
+\mathcal O(\lambda^3),
\\[1mm]
\implies y_c^\phi
&=
\frac{\mathcal N_c^\phi}{\mathcal D^\phi}
\nonumber\\
&=
\left(
1+\lambda N_1+\lambda^2N_2
\right)
\left[
1-\lambda D_1
+\lambda^2(D_1^2-D_2)
\right]
+\mathcal O(\lambda^3)
\nonumber\\
&=
1
+\lambda(N_1-D_1)
+\lambda^2
\left[
N_2-D_2-N_1D_1+D_1^2
\right]
+\mathcal O(\lambda^3)
\nonumber\\
&=
1+\lambda^2
\Bigg[\underbrace{{
\langle a_c\hat S_Ka_c^\dagger\rangle_0
-\langle\hat S_K\rangle_0
}}_{\left[ y_c^\phi(t)\right]_{K}^{(1)}}
+
\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}
{
\left(
\langle a_cT\hat S_W^2a_c^\dagger\rangle_0
-\langle T\hat S_W^2\rangle_0
\right)
}}_{\left[y_c^\phi(t)\right]_{WW}^{(2)}}
\Bigg]
+\mathcal O(\lambda^3),
\end{align}
where we used that for a normal-ordered residual correlation interaction $\langle\hat S_W\rangle_0=0$, $\langle a_c\hat S_Wa_c^\dagger\rangle_0=0$ (there is no contribution of order $\lambda$), and $\langle a_ca_c^\dagger\rangle_0=1$ for $c\in\mathcal V$. This 
implies that
\begin{align}
    \ln y_c^\phi(t)
    &=
    \left[\ln y_c^\phi(t)\right]_{K}^{(1)}
    +
    \left[\ln y_c^\phi(t)\right]_{WW}^{(2)}
    +
    \mathcal O(\lambda^3)\end{align}
with
\begin{align}
    \left[\ln y_c^\phi(t)\right]_{K}^{(1)}
    &=
    i\int_0^t d\tau\,
    \sum_r\dot{\phi}_r(\tau)
    \Big[
        \langle
        a_c\hat n_r a_c^\dagger
        \rangle_0
        -
        \langle
        a_ca_c^\dagger
        \rangle_0
        \langle\hat n_r\rangle_0
    \Big]\\
&\overset{c\in\mathcal V}{=}
    i\int_0^t d\tau\,
    \dot{\phi}_c(\tau) \\
&= i\phi_c(t),
\end{align}
% For $c\in\mathcal V$,
% \begin{equation}
%     \langle
%     a_c\hat n_r a_c^\dagger
%     \rangle_0
%     -
%     \langle
%     a_ca_c^\dagger
%     \rangle_0
%     \langle\hat n_r\rangle_0
%     =
%     \delta_{cr},
% \end{equation}
Thus, the explicit auxiliary phase cancels from our definition of the exact cumulant in equation \eqref{eq:C_phi_y} and so, through second order,
\begin{equation}
    {
    C_c(t)
    =
    \left[\ln y_c^\phi(t)\right]_{WW}^{(2)}
    +
    \mathcal O(\lambda^3).
    }
    \label{eq:C_equals_WW_second}
\end{equation}
So now we are left to evaluate
\begin{align}
    \left[\ln y_c^\phi(t)\right]_{WW}^{(2)}
    &=
    \frac{(-i)^2}{2}
    \int_0^t d\tau_1
    \int_0^t d\tau_2
    \nonumber\\
    &\quad\times
    \Big[
        \langle
        a_c
        T\{
        \hat W_{\phi,I}(\tau_1)
        \hat W_{\phi,I}(\tau_2)
        \}
        a_c^\dagger
        \rangle_0 -
        \langle a_ca_c^\dagger\rangle_0
        \langle
        T\{
        \hat W_{\phi,I}(\tau_1)
        \hat W_{\phi,I}(\tau_2)
        \}
        \rangle_0
    \Big]\\
&    =
    -
    \int_0^t d\tau
    \int_0^\tau d\tau'\,
    \left\langle
    a_c
    \hat W_{\phi,I}(\tau)
    \hat W_{\phi,I}(\tau')
    a_c^\dagger
    \right\rangle_{0,c,c}.
\end{align}
with 0,c,c standing for the connected contractions with respect to the reference state $|\Phi_0\rangle$ and with the external creation and annihilation operators $a_c^\dagger$ and $a_c$.
\subsubsection{Second-order linked contractions}
Simplifying the $\phi$-interaction picture two-body interaction gives:
% \begin{equation}
%     \hat V
%     =
%     \frac{1}{4}
%     \sum_{pqrs}
%     \overline v_{pqrs}
%     a_p^\dagger a_q^\dagger a_s a_r,
%     \qquad
%     \overline v_{pqrs}
%     \equiv
%     \langle pq\Vert rs\rangle .
% \end{equation}
% In the ,
\begin{align}
\hat W_{\phi,I}(\tau)
&=
\hat U_0^{\phi\dagger}(\tau,0)\,
\hat V\,
\hat U_0^\phi(\tau,0)\\
&=
e^{+i\hat\Phi(\tau)}
e^{+i\hat H_0\tau}
\left[
\frac{1}{4}
\sum_{pqrs}
\overline v_{pqrs}\,
a_p^\dagger a_q^\dagger a_s a_r
\right]
e^{-i\hat H_0\tau}
e^{-i\hat\Phi(\tau)}\\
&=
\frac{1}{4}
\sum_{pqrs}
\overline v_{pqrs}\,
e^{+i\hat\Phi(\tau)}
\left[
e^{+i\hat H_0\tau}
a_p^\dagger
e^{-i\hat H_0\tau}
\right]
\left[
e^{+i\hat H_0\tau}
a_q^\dagger
e^{-i\hat H_0\tau}
\right]
\left[
e^{+i\hat H_0\tau}
a_s
e^{-i\hat H_0\tau}
\right]
\left[
e^{+i\hat H_0\tau}
a_r
e^{-i\hat H_0\tau}
\right]
e^{-i\hat\Phi(\tau)}\\
&=
\frac{1}{4}
\sum_{pqrs}
\overline v_{pqrs}\,
e^{+i(\epsilon_p+\epsilon_q-\epsilon_s-\epsilon_r)\tau}
e^{+i\hat\Phi(\tau)}
a_p^\dagger a_q^\dagger a_s a_r
e^{-i\hat\Phi(\tau)}\\
&=
\frac{1}{4}
\sum_{pqrs}
\overline v_{pqrs}\,
e^{+i(\epsilon_p+\epsilon_q-\epsilon_r-\epsilon_s)\tau}
\left[
e^{+i\hat\Phi(\tau)}
a_p^\dagger
e^{-i\hat\Phi(\tau)}
\right]
\left[
e^{+i\hat\Phi(\tau)}
a_q^\dagger
e^{-i\hat\Phi(\tau)}
\right]
\left[
e^{+i\hat\Phi(\tau)}
a_s
e^{-i\hat\Phi(\tau)}
\right]
\left[
e^{+i\hat\Phi(\tau)}
a_r
e^{-i\hat\Phi(\tau)}
\right]\\
&=
\frac{1}{4}
\sum_{pqrs}
\overline v_{pqrs}\,
e^{+i(\epsilon_p+\epsilon_q-\epsilon_r-\epsilon_s)\tau}
\left[e^{+i\phi_p(\tau)}a_p^\dagger\right]
\left[e^{+i\phi_q(\tau)}a_q^\dagger\right]
\left[e^{-i\phi_s(\tau)}a_s\right]
\left[e^{-i\phi_r(\tau)}a_r\right]
\\
&={
\frac{1}{4}
\sum_{pqrs}
\overline v_{pqrs}\,
e^{+i(\epsilon_p+\epsilon_q-\epsilon_r-\epsilon_s)\tau}
e^{+i[
\phi_p(\tau)+\phi_q(\tau)
-\phi_r(\tau)-\phi_s(\tau)
]}
a_p^\dagger a_q^\dagger a_s a_r
},
\label{eq:V_phi_second_order}
\end{align} where we have used the result
\begin{align}
e^{+i\hat\Phi(\tau)}
a_p^\dagger
e^{-i\hat\Phi(\tau)}
&=
a_p^\dagger
+
\left[
i\hat\Phi(\tau),a_p^\dagger
\right]
+
\frac{1}{2!}
\left[
i\hat\Phi(\tau),
\left[
i\hat\Phi(\tau),a_p^\dagger
\right]
\right]
+\cdots\\
&=
a_p^\dagger
+
i\phi_p(\tau)a_p^\dagger
+
\frac{1}{2!}
\left[i\phi_p(\tau)\right]^2a_p^\dagger
+
\frac{1}{3!}
\left[i\phi_p(\tau)\right]^3a_p^\dagger
+\cdots\\
&=
\left[
1
+i\phi_p(\tau)
+\frac{\left[i\phi_p(\tau)\right]^2}{2!}
+\frac{\left[i\phi_p(\tau)\right]^3}{3!}
+\cdots
\right]
a_p^\dagger\\
&=
{
e^{+i\phi_p(\tau)}a_p^\dagger
}
\label{eq:phi_creation_similarity}
\end{align}
with an analogous one holding for the annihilation operator. Since we are considering the particle-addition
propagator, the connected contributions are obtained via propagation through the intermediate
$2p1h$ configuration, so for $\tau>\tau'$, we have
\begin{align}
    \left\langle a_c
    T\left\{
    \hat W_{\phi,I}(\tau)
    \hat W_{\phi,I}(\tau')
    \right\} a_c^\dagger
    \right\rangle_{0}&\propto
\frac{1}{16}
\sum_{qrst}
\sum_{uvwx}
\bar v_{qrst}\bar v_{uvwx}
\left\langle
a_c
a_q^\dagger a_r^\dagger a_t a_s
a_u^\dagger a_v^\dagger a_x a_w
a_c^\dagger
\right\rangle_0
\\
&\overset{2p1h}{=}
\frac{1}{16}
\sum_{\substack{i,i'\in\mathcal O\\
a,a',b,b'\in\mathcal V}}
\Bigg[
%
\bar v_{ciab}\bar v_{a'b'ci'}
\left\langle
a_c
a_c^\dagger a_i^\dagger a_b a_a
a_{a'}^\dagger a_{b'}^\dagger a_{i'} a_c
a_c^\dagger
\right\rangle_0
\nonumber\\
&\qquad+
\bar v_{icab}\bar v_{a'b'ci'}
\left\langle
a_c
a_i^\dagger a_c^\dagger a_b a_a
a_{a'}^\dagger a_{b'}^\dagger a_{i'} a_c
a_c^\dagger
\right\rangle_0
\nonumber\\
&\qquad+
\bar v_{ciab}\bar v_{a'b'ic}
\left\langle
a_c
a_c^\dagger a_i^\dagger a_b a_a
a_{a'}^\dagger a_{b'}^\dagger a_c a_{i'}
a_c^\dagger
\right\rangle_0
\nonumber\\
&\qquad+
\bar v_{icab}\bar v_{a'b'ic}
\left\langle
a_c
a_i^\dagger a_c^\dagger a_b a_a
a_{a'}^\dagger a_{b'}^\dagger a_c a_{i'}
a_c^\dagger
\right\rangle_0
\Bigg]\\
&{=}
\frac{4}{16}
\sum_{\substack{i,i'\in\mathcal O\\
                 a,a',b,b'\in\mathcal V}}
\bar v_{ciab}\bar v_{a'b'ci'}
\Bigg[
\wick{
\c1 a_c
\c1 a_c^\dagger
\c2 a_i^\dagger
\c3 a_b
\c4 a_a
\c4 a_{a'}^\dagger
\c3 a_{b'}^\dagger
\c2 a_{i'}
\c5 a_c
\c5 a_c^\dagger
}+
\wick{
\c1 a_c
\c1 a_c^\dagger
\c2 a_i^\dagger
\c3 a_b
\c4 a_a
\c3 a_{a'}^\dagger
\c4 a_{b'}^\dagger
\c2 a_{i'}
\c5 a_c
\c5 a_c^\dagger
}
\Bigg]\\
\\
&=
\frac{4}{16}
\sum_{iab}
\bar v_{ciab}
\left(
\bar v_{abci}-\bar v_{baci}
\right)
\\
&=
\frac{8}{16}
\sum_{iab}
\bar v_{ciab}\bar v_{abci}
\\
&=
{
\frac{1}{2}
\sum_{iab}
\left|\langle ab\Vert ci\rangle\right|^2
}.
\end{align}
In summary, we obtain
\begin{align}
    &
    \left\langle a_c
    T\left\{
    \hat W_{\phi,I}(\tau)
    \hat W_{\phi,I}(\tau')
    \right\} a_c^\dagger
    \right\rangle_{0,c,c}
    =
    \frac{1}{2}
    \sum_{i\in\mathcal O}
    \sum_{a,b\in\mathcal V}
    \left|
    \langle ab\Vert ci\rangle
    \right|^2
    \,
    \mathcal P_{cabi}(\tau,\tau'),
    \qquad
    \tau>\tau',
    \label{eq:wick_2p1h_result}
\end{align}
where $\mathcal P_{cabi}$ contains the time-dependent one-particle
phases generated by the two vertices.
\subsubsection{Time-dependent one-particle
phases}
Based on equation \ref{eq:V_phi_second_order}, we know that at the earlier time $\tau'$, the transition $
    p+i\longrightarrow a+b$
carries the phase
\begin{align}
    &
    \exp\Big\{
    +i[
        \epsilon_a+\epsilon_b-\epsilon_c-\epsilon_i
    ]\tau'
    \Big\}
    \exp\Big\{
    +i[
        \phi_a(\tau')+\phi_b(\tau')
        -\phi_c(\tau')-\phi_i(\tau')
    ]
    \Big\},
\end{align}
while at the later time $\tau$, the inverse transition $
    a+b\longrightarrow p+i
$
carries
\begin{align}
    &
    \exp\Big\{
    -i[
        \epsilon_a+\epsilon_b-\epsilon_c-\epsilon_i
    ]\tau
    \Big\}
    \exp\Big\{
    -i[
        \phi_a(\tau)+\phi_b(\tau)
        -\phi_c(\tau)-\phi_i(\tau)
    ]
    \Big\}.
\end{align}
Their product is therefore
\begin{align}
    \mathcal P_{cabi}(\tau,\tau')
    &=
    \exp\left[
    -i\Delta_{cabi}^{(+)}(\tau-\tau')
    \right]
    \exp\Big\{
    -i[
        \phi_a(\tau)-\phi_a(\tau')
        +
        \phi_b(\tau)-\phi_b(\tau')
        \nonumber\\
    &\qquad\qquad
        -
        \phi_c(\tau)+\phi_c(\tau')
        -
        \phi_i(\tau)+\phi_i(\tau')
    ]
    \Big\},
    \label{eq:P_pabi}
\end{align}
where
\begin{equation}
    {
    \Delta_{cabi}^{(+)}
    \equiv
    \epsilon_a+\epsilon_b-\epsilon_i-\epsilon_c.
    }
\end{equation}
So we are left with 
\begin{align}
    C^{(2)}_c(t)=\ln y_c^\phi(t)
    &=
    -
    \frac{1}{2}
    \sum_{i\in\mathcal O}
    \sum_{a,b\in\mathcal V}
    \left|
    \langle ab\Vert ci\rangle
    \right|^2
    \int_0^t d\tau
    \int_0^\tau d\tau'
    e^{-i\Delta_{cabi}^{(+)}(\tau-\tau')}
    \nonumber\\
    &\qquad\times
    \exp\Big\{
    -i[
        \phi_a(\tau)-\phi_a(\tau')
        +
        \phi_b(\tau)-\phi_b(\tau')
        -
        \phi_c(\tau)+\phi_c(\tau')
        -
        \phi_i(\tau)+\phi_i(\tau')
    ]
    \Big\}.
    \label{eq:lny_particle_2p1h}
\end{align}
Looking back to the equation \ref{eq:C_phi_y}, we see that the self-consistent choice is $C_c(t)=-i\phi_c(t)$.
\subsection{Hole Green's function in the interaction picture}
Here we are interested in solving for 
\begin{align}
    G_k(t)
    &=
    +i\Theta(t)
    \langle
    a_k^\dagger(t)a_k(0)
    \rangle_0 \\
    &= G_k^{(0)}(t)
    e^{+i\phi_k(t)}
    y_k^{\phi}(t),
\end{align}
where $G_k^{(0)}(t)=+i\Theta(t)e^{+i\epsilon_k t}$ is the bare hole propagator. Comparing with the cumulant ansatz yields
\begin{equation}
    {
    C_k(t)
    =
    +i\phi_k(t)
    +
    \ln y_k^{\phi}(t)
    }.
\end{equation}
The ensuing derivation is similar to the one we did previously, with the exception that now our intermediate configuration is $2h1p$. The final result is
\begin{align}
    C^{(2)}_k(t)
    &=
    -
    \frac{1}{2}
    \sum_{a\in\mathcal V}
    \sum_{i,j\in\mathcal O}
    \left|
    \langle ka\Vert ij\rangle
    \right|^2
    \int_0^t d\tau
    \int_0^\tau d\tau'
    e^{-i\Delta_{kija}^{(-)}(\tau-\tau')}
    \nonumber\\
    &\qquad\times
    \exp\Big\{
    -i[
        \phi_i(\tau)-\phi_i(\tau')
        +
        \phi_j(\tau)-\phi_j(\tau')
        -
        \phi_k(\tau)+\phi_k(\tau')
        -
        \phi_a(\tau)+\phi_a(\tau')
    ]
    \Big\},
\end{align}
where $\Delta_{kija}^{(-)}=\epsilon_k+\epsilon_a-\epsilon_i-\epsilon_j$. Now, the self-consistent choice is $C_k(t)=+i\phi_k(t)$. $c\in\mathcal V$ and $k\in\mathcal O$ are particle and hole indices, respectively.
\subsection{BWs2-partitioned self-consistent cumulant}
In the spin-restricted formalism,
we define the static BWs2 shift as
\begin{equation}
\Lambda_p
=
\begin{cases}
\alpha_1 W_p^{O}, & p\in\mathcal O,\\[1mm]
\alpha_2 W_p^{V}, & p\in\mathcal V,
\end{cases}
\qquad
\tilde{\epsilon}_p
=
\epsilon_p+\Lambda_p.
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align}
{W}^{O}_{ii} &= \frac{1}{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{ii}^{(2),2p1h}(\omega = \tilde{\varepsilon}_i)= \frac{1}{2}\sum_{a b j} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{ab}}{\tilde{\varepsilon}_i + \tilde{\varepsilon}_j - \tilde{\varepsilon}_a - \tilde{\varepsilon}_b},\\
\mathbf{W}^{V}_{aa} &= \frac{1}{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{aa}^{(2),2h1p}(\omega = \tilde{\varepsilon}_a)= \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i j b} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{ab}}{\tilde{\varepsilon}_a + \tilde{\varepsilon}_b - \tilde{\varepsilon}_i - \tilde{\varepsilon}_j},
\end{align}
where $\tilde{\varepsilon}$ is the BWs2 ground-state MO energy and we defined $\mathcal K_{pq}^{rs} =
(pr|qs)
\left[
2(pr|qs)-(ps|qr)
\right]$.
The Hamiltonian is partitioned as
\begin{align}
\hat H
&=
\hat H_0^{\rm BW}
+
\hat V^{\rm BW},\\
\hat H_0^{\rm BW}
&=
\sum_p
\tilde{\epsilon}_p\hat n_p,\\
\hat V^{\rm BW}
&=
\hat V
-
\sum_p
\Lambda_p\hat n_p .
\end{align}
% Introducing the auxiliary phases $\phi_p(t)$ gives
% \begin{align}
% \hat H_0^{{\rm BW},\phi}(t)
% &=
% \hat H_0^{\rm BW}
% +
% \sum_p\dot{\phi}_p(t)\hat n_p,\\
% \hat V^{{\rm BW},\phi}(t)
% &=
% \hat V
% -
% \sum_p\Lambda_p\hat n_p
% -
% \sum_p\dot{\phi}_p(t)\hat n_p .
% \end{align}
% Counting $
% \hat V=\mathcal O(\lambda)$, $
% \Lambda_p=\mathcal O(\lambda^2)$, and $
% \phi_p=\mathcal O(\lambda^2)$, suggests that we can treat the BWs2 and auxiliary one-body counterterms the same way. So after the cancellation of the explicit auxiliary phase, we have
% \begin{align}
% C_c^+(t)
% &=
% +i\Lambda_c t
% +
% \left[\ln y_c^\phi(t)\right]_{WW}^{(2)},\\
% C_k^-(t)
% &=
% -i\Lambda_k t
% +
% \left[\ln y_k^\phi(t)\right]_{WW}^{(2)}.
% \end{align}
Thus, the end result for the coupled VIDEs
\begin{align}
\dot C_c^+(t)
&=
+i\Lambda_c
-
\sum_{iab}
\mathcal{K}_{ci}^{ab}
\int_0^t d\tau\,
e^{-i\widetilde{\Delta}_{cabi}^{(+)}(t-\tau)}
\nonumber\\
&\qquad\times
\exp\left[
-\delta C_c^+
+\delta C_a^+
+\delta C_b^+
+\delta C_i^-
\right],
\\[2mm]
\dot C_k^-(t)
&=
-i\Lambda_k
+
\sum_{ija}
\mathcal{K}_{ij}^{pa}
\int_0^t d\tau\,
e^{-i\widetilde{\Delta}_{kija}^{(-)}(t-\tau)}
\nonumber\\
&\qquad\times
\exp\left[
-\delta C_k^-
+\delta C_i^-
+\delta C_j^-
+\delta C_a^+
\right],
\end{align}
with $\delta C_q\equiv C_q(t)-C_q(\tau)$, $\widetilde{\Delta}_{cabi}^{(+)}=\tilde{\epsilon}_a+\tilde{\epsilon}_b-\tilde{\epsilon}_c-\tilde{\epsilon}_i$, and $\widetilde{\Delta}_{kija}^{(-)}=\tilde{\epsilon}_k+\tilde{\epsilon}_a-\tilde{\epsilon}_i-\tilde{\epsilon}_j$. Note that the BWs2 shifts of $\Lambda_c$ and $\Lambda_k$ take the place of the auxiliary phases $\phi_c$ and $\phi_k$ in the VIDEs; this is to be expected, though, since we're just doing another repartitioning of the Hamiltonian, just as we did when we introduced the auxiliary phases in \ref{eq:repartitioning}. 
\subsection{Coupled VIDEs}
Putting the results together and differentiating once gives 
% $\begin{aligned} & \dot{C}_p^{+}(t)=-\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i a b}|\langle a b \| p i\rangle|^2 \int_0^t d \tau e^{-i \Delta_{p o b i}^{+}(t-\tau)} e^{-\delta C_p^{+}+\delta C_a^{+}+\delta C_b^{+}+\delta C_i^{-}} \\ & \dot{C}_p^{-}(t)=-\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i j a}|\langle p a \| i j\rangle|^2 \int_0^t d \tau e^{-i \Delta_{p j a}^{-}(t-\tau)} e^{-\delta C_p^{-}+\delta C_i^{-}+\delta C_j^{-}+\delta C_a^{+}}\end{aligned}$
\begin{align}
    \dot{C}_c(t) =-\ \sum_{i a b}|\langle a b \| c i\rangle|^2 \int_0^t d \tau e^{-i \Delta_{cab i}^{+}(t-\tau)} e^{-\delta C_c+\delta C_a+\delta C_b+\delta C_i} \\
    \dot{C}_k(t) =-\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i j a}|\langle k a \| i j\rangle|^2 \int_0^t d \tau e^{-i \Delta_{kija}^{-}(t-\tau)} e^{-\delta C_k+\delta C_i+\delta C_j+\delta C_a}
\end{align}
with $\delta C_q\equiv C_q(t)-C_q(\tau)$, $\Delta_{cab i}^{(+)}=\epsilon_a+\epsilon_b-\epsilon_c-\epsilon_i$, and $\Delta_{kija}^{(-)}=\epsilon_k+\epsilon_a-\epsilon_i-\epsilon_j$.
\subsubsection{How to solve them? }
\begin{algorithm}[H]
\caption{Coupled propagation of particle and hole cumulants}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]

\State Define the time grid
\[
t_n=n\Delta t,
\qquad
n=0,\ldots,N_t-1,
\]
and initialize
\[
C_a^+(0)=0,
\qquad
C_i^-(0)=0.
\]

\For{$n=0,\ldots,N_t-2$}

    \State Evaluate all particle and hole memory integrals from the common history
    \[
    \mathbf F_n
    =
    \mathbf F[\mathbf C](t_n)
    =
    \begin{pmatrix}
        \{F_{a,n}^+\}\\
        \{F_{i,n}^-\}
    \end{pmatrix},
    \]
    where
    \[
    F_{a,n}^+
    =
    \dot C_a^+(t_n),
    \qquad
    F_{i,n}^-
    =
    \dot C_i^-(t_n).
    \]

    \State Form a simultaneous predictor
    \[
    \mathbf C_{n+1}^{\rm P}
    =
    \mathbf C_n
    +
    \Delta t\,\mathbf F_n.
    \]

    \State Evaluate the predicted derivative using the history augmented by
    $\mathbf C_{n+1}^{\rm P}$,
    \[
    \mathbf F_{n+1}^{\rm P}
    =
    \mathbf F[
        \mathbf C_0,\ldots,\mathbf C_n,
        \mathbf C_{n+1}^{\rm P}
    ](t_{n+1}).
    \]

    \State Correct all particle and hole cumulants simultaneously,
    \[
    \mathbf C_{n+1}
    =
    \mathbf C_n
    +
    \frac{\Delta t}{2}
    \left(
        \mathbf F_n
        +
        \mathbf F_{n+1}^{\rm P}
    \right).
    \]

\EndFor

\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}
\subsection{Comparing self-consistent cumulants on a GW reference}
\subsubsection{Initial definitions}
We use the retarded convention throughout, so that the Green's function is
\begin{equation}
    G_{0,k}(t)
    =
    -i e^{-i\epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}}t},
    \qquad
    G_k(t)
    =
    G_{0,k}(t)e^{C_k(t)},
\end{equation}
which is applicable for the general state $k$. We assume that the chemical potential can be written as the perturbative correction $\mu = \mu_0 + \delta\mu$, where $\delta\mu$ is the correction from its initial value of $\mu_0=\frac{\left(k^2/2 + \Gamma^1_k\right)_{\rm HOMO}+\left(k^2/2 + \Gamma^1_k\right)_{\rm LUMO}}{2}$. Defining the
 static correction
\begin{equation}
    \Delta_k[\mu_0]
    =
    \Sigma_{k}^x[\mu_0]
    -
    V_{xc,k}^{\mathrm{LDA}},
\end{equation}
with $\Sigma_{k}^x[\mu]=n_{k}[\mu]v_m-\sum_{{q}\neq{0}} n_{{k}+{q}}[\mu]v({q})$, where $n_k[\mu]=\int_0^\mu A_k(\omega)d\omega\implies n_k[\mu_0]=\Theta(k_F-k)$,
we can write the cumulant as
\begin{align}
    C_k(t)
    &=
    -i\Delta_k[\mu_0] t+c_k(t)
% \\
% \implies 
%     \dot C_k(0)&=-i\Delta_k,
\end{align}
where we used $C_k(0)=0$. Our initial formulation assumes a GW self-energy, so the quasibosonic frequencies $\Omega_\nu$ and couplings $g_{pq\nu}$ are also
held fixed. It can be shown that both schemes described below reduce to the one-shot G0W0+C result with $c_k(t)=0$ in the initial iteration. Because we are linearizing the equations, this means that we can solve the forward time integration approximating $\mu \approx \mu_0$ and then just add a perturbative correction at the very end. 
% Also, define
% \begin{equation}
%     D_{ki\nu}^{\mathrm{LDA}}
%     =
%     \epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}}
%     -
%     \epsilon_i^{\mathrm{LDA}}
%     +
%     \Omega_\nu.
% \end{equation}
\subsubsection{Holms' scheme}
If we assume that the chemical potential stays fixed, which he does NOT do, then we can write EOMs in the time-domain. For the state $j\in\mathcal O$ below the Fermi level, we have
\begin{align}
    \dot G^{\mathrm H}_j(t)
    &=
-i\bigl(\epsilon_j^{\mathrm{LDA}}+\Delta_j[\mu_0]\bigr)G^{\mathrm H}_j(t)
-\sum_{j\in\mathcal O,\nu}|g_{ji\nu}|^2
\int_0^t ds\,e^{i\Omega_\nu s}
\frac{G^{\mathrm H}_i(s)G^{\mathrm H}_j(t)}{G_{0,j}(s)}\\
&- \sum_{a\in\mathcal V,\nu}|g_{ja\nu}|^2
\int_0^t ds\,e^{i\Omega_\nu s}
\frac{G^{\mathrm H}_a(s)G^{\mathrm H}_j(t)}{G_{0,j}(s)},
\end{align}
while for the state $a\in\mathcal V$ above the Fermi level, we have
\begin{align}
    \dot G^{\mathrm H}_a(t)
    &=
-i\bigl(\epsilon_a^{\mathrm{LDA}}+\Delta_a[\mu_0]\bigr)G^{\mathrm H}_a(t)
-\sum_{j\in\mathcal O,\nu}|g_{aj\nu}|^2
\int_0^t ds\,e^{i\Omega_\nu s}
\frac{G^{\mathrm H}_j(s )G^{\mathrm H}_a(t)}{G_{0,a}(s)}\\
&- \sum_{b\in\mathcal V,\nu}|g_{ab\nu}|^2
\int_0^t ds\,e^{i\Omega_\nu s}
\frac{G^{\mathrm H}_b(s)G^{\mathrm H}_a(t)}{G_{0,a}(s)}.
\end{align}
I have demonstrated numerically that this is equivalent to carrying out self-consistency in the frequency domain, \emph{only on the quasiboson Hamiltonian}.
\subsubsection{Dunn's scheme}
Dunn's EOM for holes is
\begin{align}
    \dot G_j^{\rm D}(t)
    &=
    -i\bigl(\epsilon_j^{\mathrm{LDA}}+\Delta_j[\mu_0]\bigr)G^{\rm D}_j(t)
    -
    \sum_{i\in \mathcal{O},\nu}|g_{ji\nu}|^2
    \int_0^t ds\,e^{i\Omega_\nu s}
    \frac{G^{\rm D}_i(t)G^{\mathrm{D}}_j(t-s)}{G^{\mathrm{D}}_i(t-s)}\\
&- \sum_{a\in\mathcal V,\nu}|g_{ja\nu}|^2
\int_0^t ds\,e^{i\Omega_\nu s}
\frac{G^{\rm D}_a(t)G^{\mathrm{D}}_j(t-s)}{G^{\mathrm{D}}_a(t-s)},
\end{align}
while for the particle states, we have
\begin{align}
    \dot G_a^{\rm D}(t)
    &=
    -i\bigl(\epsilon_a^{\mathrm{LDA}}+\Delta_a[\mu_0]\bigr)G^{\rm D}_a(t)
    -
    \sum_{j\in \mathcal{O},\nu}|g_{aj\nu}|^2
    \int_0^t ds\,e^{i\Omega_\nu s}
    \frac{G^{\rm D}_j(t)G^{\mathrm{D}}_a(t-s)}{G^{\mathrm{D}}_j(t-s)}\\
&- \sum_{b\in\mathcal V,\nu}|g_{ab\nu}|^2
\int_0^t ds\,e^{i\Omega_\nu s}
\frac{G^{\rm D}_b(t)G^{\mathrm{D}}_a(t-s)}{G^{\mathrm{D}}_b(t-s)}.
\end{align}
If we solve these equations, then we get 
\begin{equation}
    G^D_j(t)= \exp(-i(\epsilon_j^{\mathrm{LDA}}+\Delta_j[\mu_0])t) \exp(c_j(t))
\end{equation}
But we really want 
\begin{align}
    G^D_j(t)&= \exp(-i(\epsilon_j^{\mathrm{LDA}}+\Delta_j[\mu_0+\delta\mu])t) \exp(c_j(t))\\
&\approx \exp(-i(\epsilon_j^{\mathrm{LDA}}+\Delta_j[\mu_0] + \frac{\partial \Delta_j}{\partial \mu}\delta\mu)t) \exp(c_j(t))\\
\end{align}
where $G_{0,k}^{n,R}(t) = -i \Theta(t) e^{-i \left(k^2/2 + \Gamma_k^{n}\right) t}$ with, for $n\geq0$, $\Gamma_k^{n+1} = n_{k}^{(n)}v_m+\Sigma_k^{n+1,x(E)}[G^{(n)}]=n_{k}^{(n)}v_m-\sum_{\bm{q}\neq\bm{0}} n_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}}^{(n)}v(\bm{q})$ and $\Gamma_k^{0} = V_k^{xc}$. $\Sigma_k^{n+1,x(E)}[G^{(n)}]$ is the exchange self-energy at iteration $n+1$ computed with Ewald summation (neglecting the $\bm{q}=0$ term) and $n_{k}^{(n)}v_m$ is the Madelung potential at iteration $n$. Note that this may change during self-consistent iteration as the chemical potential and thus occupation numbers are updated. We are always e
\subsection{Comparing self-consistent cumulants on the hole-only quasiboson model}
\subsubsection{Holms' scheme}
This comparison is most transparent if we assume that the exchange stays fixed.
We use the retarded convention throughout, so that the Green's function is
\begin{equation}
    G_{0,k}(t)
    =
    -i e^{-i\epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}}t},
    \qquad
    G_k(t)
    =
    G_{0,k}(t)e^{C_k(t)},
\end{equation}
which is applicable for the general state $k$. Defining the
 static correction
\begin{equation}
    \Delta_k
    =
    \Sigma_{k}^x
    -
    V_{xc,k}^{\mathrm{LDA}},
\end{equation}
we can write the cumulant as
\begin{align}
    C_k(t)
    &=
    -i\Delta_k t+c_k(t)\\
\implies 
    \dot C_k(0)&=-i\Delta_k,
\end{align}
where we used $C_k(0)=0$. Our initial formulation assumes a GW self-energy, so the bosonic frequencies $\Omega_\nu$ and couplings $g_{pq\nu}$ are also
held fixed. Also, define
\begin{equation}
    D_{ki\nu}^{\mathrm{LDA}}
    =
    \epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}}
    -
    \epsilon_i^{\mathrm{LDA}}
    +
    \Omega_\nu.
\end{equation}
Matching the first Dyson correction to the cumulant expansion, and
then evaluating the correlation self-energy with dressed propagators,
Holm's self-consistent map is
\begin{align}
    C_k^{\mathrm H}(t)
    &=
    -i\Delta_k t
    -
    \int_0^t ds\,(t-s)
    \frac{\Sigma_{k,c}[G^{\mathrm H}](s)}{G_{0,k}(s)}\\
\implies 
    \dot C_k^{\mathrm H}(t)
    &=
    -i\Delta_k
    -
    \sum_{i\in\mathcal{O},\nu}|g_{ki\nu}|^2
    \int_0^t ds\,
    e^{+iD_{ki\nu}^{\mathrm{LDA}}s}
    e^{C^{\mathrm H}_i(s)}
\end{align}
with
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma_{k,c}[G^{\mathrm H}](s)
    =
    \sum_{i\in\mathcal O,\nu}
    |g_{ki\nu}|^2
    e^{+i\Omega_\nu s}G^{\mathrm H}_i(s),
    \qquad s>0.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, the interacting Green's function obeys
\begin{align}
\dot G^{\mathrm H}_k(t)
&=\dot G_{0,k}(t)e^{C^{\mathrm H}_k(t)}+G^{\mathrm H}_k(t)\dot C^{\mathrm H}_k(t) \\
&=-i\bigl(\epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}}+\Delta_k\bigr)G^{\mathrm H}_k(t)
-\sum_{i\in\mathcal O,\nu}|g_{ki\nu}|^2
\int_0^t ds\,
e^{iD_{ki\nu}^{\mathrm{LDA}}s}e^{C^{\mathrm H}_i(s)}G^{\mathrm H}_k(t)
\\
&=-i\bigl(\epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}}+\Delta_k\bigr)G^{\mathrm H}_k(t)
-\sum_{i\in\mathcal O,\nu}|g_{ki\nu}|^2
\int_0^t ds\,e^{i\Omega_\nu s}
\frac{G^{\mathrm H}_i(s)G^{\mathrm H}_k(t)}{G_{0,k}(s)},
\label{eq:holm_gdot_fixed_exchange}
\end{align}
where we used $e^{iD_{ki\nu}^{\mathrm{LDA}}s}e^{C^{\mathrm H}_i(s)}=e^{i\Omega_\nu s}
e^{i(\epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}}-\epsilon_i^{\mathrm{LDA}})s}
e^{C^{\mathrm H}_i(s)}
=e^{i\Omega_\nu s}\frac{G^{\mathrm H}_i(s)}{G_{0,k}(s)}$.
For the same quasiboson model and static correction, Dunn's EOM is
\begin{align}
    {
    \dot G_k^{\rm D}}(t)
    &=
    -i\bigl(\epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}}+\Delta_k\bigr)G^{\rm D}_k(t)
    -
    \sum_{i\nu}|g_{ki\nu}|^2
    \int_0^t ds\,e^{+i\Omega_\nu s}
    \frac{G^{\rm D}_i(t)G^{\mathrm{D}}_k(t-s)}{G^{\mathrm{D}}_i(t-s)}
    \label{eq:pj_gdot_fixed_exchange} \\
\implies \dot C_k^{\mathrm D}(t)
&=-i\Delta_k
-\sum_{i\nu}|g_{ki\nu}|^2
\int_0^t ds\,e^{i\Omega_\nu s}
\frac{G_i^{\mathrm D}(t)G_k^{\mathrm D}(t-s)}
     {G_i^{\mathrm D}(t-s)G_k^{\mathrm D}(t)} \\
    &=
    -i\Delta_k
    -
    \sum_{i\nu}|g_{ki\nu}|^2
    \int_0^t ds\,
    e^{+iD_{ki\nu}^{\mathrm{LDA}}s} \left[
    e^{C^{\mathrm{D}}_i(t)-C^{\mathrm{D}}_i(t-s)+C^{\mathrm{D}}_k(t-s)-C^{\mathrm{D}}_k(t)} \right],
    \label{eq:pj_cdot_fixed_exchange}
\end{align}
where we use the identity $\frac{G_i^{\mathrm D}(t)G_k^{\mathrm D}(t-s)}
     {G_i^{\mathrm D}(t-s)G_k^{\mathrm D}(t)}=
e^{i(\epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}}-\epsilon_i^{\mathrm{LDA}})s}
e^{C_i^{\mathrm D}(t)-C_i^{\mathrm D}(t-s)
  +C_k^{\mathrm D}(t-s)-C_k^{\mathrm D}(t)}$. 
\subsection{$G\tilde{v}_0$}
Define the noninteracting Green's function for state $p$ as
\begin{equation}
G^{n}_p(\omega)
=
\big( \omega - E^n_p \big)^{-1}
\end{equation}
where% $E^0_p=\left(\mathbf{F} + \alpha \mathbf{W}[G^{(0)}]\right)_{pp}$, where $\mathbf{F}$ is the Fock matrix and $\mathbf{W}^{(0)}$ is the initial guess for the self-energy correction. The quasiparticle energies $E^n_p$ at iteration $n$ are then updated using the effective self-energy $\Sigma_{\rm eff}^{(n)}$ as follows:
\begin{align}
    E^n_p &= \left(\mathbf{F}[G^{(n-1)}] + \alpha \mathbf{W}[G^{(n)}] \right)_{pp} + (1-\delta_{n0})\operatorname{Re}\Sigma_{\rm eff,pp}[G^{(n-1)}](E^{n-1}_p).
\end{align}
If we define $\tilde{\Omega}_{ia} = E^0_a - E^0_i$, for the initial iteration $n=0$, we have
\begin{align}
    E^0_p &= \left(\mathbf{F}^{\rm HF} + \alpha \mathbf{W}[G^{(0)}] \right)_{pp}\\
&=  p^2/2 + \Gamma_p^{n}+ \alpha \Sigma^{2h1p}_{pp}[G^{(0)}](E^0_p)\\
&= p^2/2 + \Gamma_p^{n} + \alpha\sum_{ij,a} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{paij}}{E^0_p + \tilde{\Omega}_{ia} - E^0_j}.
\end{align}
where $\mathcal{K}_{paij} = (pa|ij)\left(2(pa|ij)-(pi|aj)\right)$ in the restricted formalism and the BW single-particle energies are determined self-consistently in the ground-state calculation, when they happen to take on the values 
\begin{equation}
    E^0_r = \begin{cases}
\varepsilon^{\rm HF}_r, & \text{if } |\bm{r}| > k_F,\\
\varepsilon^{\rm HF}_r + \alpha\sum_{ij,a} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{raij}}{E^0_r + E^0_a - E^0_i - E^0_j}, & \text{if } |\bm{r}| \leq k_F.
    \end{cases}
\end{equation}
With the definition 
\begin{align}
\bm{\Sigma}^{(n)}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\omega)
&\equiv
\bm{\Sigma}(\omega) - \alpha \mathbf{W} \\
&\approx
\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}^{(2)}\!\left[ G^{(n-1)} \right](\omega)
- \alpha \mathbf{W}\left[ G^{(n-1)} \right].
\end{align}
for iterations $n>0$, we have
\begin{align}
    E^n_p
&= \left(\mathbf{F} + \alpha \mathbf{W}[G^{(n)}] \right)_{pp} + \Sigma_{\rm eff,pp}^{(n)}(E^{n-1}_p)\\
&\approx \left(\mathbf{F} + \alpha \mathbf{W}[G^{(n)}] \right)_{pp} + \operatorname{Re}\tilde{\Sigma}_{pp}^{(2)}\!\left[ G^{(n-1)} \right](E^{n-1}_p) - \alpha \mathbf{W}[G^{(n)}]_{pp} \\
    &= p^2/2 + \Gamma_p^{n} + \operatorname{Re}\tilde{\Sigma}_{pp}^{(2)}\!\left[ G^{(n-1)} \right](E^{n-1}_p)\\
&= p^2/2 + \Gamma_p^{n} + \sum_{ij,a} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{paij}}{E^{n-1}_p - E^{n-1}_j + \tilde{\Omega}_{ia}} + \sum_{i,ab} \frac{\mathcal{K}_{paib}}{E^{n-1}_p - E^{n-1}_b - \tilde{\Omega}_{ia}}.
% &= \mathbf{F} + \frac{1}{2}\sum_{ij,a} \frac{|\langle p a||ij\rangle|^2}{E^{n-1}_p - E^{n-1}_j + \tilde{\Omega}_{ia}} + \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,ab} \frac{|\langle p i||ab\rangle|^2}{E^{n-1}_p - E^{n-1}_i - \tilde{\Omega}_{ab}}.
\end{align}
We saw previously that the following equation is approximately obeyed
\begin{equation}
    G_{pp}^1(t) \approx
   G_{pp}^{0}(t)
 + \int dt' dt'' \,
   G_{pp}^{0}(t - t')
   \bm{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{eff}}(t' - t'')
   G_{pp}^{0}(t'')
\end{equation}
  Then, for iterations $n>0$, previous experience with GW suggests that we take the quasiparticle Green's function as our non-interacting reference. 
\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=red!5!white, colframe=red!75!black, title=How we should choose $W^{(n)}$ ]
If we are supposed to freeze the interaction, this implies that the QB couplings and frequencies always stay at the non-interacting level as $\langle p a||ij\rangle$ and $\tilde{\Omega}_{ia}=E^0_a - E^0_i$.
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsubsection{Getting the quasiparticle energies}
\subsubsection{What choice we take for the self-energy}

For true self-consistency, I was thinking that we should do 
\begin{align}
\tilde{\Sigma}_{m, c}^{(2),n+1,R}[G^{(n)}](t) &= -i\theta(t)\left[\sum_{ijb}\mathcal{K}_{pijb} G^{(n),<}_{i}(t) e^{-i\tilde{\Omega}_{jb} t} + \sum_{iab}\mathcal{K}_{paib} G^{(n),>}_{a}(t) e^{-i\tilde{\Omega}_{ib} t}\right]
\label{eq:correlation_self_energy_bw}
\end{align}
with $\mathcal{K}_{pijb} = (pi|jb)\left(2(pi|jb)-(pj|ib)\right)$ and $\mathcal{K}_{paib} = (pa|bi)\left(2(pa|bi)-(pb|ai)\right)$ in the restricted formalism. $\tilde{\Omega}_{kc} \equiv \tilde{\varepsilon}_c - \tilde{\varepsilon}_k={\varepsilon}^{\rm HF}_c - \tilde{\varepsilon}_k$ are the BW excitation energies frozen at the non-interacting level and $\tilde{\varepsilon}$ are the dressed BW single-particle energies. 
\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=red!5!white, colframe=red!75!black, title=How this reduces to the one-shot BWs2 self-energy ]
We can check that this reduces to the known form of the one-shot BWs2 self-energy when $n=0$ and $G^{(0)}$ is the non-interacting Green's function. We have that
\begin{align}
    &G^{(0),R}_{n}(t)=-i\Theta(t)e^{-i\tilde{\varepsilon}_nt}\\
&\implies G^{(0),<}_{i}(t)=ie^{-i\tilde{\varepsilon}_i t}, \quad G^{(0),>}_{a}(t)=-ie^{-i\tilde{\varepsilon}_a t}=-ie^{-i{\varepsilon}^{\rm HF}_a t}= \quad \forall t>0\\
\implies \tilde{\Sigma}_{m, c}^{(2),1,R}[G^{(0)}](t)&=-i\theta(t)\left[\sum_{ijb}\left(\mathcal{K}_{pijb}\right) e^{-i(\tilde{\varepsilon}_i-\tilde{\Omega}_{jb}) t}\right.\\
&\left. + \sum_{iab}\left( \mathcal{K}_{paib}\right) e^{-i(\tilde{\varepsilon}_a+\tilde{\Omega}_{ib}) t}\right],
\end{align}
Since $F(\omega) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} dt e^{i(\omega t)} F(t)$ and $\int_{0}^{\infty} (-i) e^{i(\omega -E) t} dt = \frac{1}{\omega - E + i\eta}$, we have that
\begin{align}
    \tilde{\Sigma}_{m, c}^{(2),1,R}[G^{(0)}](\omega) &= \sum_{ijb}\frac{\mathcal{K}_{pijb}}{\omega - \tilde{\varepsilon}_i+\tilde{\Omega}_I + i\eta} + \sum_{aij}\frac{\mathcal{K}_{paib}}{\omega - \tilde{\varepsilon}_a-\tilde{\Omega}_I + i\eta},
\end{align}
which is exactly the form of the second order self energy evaluated at a BW reference.
\end{tcolorbox}
In all instances past the first iteration, we can do a discrete Fourier transform to get into the frequency domain, take the real part, and evaluate at $E^{n-1}_p$ to get the correlation self-energy contribution to the quasiparticle energy.
Equating the Dyson and
cumulant expansions to first order in the screened interaction yields,
\begin{align}
C_k^{n+1,R}(t) &= i e^{iE_k^{(n)} t}\int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t}
\frac{k^2/2+\Gamma_k^{n+1}-\left(k^2/2 + \Gamma_k^{n}\right) +\Sigma_k^{n+1,c}[G^{(n)}](\omega) }{(\omega - E_k^{(n)} + i\eta)^2}\\
&= -i \left(\Gamma_k^{n+1}-\Gamma_k^{n}\right) t \Theta(t) + \underbrace{i
\int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t}
\frac{\Sigma_k^{n+1,c}[G^{(n)}](\omega + E_k^{(n)})}{(\omega + i\eta)^2}}_{C_k^{n+1,\text{dyn}}(t)}.
\end{align}
where $\Sigma_k^{n+1,c}[G^{(n)}](\omega)$ is the correlation self-energy at iteration $n+1$.
We can rewrite the dynamical cumulant as 
\begin{align}
C_k^{n+1,\text{dyn}}(t) &= \int d\omega\, \beta_{k}^{n+1,R}(\omega)\,
\frac{e^{-i\omega t}-1}{\omega^{2}}\\
&= \underbrace{\int\!\dd\omega\,\beta_{k}^{n+1,R}(\omega)
    \frac{e^{-i\omega t}+i\omega t-1}{\omega^2}}_{C^{\rm Landau}_k(t)}
  \;-\; i\,S_1[\beta_k]\,t
\end{align}
where $\beta_{k,c}^{n+1,R}(\omega)=\frac{1}{\pi}\left|\operatorname{Im}\Sigma_k^{n+1,c}
\!\left(\omega+E_k^{(n)}\right)\right|$ and $S_1[\beta_k]=\mathcal{P} \int\!\dd\omega\,\beta_{k}^{n+1,R}(\omega)/\omega$.
This allows us to write the Green's function as 
\begin{align}
G_k^{n+1,R}(t) &= - i\Theta(t)\, \exp\left[-i \left(k^2/2+\Gamma_k^{n+1}\right) t \right] e^{C_k^{n+1,\text{dyn}}(t)}.
\end{align}
\subsubsection{How this reduces to the MBPT self-energy with the MF Green's function}
% \subsubsection{Implementation scheme}
When we are iterating on BW, the correlation self-energy takes the form: 
\begin{align}
\Sigma_{m, c}^{(n+1),R}[G^{(n)}](t) &= -i\theta(t)\left[\sum_{ijb}\mathcal{K}_{pijb} G^{(n),<}_{i}(t) e^{-i\tilde{\Omega}_{jb} t} + \sum_{iab}\mathcal{K}_{paib} G^{(n),>}_{a}(t) e^{-i\tilde{\Omega}_{ib} t}\right],
\label{eq:correlation_self_energy_bw}
\end{align}
with $\mathcal{K}_{pijb} = (pi|jb)\left(2(pi|jb)-(pj|ib)\right)$ and $\mathcal{K}_{paib} = (pa|bi)\left(2(pa|bi) - (pb|ai)\right)$ in the restricted formalism.
\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=red!5!white, colframe=red!75!black, title=How this reduces to the BWs2 self-energy with a HF Green's function]
For $n=0$, we have that
\begin{align}
    &G^{(0),R}_{n}(t)=-i\Theta(t)e^{-i\tilde{\varepsilon}_nt}\\
&\implies G^{(0),<}_{i}(t)=ie^{-i\tilde{\varepsilon}_i t}, \quad G^{(0),>}_{a}(t)=-ie^{-i\tilde{\varepsilon}_a t} \quad \forall t>0\\
\implies \tilde{\Sigma}_{m, c}^{(2),1,R}[G^{(0)}](t)&=-i\theta(t)\left[\sum_{ijb}\left(\mathcal{K}_{pijb}\right) e^{-i(\tilde{\varepsilon}_i-\tilde{\Omega}_{jb}) t}\right.\\
&\left. + \sum_{iab}\left( \mathcal{K}_{paib}\right) e^{-i(\tilde{\varepsilon}_a+\tilde{\Omega}_{ib}) t}\right],
\end{align}
Since $F(\omega) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} dt e^{i(\omega t)} F(t)$ and $\int_{0}^{\infty} (-i) e^{i(\omega -E) t} dt = \frac{1}{\omega - E + i\eta}$, we have that
\begin{align}
    \tilde{\Sigma}_{m, c}^{(2),1,R}[G^{(0)}](\omega) &= \sum_{ijb}\frac{\mathcal{K}_{pijb}}{\omega - \tilde{\varepsilon}_i+\tilde{\Omega}_I + i\eta} + \sum_{aij}\frac{\mathcal{K}_{paib}}{\omega - \tilde{\varepsilon}_a-\tilde{\Omega}_I + i\eta},
\end{align}
\end{tcolorbox}
When we are iterating on GW, the correlation self-energy takes the form:
\begin{align}
\Sigma_{m, c}^{(n+1),R}[G^{(n)}](t) &= -i\theta(t)\left[\sum_{i, I}\left|M_{ m,i}^I\right|^2 G^{(n),<}_{i}(t) e^{-i\Omega_I t} + \sum_{a, I}\left|M_{ m,a}^I\right|^2 G^{(n),>}_{a}(t) e^{-i\Omega_I t}\right],
\label{eq:correlation_self_energy_gw}
\end{align}
with $M$ and $\Omega$ the transition densities and energies from the dRPA problem.
\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=red!5!white, colframe=red!75!black, title=How this reduces to G0W0 with a noninteracting Green's function]
For $n=0$, we have that
\begin{align}
    &G^R_{0,n}(t)=-i\Theta(t)e^{-i\varepsilon_n^{0}t}\\
&\implies G^<_{0,i}(t)=ie^{-i\varepsilon_i^{0}t}, \quad G^>_{0,a}(t)=-ie^{-i\varepsilon_a^{0}t} \quad \forall t>0\\
&\implies \Sigma_{m, c}^{R}(t)=-i\theta(t)\left[\sum_{i, I}\left|M_{ m,i}^I\right|^2 e^{-i(\varepsilon_i^{0}-\Omega_I) t} + \sum_{a, I}\left|M_{ m,a}^I\right|^2 e^{-i(\varepsilon_a^{0}+\Omega_I) t}\right]
\end{align}
Since $F(\omega) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} dt e^{i(\omega t)} F(t)$ and $\int_{0}^{\infty} (-i) e^{i(\omega -E) t} dt = \frac{1}{\omega - E + i\eta}$, we have that
\begin{align}
    \Sigma_{m, c}^{R}(\omega) &= \sum_{i, I}\frac{\left|M_{ m,i}^I\right|^2}{\omega - \varepsilon_i^{0}+\Omega_I + i\eta} + \sum_{a, I}\frac{\left|M_{ m,a}^I\right|^2}{\omega - \varepsilon_a^{0}-\Omega_I + i\eta}
\end{align}
\end{tcolorbox}
For iterations $n \geq 1$: Assume that we have $A_k^{(n-1)}(\omega)\propto \operatorname{Im}
G_k^{(n-1)}(\omega)$. 
This gives us a recipe to update the chemical potential $\mu^{(n+1)}$, where $\mu^{(1)}=\frac{\left(k^2/2 + \Gamma^1_k\right)_{\rm HOMO}+\left(k^2/2 + \Gamma^1_k\right)_{\rm LUMO}}{2}$ as
\begin{equation}
    N/2 = \sum_k \int_{-\infty}^{\mu^{(n+1)}} A_k^{(n)}(\omega) d \omega,
\end{equation}
where we can take $N/2$ as the number of occupied bands in our spin-restricted UEG. Once we have the chemical potential, we determine the lesser and greater Green's functions as 
\begin{align}
    G_k^{<,(n)}(t)&=i \int_{-\infty}^{\mu^{(n+1)}} d \omega A_k^{(n)}(\omega) e^{-i \omega t} \\
    G_k^{>,(n)}(t)&=-i \int_{\mu^{(n+1)}}^{\infty} d \omega A_k^{(n)}(\omega) e^{-i \omega t} .
\end{align}
This gives a recipe for updating the correlation self-energy according to \ref{eq:correlation_self_energy_bw} or \ref{eq:correlation_self_energy_gw}, for BWs2 and GW, respectively. Then, we would do a DFT to get the real and imaginary parts of $\Sigma_{p, c}^{n+1,R}(\omega)$, which can then be used to update $G_k^{n+1,R}(t)$. This closes the self-consistency loop.

\subsection{Alternative expansion points}
In the diagonal approximation, the cumulant ansatz is
\begin{equation}
    \bm{G}_{pp}(t) = \bm{G}_{pp}^{0}(t) e^{\bm{C}_{pp}(t)}.
\end{equation}
In the retarded convention, $G_{pp}^{0}(t) = -i \Theta(t) e^{-i(p^2/2 + \Sigma_{pp}^x) t}$, whereas in the time-ordered convention, $G_{pp}^{0}(t) = \mp i \theta( \pm t) \theta\left( \pm\left(\epsilon_p-\epsilon_{k_F}\right)\right) e^{-i (p^2/2 + \Sigma_{pp}^x) t}$, where the upper (lower) sign refers to particles with $k>k_F$ (holes $k<k_F$ ), and $\theta$ is the unit step function.
Equating the Dyson expansion to the cumulant ansatz to first order in W yields
\begin{align}
    \bm{G}_{pp}^{0}(t) \bm{C}_{pp}(t) &= \int \frac{\dd \omega}{2\pi} e^{-i\omega t} \bm{G}_{pp}^{0}(\omega)\bm{\Sigma}_{pp}^c(\omega)\bm{G}_{pp}^{0}(\omega) \\
\implies 	 C_{pp}(t) &= i \int \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} \frac{ \Sigma_{pp}^c\left(\omega+\epsilon_p\right)}{(\omega + i \eta)^2} e^{-i \omega t}\\
&= \int d\omega \frac{\beta_{pp}(\omega)}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right),
    \label{eqn:cumulant_connection}
\end{align}
where $\beta_{pp}(\omega) = \frac{1}{\pi} |\text{Im} \Sigma_{pp}^c(\omega + \epsilon_p)|$.
\subsubsection{Bare non-interacting reference }
Here, we take $\epsilon_p = p^2/2$. Correspondingly, $\epsilon_{k_F} = \frac{1}{2} \left(k_{\text{nocc}}^2 + k_{\text{nocc}-1}^2\right)$, where $k_{\text{nocc}}$ and $k_{\text{nocc}-1}$ are the wave vectors of the LUMO and HOMO, respectively. 
\subsubsection{LDA non-interacting reference }
Here, we take $\epsilon_p = \epsilon_p^{\mathrm{LDA}}$. Correspondigly, $\epsilon_{k_F} = \frac{1}{2} \left(\epsilon_{\text{nocc}}^{\mathrm{LDA}} + \epsilon_{\text{nocc}-1}^{\mathrm{LDA}}\right)$.
\subsubsection{Quasiparticle non-interacting reference }
Here, we take $\epsilon_p = E_p^{GW}$. Correspondingly, $\epsilon_{k_F} = \frac{1}{2} \left(E_{\text{nocc}}^{GW} + E_{\text{nocc}-1}^{GW}\right)$.
\subsection{SCCE(GW0) on the LDA reference}
\subsubsection{Iteration on the retarded cumulant}
Here,
% if we are doing self-consistent iteration and 
% \begin{equation}
%   G_{0,k}^R(t) = -i \Theta(t) e^{-i(k^2/2 + \Sigma_k^x) t}
% \end{equation}
% once we terminate self-consistent iteration and go to plot the spectral function.
%  Comparing the cumulant and Dyson expansions of $G$ up to first order in $W$ leads to the Landau cumulant
\begin{equation}
C_{k}^R(t) = \int d\omega \frac{\beta_{k}(\omega)}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right)
\label{eq:cumulant_beta}
\end{equation}
where $\beta_{k}(\omega) = \frac{1}{\pi} |\text{Im} \Sigma^R_{k}[\etaS](\omega + \varepsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}})|$.
Then,
\begin{equation}
G_k^R(t)=G_k^{R, 0}(t) e^{\tilde{C}_k^R(t)} e^{-\eta_{\rm X} |t|}
\end{equation}
\subsubsection{Iteration on the time-ordered cumulant}
With the lower (upper) sign referring to particles (holes), here we take
\begin{equation}
    \tilde{C}_k^T(t)=\int d \omega \frac{\beta_k^{ \pm}(\omega)}{\omega^2}\left(e^{-i \omega t}+i \omega t-1\right) .
\end{equation}
where $\beta_k^{ \pm}(\omega)= \frac{1}{\pi}\left|\operatorname{Im} \Sigma_k^R[\etaS]\left(\omega+\varepsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}}\right)\right| \theta\left( \pm\left(\varepsilon_{k_F}^{\mathrm{LDA}}-\varepsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}}-\omega\right)\right)$. 
Then,
\begin{equation}
G_k^T(t)=G_k^{T, 0}(t) e^{\tilde{C}_k^T(t)} e^{-\eta_{\rm X} |t|}
\end{equation}
% where the non-interacting part is $G_k^{T, 0}(t)=\mp i \theta( \mp t) \theta\left( \mp\left(\tilde{\epsilon}_k-\tilde{\epsilon}_{k_F}\right)\right) e^{-i \bar{\epsilon}_k t}$. Note that $\tilde{\epsilon}_{k_F} = \frac{\tilde{\epsilon}_{k\equiv\text{HOMO}} + \tilde{{\epsilon}}_{k\equiv\text{LUMO}}}{2}$.

\subsubsection{The self energy}
At a given iteration $n$, including the one-shot level (I checked analytically), the self-energy is always constructed as 
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma_{k, c}^{n,R}[\etaS](t)=\theta(t)\left[-\sum_{l<k_F, I}\left|M_{ k,l}^I\right|^2 G_{l}^{<,(n)}(t) e^{+i \Omega_I t} e^{-\etaS t} + \sum_{l>k_F, I}\left|M_{ k,l}^I\right|^2 G_{l}^{>,(n)}(t) e^{-i \Omega_I t} e^{- \etaS t}\right],
\end{equation}
with the quasiboson quantities fixed at the RPA level. 
% , which should be invariant to the choice of single-particle reference at the bare versus LDA level. This implies that in a self-consistent theory we need to get lesser and greater time-domain cumulant Green's functions for each state $l$ (which may be below or above the Fermi level) at each iteration. If we choose to iterate on the time-ordered cumulant, we have

% functions, respectively. For a retarded self-energy, all that we need are $$

\subsubsection{Broadening of the self-energy}
Following the convention that allowed us to converge to the TDL, we use $\etaS = 0.0272$~eV during self-consistent iteration. When plotting the self-energy at a given iteration, I instead use $\etaS=0.4$~eV to facilitate visualization.
\subsubsection{Broadening of the Green's function}
$\eta_X\equiv\eta_{\text{loop}}=0$~eV, except in the final iteration where $\eta_X = 0.8$~eV, which is the final broadening applied to facilitate visualisation of the spectral function. We cannot set $\eta_X = 0.8$~eV during the self-consistent loop because this would smear out the self-energy; it is essential that the Landau cumulant can detect the fine structure of the self-energy in order to get a spectral function with features.

\subsection{SCCE(GW0) in the TDL}
Then the idea for the self-consistent scheme would be to build a new retarded self-energy from the time-ordered cumulant Green's function. I think we would need to do this for all $k=|\bm{k}|$ shells in the plane wave basis, but we wouldn't need to enumerate over all basis states? More precisely, we would only need to compute the Green's function for each state in a given shell once, but when we are summing over all possible Green's functions, we would need to sum over all states in the shell. 
\subsubsection{Constructing the Retarded Self Energy }
We might want to use the form
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma_{k, c}^R(t)=\theta(t) \sum_{m I}\left|M_{ k, m}^I\right|^2\left[G_{m,CE}^{>}(t) e^{-i \Omega_I t}-G_{m,CE}^{<}(t) e^{+i \Omega_I t}\right],
\end{equation}
where $M_{k, m}^I$ is the screened Coulomb matrix element between states $k$ and $m$ for the quasiboson mode $I$ and $\Omega_I$ is the quasiboson frequency, as obtained from the preceding Casida diagonalization. $G_{m,CE}^{>}(t)$ and $G_{m,CE}^{<}(t)$ are the greater and lesser cumulant Green's functions, respectively. For a retarded self-energy, all that we need are $$
G_{i, \mathrm{CE}}^{<}(t)=-\left[G_{i, \mathrm{CE}}^T(-t)\right]^*, \quad t>0,
$$
and
$$
G_{a, \mathrm{CE}}^{>}(t)=G_{a, \mathrm{CE}}^T(t), \quad t>0 .
$$, for occupied states $i$, and unoccupied states $a$,
resulting in
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma_{k, c}^R(t)=\theta(t)\left[-\sum_{m<k_F, I}\left|M_{ k, m}^I\right|^2 G_{m,CE}^{<}(t) e^{+i \Omega_I t} + \sum_{m>k_F, I}\left|M_{ k, m}^I\right|^2 G_{m,CE}^{>}(t) e^{-i \Omega_I t}\right]
\end{equation}
\subsection{Getting the cumulant Green's function }
We always have 
\begin{equation}
G_k(t)=G_k^{ 0}(t) e^{\tilde{C}_k(t)}
\end{equation}
\subsubsection{Retarded formalism}
Here, the dynamical cumulant is always given as 
\begin{equation}
    \tilde{C}_k^R(t) = \frac{1}{\pi}\int d\omega \frac{|\operatorname{Im} \Sigma_k^R(\omega+\tilde{\epsilon_k})|}{\omega^2} \left( e^{-i\omega t} + i\omega t - 1 \right),
\end{equation}
where for $G_{k,0}@\mathrm{LDA}$, $\tilde{\epsilon_k} = \epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}}$. Note that we should get equivalent results if we instead use $\tilde{\epsilon_k} = \epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}} - v_{kk}^{xc} = k^2/2$. All that LDA really does is to introduce a non-dispersive exchange-correlation potential to each single-particle state. This does not change the RPA screening and we get a further cancellation of the exchange-correlation potential in the self-energy evaluations. The shifted argujment of the cumulant kernel introduces a positive exchange-correlation contribution, while in the self-energy denominator, we have a negative exchange-correlation, and these cancel out. The non-interacting reference always has the form $G_{k,0}^R(t) = -i \Theta(t) e^{-i\epsilon_k t}$.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item We can take $\epsilon_k = \epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}}$.
    \item We can take $\epsilon_k = \epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}} - v_{kk}^{xc} + \Sigma_{kk}^x = k^2/2 + \Sigma_{kk}^x$.
\end{enumerate}
\subsubsection{Time-ordered formalism}
Here, the dynamical cumulant is always given as
\begin{equation}
    \tilde{C}_k^T(t) = \int d\omega \frac{\beta_k^{ \pm}(\omega)}{\omega^2}\left(e^{-i \omega t}+i \omega t-1\right) ,
\end{equation}
where $\beta_k^{ \pm}(\omega)= \frac{1}{\pi}\left|\operatorname{Im} \Sigma_k^R\left(\omega+\epsilon_k\right)\right| \theta\left( \pm\left(\epsilon_{k_F}-\epsilon_k-\omega\right)\right)$. The non-interacting part is $G_k^{T, 0}(t)=\mp i \theta( \pm t) \theta\left( \pm\left(\epsilon_k-\epsilon_{k F}\right)\right) e^{-i \epsilon_k^{\text{eff}} t}$, where the upper (lower) sign refers to particles with $k>k_F$ (holes $k<k_F$ ), and $\theta$ is the unit step function. Our reference is an LDA calculation and we are at zero temperature, so we can take $\epsilon_k=\epsilon_k^{\text{LDA}}$, $\epsilon_k^{\text{eff}}=\epsilon_k^{\text{LDA}}+\Sigma_k^x - v_k^{xc}$, and $\epsilon_{k_F}=\epsilon_{k_F}^{\text{LDA}}=\frac{\epsilon_{k\equiv\text{HOMO}}^{\text{LDA}} + \epsilon_{\text{LUMO}}^{\text{LDA}}}{2}$. Is this an appropriate way to define the Fermi energy, or should we instead take the midpoint between the quasiparticle energies of the HOMO and LUMO? Again, we choose to either take 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item We can take $\epsilon_k^{\text{eff}} = \epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}}$.
    \item We can take $\epsilon_k^{\text{eff}} = \epsilon_k^{\mathrm{LDA}} - v_{kk}^{xc} + \Sigma_{kk}^x = k^2/2 + \Sigma_{kk}^x$.
\end{enumerate}
In total, this belies six different ways to do SCCE(GW0). We can do:
- retarded first variant
- retarded second variant
- time-ordered first variant with fermi energy in terms of the LDA energies
- time-ordered variant one with fermi energy defined in terms of quasiparticle energies
- time-ordered variant two with both options
Do you think this is complete? Which way do we currently do? Also, Holms synthesize
s green functions at the chemical potential before embarking on self-consistency. Do we need to do this as well? Because right now my Greens functions, I think, are centered on the absolute frequency axis. 
I manually extracted both Garnet's and Holms' reported G0W0+C spectral functions in the TDL and ran my code towards the TDL.
\subsubsection{My red curves}
 My red curves have the plasmon-pole structure; however, they differ from Garnet's. One reason is due to the choice of manual broadening; he used 0.8 eV, whereas I used 0.2 eV. Another difference originates from computation of the cumulant spectral function; recall that when I used the exact frequency integration scheme for GW in the finite basis, I was unable to reproduce his results. We attributed this to two reasons:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item He was using a PPM to construct the GW self-energy when he wasn't supposed to; as I showed before, in the finite basis, the GW self-energy cannot be adequately represented by a single pole model. 
    \item He was constructing the cumulant kernel as $\beta_k(\omega) = 1/\pi |\operatorname{Im} \Sigma^R(\omega+E^{QP}_k)|$, whereas we (and Loos) think it should be $\beta_k(\omega) = 1/\pi |\operatorname{Im} \Sigma^R(\omega+\epsilon^{\text{LDA}}_k)|$. This is because the latter choice better reproduces the location of the first satellite peak, as predicted by the wavefunction theories of CCSD and BWs2+C. 
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{My green curves}
In his paper, Holm states that all spectral functions are centered around the chemical potential. My green curves are an attempt to reproduce this idea. Specifically, we know that at zero temperature, $\mu = \epsilon_{k_F}\equiv \frac{\epsilon_{\text{HOMO}}^{\text{LDA}} + \epsilon_{\text{LUMO}}^{\text{LDA}}}{2}$. Clearly, I am not yet at the TDL. However, beyond this basis size, it becomes difficult to store the OO+OV Choleskies in memory.
\subsubsection{Next steps}
I could do now G0W0+C with PPMs at the TDL, which may allow me to reproduce Holm's SCCE(GW0) results, but I don't know if this would be transferable to a finite basis.

then if we define

=

Then
\begin{align}
C_k^{\rm dyn}(t)

\\

where

=

with the bosonic frequency related to that of the Sommerfeld bulk plasma as
\begin{align}
\Omega^2(\mathbf q)
&=
\omega_p^2, \quad \omega_p = \sqrt{4 \pi n}
\end{align}
Then,
\begin{align}
\tilde{\omega}^2(\mathbf{q}) &= \frac{\Omega^2(\mathbf{q})}{1 - \varepsilon^{-1}(\mathbf{q},\omega=0)}\\
    % \tilde{\omega}_{\mathbf{G} \mathbf{G}'}^2 &= \frac{\Omega_{\mathbf{G} \mathbf{G}'}^2}{\delta_{\mathbf{G} \mathbf{G}'} - \epsilon_{\mathbf{G} \mathbf{G}'}^{-1}(\omega=0)},
\end{align}
where the RPA form for the dielectric function used is
\begin{align}

&\approx 1-v(\mathbf{q}) P_0(\mathbf{q}, \omega=0).

The static, non-interacting polarizability is given by

P_0(\mathbf{q}, \omega=0)

where we have a sum over the momentum transfers $\mathbf{k}$, and $f$ is the Fermi-Dirac distribution, which is 1 for occupied states and 0 for unoccupied states at zero temperature. We can take the $\epsilon_{\mathrm{k}} = k^2/2$ for the single-particle energies.
And finally,
\begin{align}
A(\mathbf{q}) &= -\frac{\pi}{2} \frac{\Omega^2(\mathbf{q})}{\tilde{\omega}(\mathbf{q})}.

Then we can determine the static part of the cumulant, which is the bare exchange self-energy component as 
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma^x_{\bm{k}} = -\sum_{\mathbf{q}} v(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}) f_{\mathbf{q}}
\end{equation}
In summary, we can obtain the cumulant Green's function as 
\begin{equation}
    G(\bm{k})^R = G_0(\bm{k})^R \exp(C_{\bm{k}(t)})
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
    C_{\bm{k}}(t) = -i \Sigma^x_{\bm{k}} t + C^{\rm dyn}_{\bm{k}}(t).
\end{equation}

\section{SCCE(GW0) in the finite basis}
We are interested in obtaining the a self-consistent cumulant spectral function for $k=[0,0,0]$. In the first iteration (one-shot), we apply $\Sigma_m^{G_0W_0}$ to get the Landau cumulant, and then a cumulant Green function ${G}_{m,CE}(t)$ for each basis state $m$. For the following iterations, we form the retarded correlation self-energy as 
\begin{equation}
    \Sigma_{m}^{c,R}(t)=\theta(t) \sum_{n I}\left|M_{ m,n}^I\right|^2\left[G_{n,CE}^{>}(t) e^{-i \Omega_I t}-G_{n,CE}^{<}(t) e^{+i \Omega_I t}\right],
\label{eq:scce_gw0}
\end{equation}
where $M_{m,n}^I$ and $\Omega_I$ are the screened Coulomb matrix elements and frequencies of quasiboson (QB) mode $I$, respectively. We then conduct a DFT to get $\Sigma_{m}^{c,R}(\omega)$, which we evaluate at $\omega+\epsilon_m^{\mathrm{LDA}}$ to form a new Landau cumulant. This closes the self-consistency loop, that we then iterate until convergence. 
This procedure is followed for both the independent boson model (IBM) and UEG, where the QB reference is always due to dRPA@LDA.

% \end{equation}
\subsection{What Holm did}
After the initial iteration, Holm updates the self-energy for target state $k$ as
\begin{equation}
\Sigma^{c}(\mathbf{k}, \omega)=\int_C \frac{\Gamma\left(\mathbf{k}, \omega^{\prime}\right)}{\omega-\omega^{\prime}} d \omega^{\prime}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
\Gamma(\mathbf{k}, \omega)=\sum_{\mathbf{q}} \int_0^\omega d \omega^{\prime} A\left(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}, \omega-\omega^{\prime}\right) B_0\left(\mathbf{q}, \omega^{\prime}\right)
\end{equation}
with $B_0(\mathbf{q}, \omega)$ the spectral representation of the screened Coulomb interaction. They fit the spectral function to a sum of Gaussians as
\begin{equation}
    A(k, \omega)=\sum_\nu \frac{W_\nu(k)}{\sqrt{2 \pi \Gamma_\nu(k)}} \exp \left[-\frac{\left[\omega-E_\nu(k)\right]^2}{2 \Gamma_\nu^2(k)}\right],
\end{equation}
 where $E_\nu(k)$, $\Gamma_\nu(k)$, and $W_\nu(k)$ are the energy, width, and weight of the $\nu$-th peak in the spectral function for state $k$. \emph{Would I want to do the same thing in my finite basis?} Then they update the cumulant to first order in the interaction as 
\begin{equation}
C(k, t)=-i \int_0^t d t^{\prime} \int_{t^{\prime}}^{\infty} d \tau \Sigma_{G W}(k, \tau) e^{i \varepsilon_k \tau}
\end{equation}
\subsubsection{Why this doesn't work in a finite basis }
 I only present results that follow the retarded cumulant formalism, but I checked and the time-ordered results aren't any better.
\subsection{Interpretation of the results for the IBM}
Here, the one-shot cumulant is known to be exact. For both the minimal and intermediate system sizes of 14e19o and 38e123o, we see that SCCE(GW0) degrades particularly the satellite features in the spectrum.
\subsection{Interpretation of the results for the UEG}
Here I present results for 305 and 1021 orbitals. The one-shot cumulant yields a quasiparticle peak, with satellites occurring at regularly spaced intervals as we move to higher binding energies. In contrast, SCCE(GW0) yields a washed out spectrum, which loses the QP peak location and second satellite peak of the one-shot result.
\subsection{Interpretation of PJ's results for the finite-size Holstein model}
In the strong coupling regime (which is representative of the electron gas at $r_s=4$), SCCE(GW0) fails to produce any satellite features with just a broad quasiparticle peak. This is similar to what I observe for the UEG.
To interpret spectra in a finite basis, we need to apply a finite broadening. 
\subsubsection{\(\nu \equiv \mathrm{QP}\)}
For the main quasiparticle peak ($\nu\equiv \mathrm{QP}$), we could determine these quantities as
\begin{equation}
E_{\mathrm{QP}}(k)=\epsilon_k+\operatorname{Re} \Sigma\left(k, E_{\mathrm{QP}}(k)\right) \equiv E_k
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
W_{\mathrm{QP}}(k)=Z_k=\left[1-\left.\frac{\partial \operatorname{Re} \Sigma(k, \omega)}{\partial \omega}\right|_{\omega=E_{\mathrm{QP}}}\right]^{-1}     
\end{equation}
and 
\begin{equation}
\Gamma_{\mathrm{QP}}(k)=\left|\operatorname{Im} \Sigma\left(k, E_{\mathrm{QP}}(k)\right)\right| .
\end{equation}
\subsubsection{\(\nu \equiv\mathrm{sat}\)}
To determine the satellite positions, Holm  $E_{h, 1}=E_k-\omega_p$, $E_{h, 2}=E_k-2 \omega_p$, $E_{p, 1}=E_k+\omega_p$, and $E_{p, 2}=E_k+2 \omega_p$, where $\omega_p = \sqrt{4 \pi n}$ is taken as the bare Sommerfeld plasma frequency. We have seen before that this is no longer applicable in the finite basis as the plasma frequencies are dispersive; it was for this reason that we considered instead
\begin{equation}

    =
   \sqrt{\frac{4v_{\mathbf{q}}}{V}
    \sum_{(i,a)\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{q}}}
    \left(\varepsilon_a-\varepsilon_i\right)},
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{q}}$ is the set of all particle-hole pairs $(i,a)$ connected by the momentum transfer $\mathbf{q}$.

Another way to interpret this is that we would be using standard Ewald summation for the self-consistency cycle and manually computing them Madelung correction and keeping the latter static (instead of using shifted Ewald summation, which would then force us to update the entire exchange self energy or keep it constant.) This scheme should allow us to selectively update the finite-basis exchange self-energy while keeping the Madelung correction constant. For the purpose of determining the single-particle energy that goes into updating the cumulant kernel $\tilde{\epsilon_k}$ during self-consistent iteration, we can try using $\tilde{\epsilon_k} = k^2/2$ or $\tilde{\epsilon_k} = k^2/2 + \Sigma_{kk}^{n,x,\rm{NOMAD}}$ or $\tilde{\epsilon_k} = k^2/2 + \Sigma_{kk}^{n,x,\rm{NOMAD}} + \Delta_{\rm MAD}$. Maybe we should try all three and see what works best for the 114e485o?
